Gudrun Thorhallsdottir (Gudrun Thorhallsdottir / Gudrun THorhallsdottir),
"The Development of Intervocalic *j in Proto-Germanic".
Ph.D. dissertation, Cornell University, 1993. viii + 266 pp.
(UMI / ProQuest order number 9410504.)

SOURCE AND METHOD
-----------------
The local PDF (293 scanned pages, untracked under docs/references/ per
.gitignore) carries no usable text layer: it is an image scan of the UMI
microfilm copy.  The text below was produced with Google Cloud Vision
DOCUMENT_TEXT_DETECTION run over 300 dpi page renderings, one API call per
PDF page, with language hints en, is, de.

PAGE NUMBERING
--------------
Markers below use the dissertation's own PRINTED page numbers, not PDF
sheet numbers.  The offset is constant: printed page N = PDF sheet N + 11.
It was verified mechanically -- on 269 of the 282 numbered body sheets the
printed folio appears as the literal first or last line of the OCR of the
expected sheet, and the 13 exceptions are OCR line-ordering artefacts, not
offset breaks.  Front matter is given as the roman folios actually printed
(iii-ix); PDF sheets 1-4 (UMI order sheet, title page, copyright page) are
unnumbered and are marked as such.

OCR CAVEAT
----------
Vision handles the running prose well but is unreliable on this work's
heavy phonetic and diacritic load.  Macrons, ogoneks, subscript diacritics,
Greek, Sanskrit transliteration and the IE laryngeal subscripts are
frequently dropped, doubled or mis-segmented; PIE *w is routinely rendered
as Greek or Cyrillic lookalikes, and italic *j/*i are confusable.  Any form
quoted from this file into a CAPR memo or reader chapter must be checked
against the page image before it is relied on.  Footnote text is generally
captured but its reading order relative to the body is not guaranteed.

KEY PASSAGES FOR CAPR (SC102, hiatus-breaking *w in the verba pura)
-------------------------------------------------------------------
Chapter 5, "The w-forms of the verba pura and the Anglo-Frisian evidence",
runs printed pp. 114-137 (PDF 125-148).  Sections: 5.1 Forms with w (114);
5.1.1 Earlier theories (115); 5.2 The Old English and Old Frisian evidence
(124); 5.2.1 The Old English w-forms (124); 5.2.2 The Old Frisian present
(128); 5.3 Conclusion (135).

The load-bearing statements for CAPR are:

* p. 120 -- the phonological explanation is credited to Bremer 1886: 72-73:
  "w arose as a phonetic hiatus filler in forms where u followed the long
  root vowel.  The forms in question are the 1sg. and 1pl. present
  indicative (confirmed by OHG 1sg. kra:uu, lo:uu) and the plural forms of
  the reduplicated preterite with the endings *-um, *-uth, *-un".

* p. 126 -- the same insertion in the preterite: "The development to the
  stem *seuw is most likely to have been the simple insertion of a
  hiatus-breaking *w before the *u of the plural endings (e.g., 3pl.
  *seu-un > *seuwun), which was generalized in the preterite paradigm."

* p. 127 -- the pre-OE present indicative paradigm is given with the hiatus
  intact (1sg. *sa:'u, 2sg. *sa:'is, 3sg. *sa:'ith, 1pl. *sa:'um, 2pl.
  *sa:'ith, 3pl. *sa:'anth), and then: "The glide w was a natural hiatus
  filler before the u of the endings of the 1st person singular and plural,
  in particular in verbs with the vowel *o:; hence, 1sg. *ro:wu, 1pl.
  *ro:wum.  The *w of the preterite probably would have assisted in
  extending the new w to all forms of the present paradigm of the verba
  pura and to the preterite participle.  In addition, the existence of
  verbs with an etymological *w, at least OE flo:wan (the root PIE *pleu-),
  may have played an important role."

* p. 123 -- the Old High German w is explicitly NOT the same event: "it is
  by no means necessary to regard the completely generalized w in the Old
  English verba pura and the scattered w-forms in Old High German (East
  Franconian) as having the same origin. ... The w in Old English is likely
  to be an older phenomenon."  Cf. pp. 114 and 119 to the same effect.

* pp. 130, 134 -- Old Frisian is derived from the same generalized type:
  "an Anglo-Frisian inflection with the hiatus breaker *w generalized
  throughout the paradigm" (130); "the Anglo-Frisian type of present with
  *w in every form" (134).

* p. 136 -- the summary statement: "The Proto-Germanic hiatus was broken by
  *w in the position before *u, which was generalized and resulted in a
  structure pre-OE inf. *sa:wan - 3sg. pret. *seuw - 3pl. pret. *seuwun -
  pret. ptc. *sa:wan- (OE sa:wan, etc.).  Old Frisian shared in the
  generalization of *w".

Note for CAPR: the infinitive, which is CAPR's citation form for `sow`, is
NOT one of the cells that receives *w by the phonological rule.  Its *w is
analogical.  Chapter 5 nowhere discusses the fronting of *a: (CAPR SC101),
so it supplies no independent evidence for the SC102 < SC101 ordering.

=== page iii ===
BIOGRAPHICAL SKETCH
Gudrún Thórhallsdóttir was born in Reykjavík, Iceland. She
graduated from Menntaskólinn í Reykjavík (junior college) in 1980 and
received her B.A. degree in Linguistics and Icelandic from the
University of Iceland (Háskóli Íslands) in 1984. In the fall of 1984 she
went to Cornell University, where she taught Old Norse and was
admitted to the Ph.D. program in the Field of Linguistics in 1985. In
1991 she was appointed Assistant Professor in the Department of
Icelandic Language and Literature at the University of Iceland,
Reykjavík.
iii

I wish to express my gratitude to the many people who have
contributed to my education and the development of my dissertation
over the years.
I would like to thank my former teachers at the University of
Iceland for their instruction and advice, in particular, Jón Gunnarsson,
Hreinn Benediktsson, and the late Jörundur Hilmarsson, who introduced
me to Indo-European studies and comparative Germanic linguistics. I
continued to enjoy support and encouragement from my professors and
colleagues in Iceland during my years at Cornell.
I thank my Cornell professors and other members of the
Department of Modern Languages and Linguistics for seeing me
through my graduate education, and my fellow students in Linguistics
and Classics for their company and moral support, among them Michael
Weiss and Þórhallur Eyþórsson for Indo-Europeanist solidarity.
In 1989, when my dissertation research was starting, I had the
opportunity to spend a semester at the University of Vienna. I wish to
thank faculty, staff, and students at the Institut für Sprachwissenschaft
der Universität Wien for making my semester in Vienna pleasant and
scholarly stimulating. Above all, I am grateful to Professor Jochem
Schindler for generous help and inspiring discussions.
This dissertation would not exist without the members of my
special committee. I thank Wayne Harbert for serving on my

his advice. I owe a great debt to Alan Nussbaum for contributing his

=== page 1 ===
CHAPTER 1
INTRODUCTION
1.1 The history of the problem
The development of the Indo-European semivowels *i and *u into
the Germanic daughter languages has been a traditional matter of
controversy. The most famous problems are probably Verschärfung
--the origin of the geminates *-jj- and *-ww- between vowels in Proto-
Germanic and their outcomes in the Germanic dialects--and the effects
of Sievers' Law in Germanic, the distribution of the suffix variants *-j-
and *-ij- according to the length of the preceding syllable. The main
subject of this dissertation, the development of single intervocalic *j in
Proto-Germanic, has not been given the same attention, and yet the
matter is far from having been solved and settled. Anyone who tries to
extract from the literature the communis opinio will encounter diffi-
culties, for many handbooks are silent on the subject or give vague,
noncommittal statements in their sections on semivowels, which means
that the discussion of forms containing intervocalic *j in morphology
sections can be quite confusing. To take a few random examples,
Streitberg in his Urgermanische Grammatik discussed Verschärfung and
Sievers' Law in the section on nonsyllabic ** and *u, but gave no
explicit account of single intervocalic *j (Streitberg 1896, 59-63). The
1

=== page 2 ===
2
same applies to the three pages devoted to the semivowels in Prokosch's
Comparative Germanic Grammar.¹ In Germanische Sprachwissenschaft
by Krahe and Meid it is stated that intervocalic *j, unless serving as
"Übergangslaut" after *i, was lost early in many cases, especially before
front vowels, without any explanation of what is meant by "in many
cases" and "early."2 Etymological dictionaries can also be difficult to
interpret. A reader who looks up the entry for the German verb säen
'to sow' in the most recent edition of Kluge's Etymologisches Wörter-
buch der deutschen Sprache (Kluge 1989, 613) finds it reconstructed as
PGmc. *sæ-(j)a-, but is not given any information about the origin or
disappearance of the intervocalic *j that appears in parentheses.
A thorough investigation of the development of Proto-Germanic
intervocalic *j is properly begun by reviewing the research history of
the subject and looking at rules which have been proposed to explain the
loss of intervocalic *j in the Proto-Germanic period.
1 Prokosch brought up the development of PIE *-eie- to PGmc. *-ji- in
his discussion of Sievers' Law and also mentioned that not every inter-
vocalic *j or *w is subject to Verschärfung (Go. ais ‘bronze' < *ajaz,
Go. bajōps ‘both,' Go. frijōn ‘to love'), but did not give any rules for
the treatment of single intervocalic *j (Prokosch 1939, 90-93).
2 "Sonst ist intervokales j, besonders vor hellen Vokalen, vielfach
frühzeitig geschwunden" (Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 1:96). One example is
given, PIE *ajeri ‘early' > PGmc. *ajir(i) > Go. air, where *j may
appear to have been lost in Proto-Germanic, but the matter is not
discussed any further. The volume on morphology refers to this rule
and thus adds examples, e.g., in the discussion of the long-stem ja-stems
(Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 2:14), but does not bring the reader closer to a
well-defined formulation.

=== page 3 ===
1.2 The nineteenth century
3
In the late nineteenth century, the view was fairly widespread that
PIE ** between vowels had been lost in many instances in the develop-
ment of the Germanic languages. A representative of this attitude is
Scherer's work Zur Geschichte der deutschen Sprache (1868), where the
author referred to the loss of *j between vowels in various contexts
without specifying further conditions or the exact time when the change
took place.³
Among Scherer's examples of an early loss of intervocalic *j
were the
present stems of the three classes of weak verbs, all of which
were formed, in his opinion, with a suffix containing *j (Class I *-ija-
3 Scherer gave the following comment on the chronology: "Die
Abneigung gegen das j zwischen Vocalen ist im Germ. jünger als
mindestens der erste Act des vocalischen Auslautsgesetzes." This was
based on the evidence of, e.g., i-stem gen. sg. *anstajas > Go. anstais
and Class I 2sg. imperat. *sandija > Go. sandei, where the last vowel
appears to have been lost before the loss of intervocalic *j could affect
the forms. He added a footnote: "Doch dürfte es am gerathensten sein,
von jeder näheren chronologischen Bestimmung über den Ausfall des j
vorläufig abzustehen, da er wohl niemals unbedingte Regel war, also zu
sehr verschiedenen Zeiten eintreten konnte." (op. cit., 113, 179-80 with
n.).
It is obvious from the examples from Scherer's work shown in
this section that he followed the views of his time on the Proto-Indo-
European vowel system; I have not updated his reconstructing *a for
PIE *e and *o, etc.

=== page 4 ===
4
> *-ia-/*-ja-, Class II *-aja-> *-aa- with subsequent contraction to *-ō-,
Class III *-aji-> *-ai-) (op. cit., 179).4 Scherer connected the preterite
form Go. iddja 'went,' following Holtzmann and Müllenhoff, with the
Sanskrit perfect iyaya ‘id.' and claimed that the second *j was lost with
a subsequent contraction to *ā in the development of the Gothic form
(PIE *ijaja > *ijaa > *ijā> *ija> iddja) (op. cit., 204-5). He assumed
the same contraction in the ending of the 1sg. present optative, which he
reconstructed as PIE *-ajam (cf. Skt. -eyam; *-ajam> *-aam> *-ām
> Go. -au) (op. cit., 472). The ending of the genitive plural of i-stems
was considered to have undergone a contraction of *-ajā- to *-ā-
(*-ajām> *-aām > *-ām > Go. -ē) (op. cit., 421).5
It is not surprising that such vague assumptions of a loss of
intervocalic *j without exact definitions of conditions or chronology--it
is, e.g., not obvious why *-aja- sometimes contracted to *-ō- and some-
times to *-ā- in the examples above--called for revisions in the 1870's
when the aim for regular sound laws was the fashion.
1.2.1 Mahlow's sound law
The first actual sound law to get lasting publicity involving the
loss of intervocalic *j in Proto-Germanic was proposed by Mahlow in
4 The contraction of *-aja- to *-ō- supposedly had a parallel in the
comparative suffix PGmc. *-ōz- and the superlative suffix PGmc.
*-ōsta- (op. cit., 187-88).
5 Scherer was by no means the author of all the reconstructions above,
nor was he the first to assume j-loss and contractions in all these cases.
His book is simply used here to demonstrate opinions typical for this
period.

=== page 5 ===
5
his work Die langen Vocale a e o in den europaeischen Sprachen (1879,
43-44). Mahlow stipulated a Proto-Germanic sound law deleting every
*j before and after *i, except perhaps in word-initial position.6 This
rule was essential to his account of the Germanic weak verbs of Class II.
Mahlow used the rule to explain the seemingly j-less present tense forms
(*-ōji-> *-ōi-> *-ō-, e.g., in 2sg. pres. ind. Go. salbos, ON kallar,
OHG salbos, OE sealfast); he assumed that all forms of the present tense
derived from forms containing the suffix PGmc. *-ōji/a- (e.g., 1sg.
pres. ind. WGmc. *salbõju > OE sealfize). Among other examples of
Proto-Germanic j-loss are present tense forms of the weak verbs of
Class I where *j was lost intervocalically following *i (1sg. pres. ind.
*nazija> *nazia > Go. nasja, 2sg. *nazijiz> *naziiz > Go. nasjis), and
forms where *j was lost after a stressed vowel (*ajiza-> Go. aiza-), but
the rule was not limited to intervocalic *j (PGmc. *bidjan with 2sg.
pres. ind. *bidiz < *bidjiz).
Mahlow's rule was a defined sound law, an improvement in that
respect over vague statements such as Scherer's, but it did not meet with
universal acceptance. The reaction was partly affected by the com-
peting claim that *j was lost before *i, except in word-initial position, in
West Germanic only (Paul 1880, 112, 160-61; a similar opinion by
Sievers 1878, 126-27). This rule accounted for the absence of the West
Germanic consonant gemination in, e.g., present tense forms of weak
verbs of Class I where *i had followed *j (2sg. pres. ind. *taljis/z(i) >
OHG zelis vs. inf. *taljan > OHG zellen 'to count, say'); it was assumed
6 "Um diese Zeit kommt ein Lautgesetz zur Geltung, wonach jedes j
vor und hinter i verloren gehen muss, ausgenommen vielleicht im
Anlaut" (Mahlow 1879, 43).

=== page 6 ===
6
that *j had been lost too early to cause gemination. Some scholars were
hesitant to express definite opinions on the antiquity of the loss of *j in
this environment (e.g., Bethge in Dieter 1898-1900, 1:188, Hartmann in
Dieter 1898-1900, 1:307; Kluge 1901, 427), others have questioned
Mahlow's evidence (Bremer 1886, 44-45, n. 2) or doubted the possi-
bility that this j-loss could have been Proto-Germanic (Lindeman 1967,
278, n. 8).
Mahlow's rule of Proto-Germanic j-loss before *i 7 was accepted
by such scholars as Osthoff (1881, 27), Noreen (1894, 176), Trautmann
(1906, 61),8 and Wissmann (1932, 199),⁹ to name a few from different
periods. On the whole it appears that in the late nineteenth century the
move was away from vague ideas of intervocalic j-loss that could take
place at various stages. It is, for instance, interesting to follow
Brugmann's views from the time when he was ready to suggest an
extensive loss of PGmc. *j between unstressed vowels¹0 to more
restrictive rules and cautious statements. In his early writings he
7 In discussions of the treatment of PGmc. *j scholars commonly refer
to Mahlow's sound law as a rule of j-loss before *i but treat the devel-
opment of *j following *i separately.
8 Trautmann stated the rule as follows: "j vor i ist geschwunden,
nachdem e in nicht haupttoniger silbe zu i geworden war; danach wurde
i + i zu î kontrahiert." He demonstrated it by the examples Go. air
‘early' < PIE *ájeri, Go. aiz ‘bronze' < *ajiz- < PIE *ájes-, Go. preis
'three' (perhaps) < PIE *tréjes.
9 Wissmann merely discussed Mahlow's account of the weak verbs of
Class II, but did not evaluate his list of examples.
10 In the first edition of his Grundriss Brugmann discussed the
problematic 1sg. present optative ending Go. -au, ON -a, and did not
reject the derivation PIE *-oim > *-aiu > *-au for the reason that it
violated established sound laws but suggested that "i wäre intervocalisch
in unbetonter Silbe geschwunden" (1886-1900, 2,2:1294
with n. 1).

=== page 7 ===
7
assumed j-loss and contraction in the 1sg. present optative ending,
comparing it with Skt. -eyam (1878, 246-47; 1886-1900, 2,2:1294 with
n. 1). He also agreed with the older view of a contraction of *-aja- to
*-ō- in the derivation of the suffix of Class II of weak verbs and the
comparative suffix (1879, 57, cf. the discussion of Scherer above).
Later he restricted the loss of PGmc. *j to intervocalic position before
*i (e.g., PGmc. *prijiz> *priiz> Go. preis ‘three'), finding j-loss
between a consonant and *i likely, but not certain (1897-1916, 1:284-
87); he abandoned his earlier opinions of the derivation of the examples
above in favor of explanations which did not require a loss of inter-
vocalic *j.
1.3 Recent years
No consensus has been reached on the development of single
intervocalic *j in Proto-Germanic. Examples which fall under
Mahlow's Proto-Germanic sound law on the loss of intervocalic *j
before and after *i, often appear in 20th century literature, but,
typically, the statements on the possibility of a Proto-Germanic j-loss
are vague, and the standard handbooks do not give detailed accounts of
the problem. Quite characteristic is, e.g., Kieckers' discussion (1928,
40) of some of the prime examples; he cautiously suggested a Proto-
Germanic loss of intervocalic *j "to a certain extent."11 Hirt (1931,
11 "In gewissem Umfang ist vielleicht schon urgerm. į zwischen
Vokalen geschwunden. So in -ii- in alter Mittelsilbe nach betontem
Vokal" (e.g., PIE *ajes-> PGmc. *aiz- ‘bronze'), and: "Nach

=== page 8 ===
8
116) was noncommittal on the antiquity of j-loss before *i, and Krahe
and Meid surprisingly brief and vague.12
The best-known contribution in recent years to the formulation of
sound changes involving the loss of Proto-Germanic intervocalic *j is
probably a sound law proposed by Cowgill in the years 1959-1960.
However, his writings did not contain a thorough investigation of the
development of intervocalic *j, and the rule has not been universally
accepted.
1.3.1 Cowgill's sound law
In two articles in Language (1959, 1960a), Cowgill proposed a
new formulation of the loss of Proto-Germanic intervocalic *j. He
accepted Mahlow's sound law but added a rule whereby "*y was lost
between vowels of noninitial syllables (except in *iy before a vowel
other than *1)" (1960a, 493-94).13 This sound change took place after
unbetontem i ist i ebenfalls früh, vielleicht schon urgerm.,
geschwunden" (e.g., PGmc. *-ije-> *-ie-). On the other hand,
Kieckers accounted for the word-final sequence *-iiiz by an early loss
of the second *i (i-stem nom. pl. *-iiiz> *-iiz> *-īz), but with similar
hesitation (loc. cit.).
12 The handbook predicts the loss of "intervokales j, besonders vor
hellen Vokalen, vielfach frühzeitig" (Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 1:96, cf.
section 1.1 above).
13 When Cowgill first introduced his rule (1959, 6), he gave the

position, but later in the paper he referred to "loss of intervocalic *j

quite unambiguous.

syllables" is equivalent to "unaccented vowels"). 14
Cowgill's evidence for this loss of intervocalic *j had three
sources. First of all, Cowgill was of the opinion (in this respect
agreeing with Mahlow) that all forms of the present tense of weak verbs
of Class II derived from the thematic suffix PGmc. *-ōji/a- (< PIE
*-eh₂-ie/o-, cf. Skt. 3sg. pṛtanāyáti, Gk. 1sg. лµáάw, OCS 1sg. dělajo̟).
He assumed, however, that every form underwent j-loss and contraction
already in Proto-Germanic (1sg. pres. ind. *-ōjō > *-ōō > *-ō, 3sg.
*-ōjið/þi > *-ōïð/þi > *-ōð/þi, 3pl. *-ōjanð/þi > *-ōanð/þi > *-ōnð/þi).
He explained Ingveonic forms which earlier had commonly been inter-
preted as preserving an inherited *j (e.g., pl. pres. ind. OE -iap) as an
innovation, secondarily modeled on long-stem weak verbs of Class I
(the stem *lapōja- having arisen from the pattern *dōmi-: *dōmija-,
*laþō- : X; X = *laþōja-) (1959, 5-9).
Second, in Cowgill's opinion, Go. iddja and OE ēode, the
preterite of the verb 'to go,' derived from a pre-Germanic active
perfect created to the root PIE *h,ei-, 1sg. *eója, 3sg. *eóje. These
forms were subject to j-loss when the accent had moved to the initial
syllable (> *eaja, *eaje> *eaa, *eae), and both contracted to PGmc. *eō
14 Cowgill initially specified further that *j had been lost "after the
change of *e to *i because the outcome of PIE *eye in Germanic is ī,
not *ē (OIc. þrír, Go. gasteis, frawardeiþ)" (1959, 9, n. 17), but
changed this later (1960a, 494 with n. 31).

Cowgill's third example was the 1sg. present optative ending Go.

schärfung in this form in spite of the hiatus after the loss of the
laryngeal (PIE *-oih,m > *-ai̟'u¹) is probably due to the fact that the
preceding vowel *a was unstressed (cf. Cowgill op. cit., 7, n. 12;
Rasmussen 1990, 427).15
1.3.2 Criticism of Cowgill's sound law
Scholars who have been hesitant to accept Cowgill's sound law
have for the most part based their doubts on the scarcity of the
evidence.
15 This origin of the ending is a Neogrammarian suggestion. Paul
(1877, 378) reconstructed PIE *-ajm and assumed a loss of *j (*-ajm
> *-a-um > *-au). Paul also assumed Proto-Germanic j-loss in his
discussion of the Germanic weak verbs and came to the same conclusion
as Cowgill concerning the verbs of Class II: "Die gesetze für die
contraction sind dann sehr einfach. Nämlich ô verschlingt überall den
thematischen vocal sogar mit moduselement, was für einen langen vocal
wol begreiflich ist" (op. cit., 377, n. 1). Later Paul formulated a rule
of j-loss: "j zwischen zwei vocalen, von denen der erste auf schwacher
stufe steht, fällt aus" where a syllable "auf schwacher stufe" bears the
lowest of three degrees of stress (Paul 1879, 131, 161). This sound law
is thus sensitive to Paul's stress rules but not to the quality of the neigh-
boring vowels. It is therefore not exactly equivalent to Cowgill's
formulation, but it is remarkable that they both account for the loss of
intervocalic *j in the Class II suffix *-ōji/a- and the 1sg. present opta-
tive ending. In Paul's opinion, the suffixes of the weak verbs of Class I
(*-iji/a-) and Class III (*-aji/a-) lost *j by his rule as well.

17 Fullerton actually held that the athematic inflection was the source of

Upper German optatives of the type 1sg. salbō(g)ē are an innovation,
formed by adding thematic endings to the inherited stem in *-ō-; the *j
was inserted to separate the *-ō- from the vowel of the ending. Al-

paradigm, in that it derives the Class II verbs from a single prototype,
his explanation is otherwise unconvincing. For instance, in order to
derive all forms of the present indicative paradigm from an athematic
inflection, he assumed an unfounded phonological development of 1sg.
PGmc. *-ōmi to Gothic, Proto-Norse -ō. He also accounted for the

extremely implausible. An optative marker PGmc. *w has no direct
Indo-European cognates, its creation in Germanic lacks justification,
and it is by no means clear why its use would only have been gener-
alized in the weak verbs of Class II.

1.3.3 Support for Cowgill's sound law
Cowgill's sound law was received favorably by many scholars,
some of whom have suggested slight modifications of his formulation,
and further examples of the effects of the loss of intervocalic *j have
been provided.
Hock (1973, 332-33, 348), essentially agreeing with Cowgill's
formulation of intervocalic j-loss, assumed that the change occurred in
21 Cf. the reconstructions Skt. -eya < PIE *-oi-h₂e (the primary middle

-eya and Gk. -ota < PIE *-oi-h₂o (Watkins 1969, 231, where the color
of the laryngeal is not specified).
22 On the other hand, an early loss of the laryngeal would rather have
produced Skt. *-aya (the -m of -eyam is a secondary addition) and Gk.

two stages, an assumption which was important to his account of the
development of Class III weak verbs. Hock operated with a pre-
Germanic suffix *-ǝje/o- (suggested by Bennett 1962), i.e., the zero
grade of the stative suffix PIE *-eh,- with *-je/o- added. As he wanted
2sg. pres. *-ǝjis/z to yield *-ais/z, but 1sg. pres. *-ǝjō to yield *-jō, he
ordered the loss of intervocalic *j before front vowels only (*-ǝjis/z
> *-ǝis/z) before the syncope of * in medial syllables (*-ǝjō> *-jō).
Later, intervocalic *j was lost before back vowels. Hock did not
support his assumption by any further examples, but merely pointed out
that "there seems to be no counterevidence" (1973, 348). Hock's contri-
bution to Cowgill's rule cannot be considered a well-founded improve-
ment, in that his evidence lacks phonological parallels. Moreover, its
morphological basis is not sound either, for Bennett's reconstruction of
the present suffix of Class III, pre-Gmc. *-ǝje/o-, is doubtful, if only
for the reason that laryngeals were lost very early in medial position (a
feature shared by Germanic, Balto-Slavic, Iranian, and Armenian) (cf.
the criticism in Jasanoff 1978b, 65).
Dishington (1976, 851-65) made use of Cowgill's rule to account
for a subgroup of the Germanic weak verbs of Class III, the factitives
of the type Go. gaþiwan ‘to enslave,' which he interpreted as continuing
Indo-European denominative presents in *-o-je/o- (3sg. gaþiwaiþ
< *-o-je-ti, cf. Gk. Sovλóɛ ‘enslaves'). In PGmc. *-aji/a-, the con-
tinuant of PIE *-o-je/o, Mahlow's j-loss before *i would have taken
place in forms with *-aji- (cf. 3sg. pres. ind. Go. -aip), but Cowgill's
additional rule was needed to explain *-aja-> *-aa- (3pl. pres. ind. Go.
-and). Dishington cautiously assumed a contraction of *-aa- to either

*-ō- or *-ā- depending on the chronology of the loss of intervocalic *j,
but the outcome does not seem to be crucial to his argument. 23 The
morphological foundation of Dishington's explanation, the comparison
with the Greek verbs in -ów, is hardly sound, however, since these do
not represent an inherited type, but were apparently backformed from
adjectives in -wrós (Schwyzer 1939, 727, as pointed out by Jasanoff
1978b, 87 with n. 60).
Hollifield (1980a, 36-38), who accepted Cowgill's explanation of
the weak verbs of Class II and his reconstruction of the 1sg. present
optative, gave a narrow formulation of the loss of intervocalic *j. He
restricted the preceding vowels to those of which there were actual
examples, unaccented *ā (presumably the pre-Gmc. *ā of the suffix
*-ā-je/o-) and *o (by the same token, the pre-Gmc. *o of *-oih,m).24
Hollifield thus clearly accepted Cowgill's j-loss in essence.

1.3.4 Conclusion on Cowgill's rule
The above discussion indicates that serious, compelling improve-
ments of Cowgill's sound law have not been put forward and additional
evidence that could definitely prove or disprove its validity has not
emerged. Some scholars have actively made use of the rule, as shown
above, whereas others have taken a sharp position against it.27
Cowgill proposed his sound law in two articles aimed at
explaining the origin of the weak verbs of Class II (Cowgill 1959) and
the preterite forms Go. iddja and OE ēode (Cowgill 1960a), not at
accounting once and for all for the development of intervocalic *j.
26 This Greek form shows analogical restoration of the diphthong. Gk.
*-oɛv with loss of intervocalic *i would have been expected.
27 Lindeman was thus not only unwilling to accept Cowgill's rule
because of lack of evidence, but also declared that Mahlow's loss of *j
before *i in Proto-Germanic "scheint vielmehr einzeldialektisch zu sein"
(1967, 278 with n. 8). Fullerton's harsh words and his alleged counter-
evidence (1977, 49) are in part due to a misunderstanding of Cowgill's
definition of the rule; Cowgill's initial introduction of the rule is mis-
leading (cf. section 1.3.1 above).

=== page 18 ===
18
Admittedly, the rule is not supported by many examples, but the
evidence is significant and serious counterexamples have not been
demonstrated. The development of Go. iddja and OE code is perhaps
still an open question, but the key idea of Cowgill's explanation of the
ō-verbs, viz., that every form lost intervocalic *j in Proto-Germanic,
and that Ingveonic ōja-forms are a dialectal analogical innovation, is a
convincing contribution. Equating the ending 1sg. pres. opt. PGmc.
*-au with forms in Sanskrit and Greek under the reconstruction PIE
*-oi̟h,m has much to recommend it and, as shown above, does not
encounter major difficulties in any of the languages.
Cowgill's rule of j-loss in noninitial syllables is thus very
plausible, and can be considered the best account to date of the treat-
ment of intervocalic *j after unstressed vowels in Proto-Germanic. One
can therefore assume a phonological loss of *j between vowels of non-
initial syllables (unstressed vowels), except where the preceding vowel
was *i and a different vowel followed. PGmc. *j was thus lost in such
examples as inf. *salbōjan> *salbō'an and 3sg. pres. ind. *salbōjið/þi
> *salbō'ið/þi, but retained in inf. *dōmijan.
1.4 Intervocalic *j after vowels of initial syllables
The treatment of intervocalic PGmc. *j after vowels of initial
syllables has not been given much attention in recent years. Mahlow's
rule of j-loss before and after *i accounts for some classic examples
which are cited in the handbooks, but, as already mentioned, typically
without firm statements on the conditions for the loss of single inter-

=== page 19 ===
19
vocalic *j after stressed vowels. Mahlow's rule accounts for this loss of
*j as follows (cf. Mahlow 1879, 43):
Single intervocalic *j was lost in Proto-Germanic after the vowel
of an initial syllable if the vowel *i preceded and/or followed *j.
Examples:
nom. pl. m. *prijiz
s-stem nom. sg. *ajiz
3sg. pres. ind. *sējið/þi
> *pri'iz 'three'
> *a'iz ‘bronze'
> *sē'ið/pi ‘sows'
Cowgill's sound law was limited to the loss of intervocalic *j in
the position between unstressed vowels, and it was specified, accord-
ingly, that *j had not been lost until the accent had been shifted to initial
syllables. This restriction was based on the apparent retention of Proto-
Germanic *j after vowels of initial syllables when a vowel other than *i
followed. Cowgill's examples of retention include Go. waja- in the
compound wajamēreins 'blasphemy,' bajōps ‘both,' Olcel. deyja 'to die,'
OS sāian, OHG säen 'to sow' ("phonemically /sæ:en/, from PGmc.
*sējana-") (1960a, 494) and further Go. ajukdūps ‘eternity' (1973, 296,
n. 51). Cowgill did not elaborate on these examples, and scholars
commenting on his rule have concentrated on j-loss in noninitial
syllables without expressing their opinions on the development of *j
after stressed vowels. One might therefore conclude that there is
general, albeit largely silent, agreement on the evidence just cited as
demonstrating the retention of Proto-Germanic intervocalic *j after
vowels of initial syllables.

=== page 20 ===
20
On the other hand, it is by no means the case that Proto-Germanic
intervocalic *j after stressed vowels was preserved unchanged into the
attested stages of the Germanic daughter languages in every environ-
ment where earlier rules of Proto-Germanic j-loss have predicted its
preservation. 28 Dialectal j-loss rules have thus been proposed to
account for its absence in one or more dialects. For instance, both
Mahlow and Cowgill derived OHG inf. stēn, stān ‘to stand' from a j-
present PGmc. *stajan through, in Cowgill's formulation, "West
Germanic loss of single intervocalic Proto-Germanic *j, followed by
contraction of aa to ā, later of course than the change of pre-Germanic
*ā to [0:]" (Cowgill 1973, 296, cf. Mahlow 1879, 138-39).29 To take a
second example, the verb PGmc. *sējan ‘to sow' supposedly retained *j
into North and West Germanic *sajan, as shown by OS saian and, with a
change of *-ja- to -e-, OHG säen, forms which are among Cowgill's
examples of retention (cf. above). On the other hand, Gothic is
commonly considered to have lost intervocalic *j after *ē in the verb
saian (e.g., Streitberg 1896, 76; Bethge in Dieter 1898-1900, 1:208).
Furthermore, the infinitive form in Old Norse, sá, and Old English,
sāwan, as well as certain forms in Old High German, e.g., inf. sãan, do
not show an overt trace of the *j and have been explained by phono-
logical and analogical processes particular to the individual dialects.
28 E.g., Mahlow's conditions of PGmc. j-loss before and after *i,
Krahe/Meid's vague conditions "especially before front vowels"
(Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 1:96), etc.
29 As a second example of this loss of intervocalic *j after *a, Cowgill
mentioned OE (Angl.) ēci ‘eternal' (< WGmc. *auki < PGmc.
*ajukijaz), where the comparison with the Gothic compound ajukdūps
‘eternity' was considered to show that this j-loss was limited to West
Germanic (loc. cit., n. 51).

=== page 21 ===
21
This state of affairs, with various rules of j-loss after vowels of
initial syllables postulated for the Germanic daughter languages, invites
the supposition that the loss of intervocalic *j in the Proto-Germanic
period was more extensive than earlier rules suggest. In other words,
there is reason to suspect that intervocalic *j was widely lost after
stressed vowels in Proto-Germanic already.
The claim that will be defended in this dissertation is that inter-
vocalic j-loss was in fact perfectly general in Proto-Germanic, and that
all apparent exceptions are analogical. Our phonological hypothesis can
be stated as follows:
Single *j was lost in Proto-Germanic between vowels.

As for the chronology, Cowgill's criteria seem plausible. It can
also be assumed that the general loss of intervocalic *j was later than
Grimm's Law and Verner's Law, the merger of *ǎ and *ŏ and *ā and
*ō, and--although this is not as crucial for this formulation as it was for
Cowgill's--the shift of stress to initial syllables.31
Our proposed hypothesis has an important advantage; it collapses
into one the rules proposed by Mahlow32 and Cowgill and states the
Proto-Germanic process in a much simpler way. The approach we shall
follow, projecting the loss of intervocalic *j after accented vowels back
into Proto-Germanic in spite of alleged counterevidence and thus
making the rule as general as possible, is in fact the same method we
have witnessed in Cowgill's solution to the problem of the Class II weak
verbs (cf. section 1.3.1). Cowgill claimed that a general Proto-
Germanic j-loss in unstressed position took place in all the present tense
forms (inf. Go. salbōn) and considered forms with apparent *j on the
surface (inf. OS ladoian 'to invite') to be an Ingveonic analogical

1.4.1 Defining the rule: Retention of intervocalic *j
In the new formulation of Proto-Germanic j-loss proposed above
the rule was stated as the loss of intervocalic PGmc. *j across the board.
One trivial exception can instantly be noted: there does not seem to be a
compelling reason to assume a loss of intervocalic PGmc. *j in
examples where *j was preceded by a stressed short *i and followed by
a vowel other than *i. Such sequences have j spelled on the surface in
Gothic and Runic Norse, and the other Germanic languages do not
provide counterexamples. The numeral ‘three' provides examples of
PGmc. *-íjV-, cf. nom.-acc. pl. n. Go. þrija, nom. pl. f. Pr.-N. þrijōr
(Tune, ca. 400 A.D., Krause 1966, 162-67; 1971, 94). Several other
examples of PGmc. *-íjV- come from weak verbs, notably including the
following:
Go. fijan Cl. III ‘to hate' can be reconstructed as PGmc. *fijan or

‘id.' This is a zero-grade formation of the root PIE *peh,- 'to hurt,
blame' (Pokorny's root *pē(i)-, *pī- (1959, 792-93); i.e., presumably
PIE *peh,-i-, etc.). 34 The ultimate origin of PGmc. *fijën might be a

When Cowgill introduced his rule of j-loss, he mentioned the
possibility that "*j was at first lost after all unaccented vowels,
including *i, and then a new [i] arose as a glide between *i and a
following low or back vowel" (1959, 6, n. 11).42 Of course, the same
possibility applies to the development of *j after stressed vowels and
would allow the rule to be stated simply as the loss of intervocalic
PGmc. *j across the board. This would, however, require a loss of *j
and subsequent glide insertion (nom.-acc. pl. n. PGmc. *þrijō> *þri'ō
> *prijō ‘three,' inf. PGmc. *dōmijan> *dōmi'an > *dōmijan ‘to
judge') for which there is no tangible evidence.
The slightly restricted sound law can therefore be formulated as
follows:
Single *j was lost in Proto-Germanic between vowels, except
where the preceding vowel was *i and a different vowel
followed.43
42 According to Mahlow's rule, *j was lost after *i, whichever vowel
followed (e.g., 1sg. pres. ind. *nazija > *nazia > Go. nasja (Mahlow
1879, 43)).
43 In fact, this formulation is identical to Cowgill's sound law, apart
from the important factor that the condition of stress has been
eliminated.

1.4.2 PGmc. *j after *ī and i-diphthongs
As PIE *ei was represented in Proto-Germanic by the monoph-
thong *I and long diphthongs were shortened before *j by Osthoff's
Law, the only instance of an i-diphthong followed by *j is PGmc.
*-aij V-. This sequence constitutes a Verschärfung environment, as does
PGmc. *-īja-, cf. the outcome of the word for ‘egg,' pre-Gmc.
*(h)ōhijo-> *ōijo-> *aija-> *ajja-> Olcel. egg, OHG ei, and the
name of the goddess Olcel. Frigg, OE Frīz (PIE *preih₂-eh₂-> *frījō-
> PGmc. *frijjō-> Olcel. Frigg, OE Frī3) (Jasanoff 1978a, 85-87).44
The relative chronology of intervocalic j-loss and j-gemination within
44 *-aija- seems to have undergone Verschärfung whether the glide was
original (*dhoih,-eje/o-> *dai'ija-> *daijijan> *daijan (with j-loss
before *1) > *dajjan > Go. daddjan, OSw. dæggia ‘to suckle') or had
arisen as a hiatus filler after the loss of a laryngeal (*uoih,-> *waiju-

=== page 29 ===
29
Proto-Germanic cannot be verified with certainty, but it does not have
grave consequences for the formulation of j-loss. In principle, there
are two possibilities. It is thinkable that *-aija- had become *-ajja- and
*-īja- had become *-ijja- before the rule of the loss of single inter-
vocalic *j became active; in that case such examples can be ignored
altogether. On the other hand, if these sequences still had the form
*-aija- and *-īja- when j-loss started to operate, *-ai- and *-ī- can be
included in the formulation of the rule, which might be stated as
follows:
Single intervocalic *j was lost in Proto-Germanic, except when
preceded by *i, *ī, or *ai and followed by a vowel other than *i.
From a phonological point of view, the exceptions to the rule are
perfectly plausible; it is exactly in this environment that *i is most likely
to develop as a glide and least likely to be lost.
1.5 The task
The claim that intervocalic *j was generally lost in Proto-
Germanic following a stressed vowel must be based on a thorough
investigation of the relevant forms. This applies, of course, to attested
forms appearing with alleged inherited intervocalic *j following the
vowel of an initial syllable, and to forms which show no j on the surface
> *wajju-> Go. -waddjus, ON veggr ‘wall') (Jasanoff 1978a, 85, 87).

=== page 30 ===
30
but are still believed to show by umlaut or inflection that intervocalic *j
was preserved into the dialectal period.
The following discussion will treat the different sequences of
Proto-Germanic intervocalic *j after stressed vowels. Chapter 2 will
deal with intervocalic *j after short vowels, which in practice means the
sequence PGmc. *-ájV-. Under that heading fall, e.g., Cowgill's alleged
cases of preservation Go. ajukdūps ‘eternity,' bajōþs ‘both,' and waja-
mēreins ‘blasphemy.' The next three chapters will focus on the verba
pura, verbs with j-presents containing the structures PGmc. *-éjV- and
PGmc. *-ój V-. Among them is Cowgill's example OS sāian, OHG sāen
'to sow' (with the *j of PGmc. *sējan supposedly preserved into the
dialects). Examples of the sequences PGmc. *-éjV- and PGmc. *-ójV-
in nominal forms will be given in Chapter 6. Chapter 7 will discuss the
structure PGmc. *-újV-, and, finally, intervocalic *j after u-diphthongs
(with ON deyja 'to die' as Cowgill's example of a preserved *j) will be
treated in Chapter 8.

CHAPTER 2
THE SEQUENCE PGMC. *-ájV-
2.1 Intervocalic *j after a short *a
A discussion of the Germanic development of single intervocalic
*j after short accented vowels other than *i must concentrate on
examples of the sequence PGmc. *-ájV-, as evidence of intervocalic *j
after other short vowels is lacking. On the other hand, PGmc. *-ájV-
occurs in several examples from different grammatical categories.
2.1.1 Examples of PGmc. *-aji-
1. The adverb Go. air ‘early' is well equipped with Germanic
cognates, e.g., ON ár, OE ær ‘id.,' OHG ēr- in the compound ēracher
‘awake early'; comparative and superlative forms occur as well. The
word has a widely accepted etymology, being understood as the locative
of an Indo-European r/n-stem. The noun is preserved in Av. ayarə,
gen. ayan 'day.' The locative occurs in Gk. άpi-σtov 'breakfast'
31

=== page 32 ===
32
(< *ajer-i, cf. Frisk 1960-70, 1:643).¹ The Germanic development
presupposes a loss of intervocalic *j (PIE *ajer-i (*h₂ejer-1) > PGmc.
*ajir(i)> *a'ir(i) > Go. air, ON ár, OE ær, OHG ēr-).² This example
has frequently been used to demonstrate the loss of (PGmc.) *j before
*i, e.g., by Trautmann 1906, 61; Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 1:96; Lühr
1982, 465 with n. 3.
2. Another classic example of j-loss is Go. aiz n., ON eir, OE ār,
OHG ēr 'bronze.' This word derives from an Indo-European s-stem
seen in Skt. áyas-, Av. ayō ‘metal, iron,' Lat. aes ‘bronze' (Feist 1909,
17; Pokorny 1959, 15). The Germanic noun continues the e-grade of
the suffix, as does Lat. aes (PIE *ajes- (*h₂ejes-) > PGmc. *ajiz-> (with
loss of *j) *aiz-).³

3. Comparable phonological development can be inferred from
the reconstruction of Go. dat. pl. haizam ‘torches' (nom. sg. *hais n.(?))
(< PGmc. *hajiz-, cf. Feist 1909, 125; Pokorny 1959, 519). However,
this reconstruction is not supported by cognates from other branches
indicating an Indo-European s-stem. Old High German cognates show
the effect of Verschärfung, viz., OHG hei ‘dry,' gihei n. ‘heat, dryness,'
3pl. pret. arheigētun ‘dried up' (< PGmc. *hajjV-). Pokorny's root PIE

laryngeal (PIE *kaih-, cf. Rasmussen 1990, 438); the lack of Ver-
schärfung in the Gothic word could be explained by a derivation from
PGmc. *haiz- (< PIE *kajh,-s-). If this is the origin of Go. haizam, this
word cannot count as an example of the loss of intervocalic *j.
4. An Indo-European s-stem has also been assumed to lie behind
ON leir n. ‘clay, mud' (< Pr.-N. *laira, as if from PGmc. *lajiz- < PIE
*lojes-, according to Pokorny 1959, 662). This derivation would add
this word to the list of examples of j-loss. On the other hand, there is
no exact cognate which can confirm this reconstruction, the closest
being ON leira f. ‘muddy beach' (< *laizōn-) and OPr. layso f. 'clay'
(< *laisā). The root is Pokorny's *lei- ‘slimy, wet and slippery ground,
etc.' of Gk. aλívw, Lat. linō ‘besmear,' OIr. lenaid 'follows.' As in the
case of Go. haizam, it is thinkable that ON leir goes back to a form
which did not contain an intervocalic *j at the time of the Proto-
Germanic j-loss (< PGmc. *laiz- < PIE *loi̟h¸-s-(?)).4
4 The presence of a laryngeal in this root is shown, e.g., by OIr. lenaid
(< *lin-ǝ-), cf. Rasmussen 1990, 438, who connected the Verschärfung
product OS leia f. ‘reef, rock in the sea' to this root as well.

=== page 34 ===
34
5. ON ár f. ‘oar,' OE är f. ‘id.' does not have much weight as an
example of intervocalic j-loss. Pokorny considered the word an er-
suffixed formation to the root PIE *ei-, *oi- 'pole,' cf. Skt. īsa 'pole,'
Gk. oinov ‘rudder' (Pokorny 1959, 298). The Germanic words and the
loanwords Finn. airō and Latv. aĩris ‘oar' indicate a preform PGmc.
*airō-; there is no reason to postulate a development from PIE *oi-er-
with a loss of *j.
6. OE blezen f., MLG bleine, and OSw. blena ‘blister' have been
interpreted as derived from PGmc. *blajinōn-, but Dan. blegn(e) ‘id.”
from PGmc. *blajjinōn-; the root is PIE *bhlei- 'to blow up, swell,' cf.
Gk. oλiá 'doorpost' (Falk/Torp 1910-11, 1:81; Pokorny 1959, 156).
This explanation, assuming identical Germanic preforms, except for the
effect of Verschärfung (perhaps the result of expressive gemination), is
not particularly convincing. Rasmussen's response to that problem was
the reconstruction of an o-grade ā-stem PIE *bhlói(h)-neh₂ 'swelling' or
'inflammation' (the type of Greek лópуn) for the forms without Ver-
schärfung, hence, PGmc. *blainōn- (Rasmussen 1990, 438). This
solution makes the word irrelevant for the discussion of j-loss.
As the sequence PGmc. *-áji- falls under Mahlow's rule of Proto-
Germanic loss of *j before and after *i, the j-loss illustrated by the
examples above is not a new suggestion. The sequence PGmc. *-ájV-,
in which the second vowel was not *i, has traditionally been considered
to have retained *j into the dialectal period; such examples must now be
investigated.

35
2.1.2 PGmc. *stajan ‘to stand' and *gajan (?) ‘to go’
Among Cowgill's examples of Proto-Germanic intervocalic *j
was OHG stān, stēn ‘to stand,' a descendant of the Indo-European root
*steh₂- ‘to stand.' He believed that the *j of PGmc. *stajan was pre-
served in the Proto-Germanic period, and he explained the attested
form stān as having undergone a West Germanic loss of *j and sub-
sequent contraction (*stajan> *sta'an> stän) (Cowgill 1973, 296).5
Cowgill derived the verb from PIE *sth₂-h₁-jé/ó-, a stative formation
with zero grade of the suffix PIE *-eh₁-; the byforms in stā- and stē-
developed phonologically in the present paradigm (*sth₂-h₁-jó- > PGmc.
*staja-> OHG stā-, *sth₂-h₁-jé-> PGmc. *stai-> OHG stē-).6
OHG stān, stēn is invariably discussed in connection with the verb
OHG gān, gēn 'to go.' Both verbs have an identical, apparently
athematic, present inflection with byforms with ā and ē (1sg. gām, gēm,
2sg. gās, gēs, 3sg. gāt, gēt, etc.). Levelings of an older paradigmatic
alternation (1sg. gām, 2sg. gēs, 3sg. gēt, etc.) evidently explain the
byforms (as already recognized by Bremer 1886, 41-44); the original
distribution of ā and ē is reflected in the Franconian dialect (cf. Franck
1909, 265; Braune 1987, 306-7). The verbs are attested in other West
Germanic languages as well, cf. OE 3ãn 'to go' (1sg. 3ā, 2sg. 3æst, 3sg.
5 Mahlow (1879, 138-39) had proposed a similar solution.
6 The same effect would, of course, be achieved by positing a regular
ie/o-present with zero grade, PIE *sth₂-ié/ó-; Cowgill found evidence
for a stative formation in the Celtic, Italic, and Slavic languages (e.g.,
Lat. stāre).

=== page 36 ===
36
3æð, etc., Brunner 1965, 359), OFr. stān, stēn, gān, OS stān, gān." East
Norse shows attestations of a genuine OSw., ODan. gā, but stā is
considered a borrowing from Low German (Torp 1919, 146, 705;
Magnússon 1989, 949). CrGo. geen is the only East Germanic
occurrence.
These presents have been explained by some scholars as originally
athematic formations. As the full grade PIE *steh₂- (cf. Dor. ï-otã-µi
‘place') would be expected to yield PGmc. *stō-, not the attested stā-,
the verb stān has been assumed to have borrowed its vocalism from the
verb gān, the latter verb having a phonological WGmc. *gā- < PGmc.
*gē- (cf. Gk. Kí-xn-μɩ 'reach,' Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 2:144-45). The
athematic inflection in Old High German does not demand this inter-
pretation, however, for it would be natural for a verbal stem ending in
a long vowel to join the inflection of the athematic OHG tōn 'to do.'
On the other hand, the zero-grade je/o-present PGmc. *stajan
(whether from PIE *sth₂-ié/ó- or Cowgill's stative *sth₂-h₁-jé/ó-) is a
much better reconstruction, as it accounts perfectly for the distribution
of the variants with ā and ē in Franconian, assuming the Proto-
Germanic loss of intervocalic *j (PGmc. *staja-> *sta'a-> OHG stā-,
PGmc. *staji-> *sta'i-> OHG ste-). In other words, in the absence of
North and East Germanic evidence for the verb *stajan, this example of
Cowgill's rule of West Germanic j-loss after *a can be considered an
instance of the Proto-Germanic j-loss.
7 Old Frisian and Old Saxon examples are taken from Bremer 1886,
42.

=== page 37 ===
37
Under the same explanation, the verb 'to go' derives from PGmc.
*gajan. For semantic reasons this verb can hardly be considered a
stative formation, and thus the preform would presumably be PIE
*ĝhh,-jé/ó-, the root being PIE *ĝheh,- 'to leave, go' (cf. Skt. jáhāti
'leaves,' Gk. (Hom.) кixáva ‘reach,' Pokorny 1959, 418-19). This verb
is attested in East Norse, which makes it clear that this loss of *j was not
limited to West Germanic (PGmc. *gajan> *ga'an > OHG, OS, OFr.
gān, OE 3ān, OSw., ODan. gā).
2.2 Potential examples of a retained PGmc. *j
In a few Gothic words the sequence -aj V- is spelled out on the
surface. These are Go. ajukdūþs f. ‘eternity,' bajōþs m. pl. ‘both,' and
compounds with the first element waja-, waja-mērei f. "bad reputation,'
waja-mēreins f. 'blasphemy.' OE twezen 'two' is an example of the
alleged preservation of *j in the sequence PGmc. *-áji-.
2.2.1 Go. ajukdūþs ‘eternity'
Go. ajukdūps f. '‘eternity' is a compound formed with the suffix
PGmc. *-dūþi- (< PIE *-tūti-) which made adjectival abstract nouns in
Germanic (cf. Go. managdūþs 'abundance,' mikildūþs ‘greatness,'
Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 3:162). The underlying adjective is unattested in
8 A j-present of this structure was assumed, e.g., by Torp 1919, 146.
CrGo. geen has been reconstructed as a j-present with a long vocalism,
*gē-jan (Seebold 1970, 216).

=== page 38 ===
38
Gothic, but Old English supplies a cognate adjective ēce, ce ‘eternal.'
The base word is sometimes reconstructed as PGmc. *ajuka-, a
derivative of the stem of Go. aiws m. ‘time' with a suffix PGmc. *-ka-
(Feist 1909, 17).9 Others have reconstructed *ajuki- (Kieckers 1928,
40). The preform *ajukija- ‘eternal,' which is reconstructed to account
for the Old English adjective, would not yield Go. ajuk-; this form
would be expected to become Go. *ajuki- in composition (as in Go.
arbi-numja ‘heir' < *arbija-numjan-, cf. Krause 1968, 92). Whatever
the exact shape of the stem formative, the reconstructed form PGmc.
*ajuka-/i-/ija- contains an intervocalic *j, and Go. ajukdūps has been
assumed to preserve the *j intact (cf. Kieckers 1928, 40; Cowgill 1973,
296, n. 51).
The connection of Go. ajukdūþs and Go. aiws m. ‘time' is without
doubt correct; further Germanic cognates are ON ævi f. ‘life,' OHG
ēwa f. 'time, eternity,' and the adverbs ON æ, OE ā, OHG io ‘always'
(Pokorny 1959, 17). The ultimate origin is an Indo-European neuter
u-stem PIE *h₂oiu- with an ablauting acrostatic paradigm (weak stem
*h₂ejų-), which is reflected in Ved. ayu-, Av. āyū-, Gk. aióv ‘life, age,'
Lat. aevum ‘age, eternity.'10 As for the mysterious k-"suffix" in the
9 This adjectival suffix does not have a firm basis. It is certainly not
well represented in Germanic; Go. ibuks ‘turned backwards' is the other
primary example (Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 3:212). The suffix has been
connected to that of Ved. árbhaga- 'young' as deriving from PIE *-go-
(Feist 1909, 17), but the significance of this Vedic example is limited, as
it is a variant of a better attested arbhaká-; in fact, there are additional
examples of Indic words with byforms in -ka- and -ga- (cf. Hoffmann
1975, 137).
10 For this interpretation of the Indo-European paradigm, cf. Cowgill

=== page 39 ===
39
Germanic adjective, it has been convincingly argued by Weiss (1993,
144-51) that the stem PGmc. *ajuki- can be equated with the adjectives
Lat. iūgis 'everflowing,' Gk. by 'sound, healthy,' and Av. yauuaējī-
‘having eternal life.' In his opinion, they all derive from a bahuvrīhi
compound PIE *hju-gih,- 'having a life which is eternal,' the second
element being a root noun of the root PIE *g"eih3-/g"ih₁- 'to live.'
PIE *hiu-g'ih,- cannot have yielded PGmc. *ajuki- entirely
phonologically. The first member of the proposed compound, PIE
*hi̟u-, should have become PGmc. *ju- with the loss of the initial
laryngeal, and one must assume that a full-grade form has taken its
place.11 Of course, the obvious explanation is early influence from the
noun PIE *h₂oju- (weak stem *h₂eju-). This noun did not preserve the
u-stem inflection into Germanic; the Gothic attestations show a- and i-
stem forms of aiws m. This is not surprising, for neuter u-stems were a
dying category in Germanic. Moreover, a u-stem of this structure, with
a root-final diphthong, must have been unusual; the paradigm nom.-acc.
sg. *(h₂)aju, weak stem *(h₂)aių-, would hardly have been left un-
touched. One method of regularizing the paradigm would have been a
generalization of the stem *(h2)aju resulting in a neuter u-stem para-
digm of the type Go. faihu 'means.'12 This leveling did not take place,
11 The development of PIE *-gih,- through *-gi- to PGmc. *-ki-
involves the loss of the labial element of the labiovelar by dissimilation
after the vowel *u. The short *i can either be explained by an early
phonological loss cf*, in this position or a morphological transfer to
the i-stem category; Germanic did not preserve i-stem adjectives.
(Weiss 1993, 151-53).
12 According to the standard belief in a preserved PGmc. *j, the
expected Gothic outcome would presumably have been a paradigm Go.

and the reason may well have been a wish to avoid the abnormal
structure *Viu with a semivowel serving as the root-final consonant.
On the other hand, the paradigm of the word *(h₂)aju was
regularized by an analogical process witnessed in some other old u-
stems as well; the weak stem *(h₂)aju- was generalized and u-stem
inflection, in all probability, at the same time kept intact. 13 A remade
u-stem *aiwu- may thus have been the earliest Proto-Germanic form
and the one appearing in the compound adjective as well, which at this
stage had the form PGmc. *aiwu-ki- ‘eternal.' The first sound change
to affect the adjective stem was the well-known Proto-Germanic loss of
*w before *u (cf. PGmc. *juwungaz > Go. juggs, ON ungr, OHG, OS,
OFr. jung, OE zeon3 ‘young,' Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 1:96).14 PGmc.
*aiwu-ki- would thus have become PGmc. *ai'u-ki- with subsequent
resyllabification; hence, PGmc. *aju-ki-. This stem survived in the
Gothic compound ajukdūps and the adjective OE ēce, ace.15 Under this
nom.-acc. sg. *aju, gen. sg. *ajaus, but the *j would naturally have
fallen prey to the proposed rule of intervocalic j-loss.

hánu- 'cheek,' Pokorny 1959, 381; Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 1:113). The
adjective ON þunnr, OE þynne ‘thin' has likewise generalized the stem

the example *stajan no longer supports his idea of a special West

expressed here.
16 This description of the history of *aju-ki- does not conflict with the

generalized and transferred to different inflections without affecting the

2.2.2 Go. bajōþs ‘both' and waja-mēreins 'blasphemy'
The formation of Go. bajōþs m. pl. ‘both' is not entirely clear.
The word is obviously related to Go. bai m. pl., ba n. pl. 'both,' which,
according to standard views, is a member of the Indo-European family
of Gk. ǎμow, Lat. ambō, Lith. abù, etc., ‘both' (Pokorny 1959, 34-35).
Among Germanic cognates are OE bā m., f., n., ON gen. beggja (with
Verschärfung < *bajjō), but mostly the uncompounded PGmc. *bai has
been replaced by compounds of *bai and the demonstrative pronoun
*pai (e.g., OHG beide, bēde). The preform PGmc. *bai is usually
explained either as an old neuter dual form in PIE *-oi or a reflex of
PIE *bhō(ų) m. (of Gk. äµow, etc.) with an analogical pronominal
plural ending (cf. *þai m. pl.) (Lloyd/Springer 1988, 513-14).17
The apparent suffix -ōþ- has been interpreted in more than one
way. It has been connected to Lith. -étas in dvejétas 'twosome' (Feist
1909, 42) or seen as containing the pronominal stem PGmc. *þa- (n. pl.
*bajō þō, Brugmann 1897-1916, 2,2:77). One view explains bajōþs as a
consonant stem comparable to Go. mēnōþs m. ‘month' formed with the
suffix PIE *-ōt- (Feist 1909, 42). Although an ancient suffix may
conceivably lie behind bajōps, it does not necessarily follow that the
word has been carried down from the protolanguage unaffected. There
is no comparative evidence for a direct Indo-European preform con-
17 On the interpretation of PGmc. *bai as derived from PIE *h,bhó-, a
bh-case form of the demonstrative pronoun PIE *h,é/ó-, cf. Jasanoff
1976, 130-31.

=== page 43 ===
43
taining an intervocalic PIE **, nor for a preform PGmc. *bajōþ-.
Whether or not the word has actually been created on Gothic ground
from the form bai, it is at least likely to have been influenced by that
form, which means that the intervocalic j seen in the Gothic spelling
bajōþs has arisen (or been restored?) after the Proto-Germanic j-loss
had taken place.18
A similar explanation applies to the words waja-mērei f. 'bad
reputation' and waja-mēreins f. ‘blasphemy.' The first member of the
compounds is the interjection Go. wai ‘woe!' (cf. ON vei, OHG, OS wē,
OE wā, Lat. vae, MIr. fãe, Latv. vaĩ ‘id.,' Pokorny 1959, 1110-11). By
looking at the Gothic evidence alone, these words can be interpreted as
modeled on compounds with waila- as the first element, i.e., waila-
mērei f. ‘good reputation' and waila-mēreins f. ‘announcement.' It can
be inferred that the j of waja- is a purely Gothic phenomenon, the result
of a word formation in which [wai] was placed before a vowel.
The Gothic compounds are claimed to have a North Germanic
cognate attested in the runic inscription of Thorsberg (ca. 200 A.D.).
The reading of niwajemarir as 'the not badly reputed (one)' interprets
wajemarir as an i-stem adjective PGmc. *waja-mēri- ‘of bad repute'
(Krause 1966, 52-55). This interpretation has the drawback, however,
that the unexpected vowel e of waje- (for *waja-) is not easily justified;
the suggestion that a was fronted by the preceding j is not confirmed by

the compounds wea-dæd 'offence' and wea-cwānian ‘to lament,' using
these examples in support of a West Germanic change of *-aj- to *-ej-.
Earlier, a development from a j-less reduplicated form had been pro-
posed, based on the comparison with the noun OE wāwa ‘woe,' OHG
wēwo‘pain.' Thus Luick (1921, 132) derived OE wāwa and wēa from
the same paradigm, assuming the development WGmc. nom. *waiwō
> wāwa, obl. *waiwun > wēan. Brunner (1965, 110) assumed a con-
traction WGmc. *wā+u> wēa.
It may thus appear that the collective evidence of forms from the

the Proto-Norse and Old English examples is quite uncertain. In fact,
only the Gothic forms are easily derived phonologically from a pre-

which the intervocalic *j could be analogically restored after the oper-

waja- is a case of a directly preserved Proto-Germanic intervocalic *j.
2.2.3 OE twezen ‘two’
OE twezen (byform twozen) m. pl. 'two' has generally been
believed to reflect PGmc. *twainai, derived from the distributive
numeral PIE *duoi-noi, cf. OHG zwēne, OS twēne, OFr. twēn(e), but to
have been influenced by OE bezen/bozen ‘both' (cf. Krahe/Meid 1967-
69, 2:88). This explanation does not involve a Proto-Germanic
sequence *-ájV- at all; the reconstruction of OE bezen/bozen as PGmc.
*bō-jenō will be discussed in section 6.2.1 below.21
On the other hand, Seebold (1968, 417-36) reconstructed OE
twezen as PGmc. *twajinaz, from which he also derived OHG zwēne
'two' and OE twenti3, OHG zweinzug 'twenty.'22 This preform was,

(bēzen/bazen, twēzen/twāzen), cf. Brunner 1965, 252-53.
22 Seebold's derivation of twezen is based on the claim that the early
attestations of twezen/twozen (and bezen/bozen) unequivocally indicate
a nominative preform with twe- (and likewise be-), which is to be
interpreted as an original short vocalism *twa-, *ba-. Other solutions
based on a long vocalism *twōjV-, *bõjV- will be discussed in section
6.2.1.

supposedly, a distributive numeral preserved in this West Germanic
form only, apart from one parallel, a Church Slavonic cognate důvojĭně

etymology is not exactly well founded, and Seebold's derivation of the
West Germanic forms is partly based on phonological changes which
are not well established. The forms required the postulation of an early
West Germanic change of *-aj- to *-ej- and a loss of intervocalic *j
after short vowels limited to Old Saxon and Old High German (*twajin-
> *twejin-> *twein-> OE twenti3, OHG zweinzug). 23 On the other
hand, *j was not lost in the derivation *twajinaz> *twajenaz> *twejen-
> OE twezen.
Needless to say, Seebold's account of twezen violates both
Mahlow's rule of Proto-Germanic j-loss before *i and the more general
loss of intervocalic PGmc. *j under discussion here. If it is actually the
case that Seebold's preform PGmc. *twajinaz was at work here, it is
conceivable that the effect of the intervocalic j-loss has been made
obscure by analogical influence from other forms of the numeral 'two.'
PGmc. *twajinaz must have replaced the form PGmc. *twai m. pl. (Go.
23 It is unfortunate that Seebold's examples in support of the change of
*-aj- to *-ej- are PGmc. *waja- 'woe' and *ajuki- ‘eternal,' which, as
was made clear in sections 2.2.1 and 2.2.2, do not provide good
evidence for the treatment of PGmc. *-aj V-. Seebold based the account
of *twajin- in the Old English word for 'twenty' on the development
*ajuki-> *ejyki-> *ejice> *eice > OE ēce ‘eternal' and defined the j-
loss as being conditioned by the following *i. This example can also be
explained by a different chain of events, cf. Cowgill's rule of early j-
loss (*ajuki-> *auki > ēce, section 2.2.1). Seebold separated the Old
English j-loss from the one in Old Saxon and Old High German, but
was unable to support his rule of j-loss in Old Saxon and Old High
German by unequivocal parallels.

=== page 47 ===
47
twai); its genitive form was *twajjō". The j-loss in PGmc. *twajinaz
would have led to a hiatus form PGmc. *twa'inaz which did not quite
match the other forms of the paradigm. It is thus thinkable that an
analogical *twaj- from *twajjōn created a new form *twajinaz which
could enter West Germanic and undergo the development described by
Seebold. This form is therefore not exactly a counterexample to the
rule of j-loss. In any case, the morphological foundation of the recon-
struction PGmc. *twajinaz is doubtful and the West Germanic develop-
ment of *twajinaz unproven; hence, Seebold's solution must be treated
with caution.
2.3 Conclusion
It emerges that sequences of PGmc. *-ájV- do not provide
counterevidence to the assumption that intervocalic *j was lost in Proto-
Germanic in that environment. Examples of PGmc. *-áji- support the
loss of *j (PIE *h₁eier-i > PGmc. *ajir(i)> *a'ir(i) > Go. air, ON ár,
OE ær, OHG ēr- ‘early'), and so does evidence for the sequence PGmc.
*-ája- (PIE *sth₂-ió- > PGmc. *staja- > OHG stā- ‘to stand'). In the
Gothic examples spelled with an intervocalic j (Go. ajukdūþs ‘eternity,'
bajōps ‘both,' and compounds with the first element waja-), the j can be
shown to have arisen after the Proto-Germanic rule of j-loss had run its
course.

=== page 48 ===
CHAPTER 3
PGMC. *-éjV- AND *-ốjV-
THE VERBA PURA
3.1 The verba pura
The majority of examples of the sequences PGmc. *-éjV- and
*-ójV- is provided by present tense forms of the so-called verba pura,
verbs made from monosyllabic roots which ended in long vowels at the
Proto-Germanic stage (e.g., PGmc. *sē- < PIE *seh₁- 'to sow,' PGmc.
*mō- < PIE *meh3- 'to torture'). It is generally accepted that this
group of verbs has preserved actual traces of genuine Indo-European
reduplicated o-grade perfects in preterite forms such as Go. 1sg. saisō,
ON sera (< PGmc. *se-zō < PIE *se-sóh₁-h₂e) 'sowed.' On the other
hand, there is no absolute consensus on the development of the present
tense formation, cf. the infinitives of 'to sow,' Go. saian, ON sá, OE
1 The term verba pura will be used here only of verbs whose roots
ended in PGmc. *ē or *ō (as done by, e.g., Lindeman 1968; Matzel
1987a, 146-47, n. 3. Matzel gave the reason that verb stems in *ū
actually belong to roots ending in sonorants, not long vowels). Strictly
speaking, roots ending in PGmc. *ū meet the description as well and are
sometimes included (cf. Flasdieck 1936, 295; Meid 1971, 71, 85), but
verba pura will be defined here as being of the root structure PGmc.
*Cē- or *Cō-, and verba impura as being PGmc. *CēC- or *CōC-.
48

=== page 49 ===
49
sawan, ModEFr. ziāhn, OS sāian, and the OHG variants sawen, säien,
sāhen, säen, and sāan.2
The present stem of the Germanic verba pura is most likely to
contain the Indo-European present suffix *-je/o- (thus, PGmc. inf.
*sējan 'to sow' < PIE *seh₁-je/o-). The comparative evidence shows
that Indo-European roots of the structure PIE *Ceh,- commonly appear
with this present suffix.³ As Jasanoff (1979, 88-89) has demonstrated,
this present formation originated in Proto-Indo-European ablauting
i-presents which were later thematized (e.g., sg. *dheh,-i-: pl. *dhh,-i-,
with full grade *dheh,-je/o- in OHG täen 'to suckle,' Arm. diem ‘suck,'
Latv. dêju ‘id.,' but zero grade *dhh,-je/o- in ON dĩa ‘to suck, suckle').4
The origin of the Germanic verba pura in je/o-presents is firmly
supported by the fact that several verbs of this group have cognates with
ie/o-presents in other branches of the family, and, not surprisingly,
Balto-Slavic cognates are particularly striking. Among examples of
such correspondences are the following (cf. Matzel 1987a, 184):
2 The verb 'to sow' serves here as a convenient example, as it is attested
in all the Germanic daughter languages.
3 Among the supporters of this view are Burghauser (1887, 45), Kluge
(1910, 106), Lindeman (1968, 48), Meid (1971, 82), Matzel (1987b,
216-17). Streitberg (1896, 76) mentioned the loanword Lapp. sājet as a
potential argument for assuming that the Germanic verba pura had had
ie/o-presents. This j is, however, likely to be a Lappish phenomenon.
De Vries (1961, 459, 512) considered Lapp. sajjit to be borrowed from
the verbum purum 'to sow' (ON sa) and Lapp. (s)lajjit ‘to mow' from
ON slá ‘to strike' < PGmc. *slahan, which certainly never had a i̟e/o-
present. Cf. also Lindeman 1968, 69-70.
4 Of course, the reconstruction of the verba pura as full-grade i̟e/o-
presents (PGmc. inf. *sējan 'to sow' < PIE *seh,-ie/o-) does not in any
way depend on Jasanoff's views of the origin of the formation.

50
PGmc. *blējan 'to bleat,' OCS blějo̟, blějati, Latv. blêju, blêt ‘id.”
PGmc. *dējan 'to suck,' Latv. dêju, dêt, Arm. die- ‘id.'
PGmc. *mōjan 'to torture,' Russ. máju, májati ‘to fatigue'
PGmc. *sējan 'to sow,' OCS sějo, sějati, Lith. séju, séti ‘id.”
PGmc. *wējan 'to blow,' OCS vějo̟, vějati ‘id.'
It is, of course, not the case that every Germanic verb of this type
goes back to a je/o-present of Proto-Indo-European date. Other present
formations were available to roots of the structure PIE *Ceh,-, cf. the
athematic present Skt. 3sg. váti 'blows,' Gk. äŋσɩ ‘id.' (< PIE *hu̟eh,-ti)
and the reduplicated present Lat. 1sg. serō ‘plant, sow' (< *sisō < PIE
*si-sh₁-oh₂), which are cognates of the verba pura Go. waian 'to blow'
and saian 'to sow,' respectively. Proto-Germanic must be assumed to
have generalized j-presents, a productive formation in Germanic, as the
normal present stems of vowel-final roots. Some scholars have appar-
ently understood the generalization to have been absolute and have
projected every Germanic verbum purum directly back to a present in
*-ie/o-.5 It is likely, however, that some of the verbs which pattern as
verba pura in the attested languages did not originate as j-presents of
roots of the shape PIE *Ceh,- but joined the group from other sources.
One candidate is the verb OE flōwan ‘to flow,' often simply derived
5 Lindeman expressed this opinion: "Man nimmt im allgemeinen an,
dass eine ursprünglich nur für einige wenige Verba charakteristische,
altererbte j-Präsensbildung im Germanischen frühzeitig produktiv
wurde und später auf alle anderen Verba desselben Typus analogisch
übertragen worden ist" (1968, 48) and reconstructed infinitives in
*-ējan and *-ōjan only (op. cit., 67-71). Matzel (1987a, 151-68)
followed his example.

=== page 51 ===
51
from PGmc. *flōwan, where the *w is believed to have been a part of
the root PIE *pley- (cf. Seebold 1970, 205; Meid 1971, 88). In such
cases it is doubtful that a j-present (e.g., PGmc. inf. *flōjan recon-
structed by Lindeman 1968, 68; Matzel 1987a, 157) ever existed.
The Proto-Germanic present tense paradigm of the verba pura
has also been reconstructed with hiatus forms containing the root
PGmc. *sē- and the regular thematic endings (PGmc. 1sg. pres. ind.
*sē-ō, 2sg. *sē-is/z(i), etc., cf. Bremer 1886, 73). This reconstruction
has nothing to recommend it. A protoform PIE *seh,-e/o- does not fit
any known Indo-European present type, as there is no evidence for
Indo-European thematic presents made to roots of this structure.6 This
view makes it therefore necessary to assume that the stem *sē- was
secondarily thematized in Germanic. Athematic root presents might
appear to be the basis; that type is found among the roots of the verba
pura, cf. Skt. 3sg. váti, Gk. äŋor (< PIE *hueh,-ti) beside Go. waian
'to blow' (Streitberg 1896, 319).7 Root aorist subjunctives of the
structure PIE *Ceh,-e/o- are also a conceivable source (cf. Ved. 3sg.
dhất < PIE *dheh,-e-t from the root PIE *dheh,- 'to put'). The creation
6 The nonexistence of such a formation in the protolanguage was
pointed out by Kluge (1910, 106), who, moreover, expected an early
contraction to have taken place in hiatus forms, had they existed: "Man
darf Zweifel darüber hegen, ob Hiatusformen wie sêô sêesi sêeti sich
überhaupt als indogerm. erweisen lassen; denn Kontraktionen wären für
solche Fälle doch sehr früh unvermeidlich gewesen, und aus keiner
indogerm. Sprache hat man noch den Beweis erbracht, dass eine solche
Singularflexion für die gemeinsame Grundsprache und somit auch für
das Germanische vorauszusetzen wäre." Matzel (1987b, 217) agreed.
7 In the same work (1896, 76), Streitberg actually allowed for two
possibilities, namely, that the stems of old root presents had either been
directly thematized or turned into je/o-presents.

=== page 52 ===
52
within Germanic of a present tense paradigm 1sg. PGmc. *sē'ō, 2sg.
*sē'is/z(i), etc., would, however, have led to a new type of present with
a hiatus in every indicative and optative form, which speaks against this
explanation. A generalization of the productive j-present formation,
which, moreover, has the support of comparative evidence, is infinitely
more plausible.

the latter interpretation, a true verbum purum PGmc. *flōjan could be
reconstructed as PIE *pleh,-je/o- (cf. PGmc. *flōjan reconstructed by

3.2 Loss of PGmc. *j and the East
and North Germanic evidence
Having thus seen that the verba pura, at least their large majority,
derive from Proto-Germanic j-presents containing the sequences PGmc.
*-éjV- and *-ójV-, we must now examine their present tense paradigm
and its outcome after the operation of the rule of loss of intervocalic *j.
The verbs 'to sow' and 'to row' will serve as examples.

In the present optative paradigm the *j was followed by the
optative marker PGmc. *-ai- (< PIE *-oih,-); hence, every form of that
paradigm must also have undergone the loss of intervocalic *j, as did
the imperative forms. In fact, every form based on the present stem
*sēj- or *rōj- had a vowel-initial suffix/ending and thus an intervocalic
*j. There was, of course, no chance of j-loss in the reduplicated
preterite forms (PGmc. *se-zō < PIE *se-sóh₁-h₂e). On the other hand,
the j-suffix, which originally was restricted to the present stem, is likely
to have spread to the preterite participle (PGmc. *sējana-, *rōjana-).
The reduplicating verbs had the same vocalism in the present and the
preterite participle, cf. PGmc. inf. *lētan 'to let,' pret. ptc. *lētana-.14
14 Kuryłowicz derived Go. saians from *sējanaz (1956, 217). Forms
without *j, PGmc. *sē-ana-, *rō-ana-, were reconstructed by Bremer
(1886, 73), who actually did not assume that the verba pura had j-
presents. Lindeman (1968, 50, n. 6), who favored the j-present origin,
saw no reason to assume that the *j had appeared outside of the present.

3.2.1 Hiatus forms in Gothic

Attestations of the Gothic verbum purum saian ‘to sow' indicate
the following present indicative paradigm:
1sg. *saia

1pl. *saiam
2sg. *saiis
2du. *saiats
2pl. *saiip
3sg. saiip
3pl. saiand
The spelling of the Gothic forms, inf. saian, etc., with ai before
vowels, is controversial. In this environment, ai has often been inter-
preted as denoting a real diphthong, although the spelling *sajan would
strictly have been expected (cf. the j spelled before a vowel in ajukdūps
‘eternity'). The development of this diphthong has been described in
different ways.15 Those who support the j-present origin have had to
explain how PGmc. *sējan could yield a diphthong [ai]. It has been
suggested that after a Gothic loss of *j after *ē the vowel *ē was diph-
thongized before vowels (Jellinek 1926, 63), or, that the syllable
boundary was moved and the tautosyllabic *ēj became Go. ai before
vowels (Helm in Braune 1928, 17).16
15 The spelling of ai before vowels appears in noninitial syllables as
well, cf. Go. armaio ‘charity' < PGmc. *armējōn-, where it poses the
same problem.
16 It was actually suggested by Mahlow (1879, 19-20) and supported by
Kögel (1884a, 509-16) that the change of *ēj to ai before vowels was
Proto-Germanic. The vowel a of the forms OHG sāen, OS sāian then
needed a special explanation, and the long vowel was said to have been

=== page 64 ===
64
The other reading of ai before vowels is that of a monophthong.
In related words e is spelled before consonants (e.g., Go. manaseps
'world'), which invites the interpretation that ai denoted a monoph-
thong, an allophone of long e before vowels. Braune (1882, 11) and
Bremer (1886, 74) thus claimed that ai represented a long, open e-
sound, explained by a Gothic rule of lowering of ē before vowels.17
For the scholars who assumed the j-less origin of saian, PGmc. *sē-an,
this was the only change needed to account for Go. saian [sɛ:an].
Those who assume the pronunciation [sɛ:an], but hold that the
verba pura had Proto-Germanic j-presents, must, on the other hand,
explain the absence of the semivowel in the Gothic forms. This has
given rise to a rule of intervocalic j-loss after *ē limited to Gothic,
which has a parallel in the loss of intervocalic *w after *ō; hence,
PGmc. *sējan > Go. saian, cf. PGmc. *sōwila-> Go. sauil ‘sun'
(Streitberg 1896, 76; Kluge 1910, 108).18
analogically extended from the to-participle PGmc. *sēða-. This
hypothesis was sharply criticized by Paul (1880, 157-60), who empha-
sized that neither the West Germanic forms nor ON sá could have
derived phonologically from *saian. Those who accept the change of
PGmc. *ēj to *ai, for which Meyer (1869, 388, 697) is credited,
generally limit it to Gothic. PGmc. *ēj gave NWGmc. *āj according to
the standard view.

vowels, but Bremer formulated a sound law lowering ē (or preventing
the raising of ē) before open vowels only. The reason for Bremer's
condition was phonetic plausibility; [&:] was subsequently generalized to
the position before closed vowels as well. Moulton (1948, 85) provided
a structuralist interpretation of Braune's view, i.e., called [e:] and [ɛ:]
allophones in complementary distribution, [&:] appearing before any
vowel.

It is easy to understand why the hiatus created in Proto-Germanic
could have been maintained in the attested stages of the daughter
languages. The hiatus created by the loss of *j in the verba pura (1sg.
pres. ind. *sē'ō, 2sg. *sē'is/z(i), etc.) was a boundary between the long,
accented root vowel and clearly identifiable endings, and under those
circumstances hiatus is exactly expected to be maintained. One can
compare Cowgill's derivation *éaje> *éa'e> *éō, where two un-
accented vowels contracted but the accented initial *é did not contract
with the following vowel (cf. Cowgill's account of OE code in section
1.3.1); the hiatus was preserved even after the function of *é- as a re-
duplicating syllable had long ceased to be recognizable.20

*sājan--is not *sæja with palatal umlaut. The same applies to the plural

One possible explanation of this lack of umlaut would be a special
phonological rule of j-loss in North Germanic, but the idea is not
inviting that *j was preserved into Proto-Norse and yet was lost too
early to leave any sign of umlaut.
Lindeman (1968, 49-51), who wished to derive all verba pura
from j-presents, sought an analogical explanation of this situation. For
the verb sá he invoked the influence of strong verbs like slá ‘to strike'
(< PGmc. *slahan, Cl. VI) at the Proto-Norse stage when their 2,3sg.
and 2pl. forms had the same structure (e.g., 2sg. *slā-ir: *sā-ir). By
analogy, the verb 'to sow' supposedly adopted j-less, strong present
plural forms and infinitive. The verbs gróa and róa similarly lost all
signs of their j-presents by analogy to strong verbs like snúa ‘to turn,
twist' (in Lindeman's opinion from Pr.-N. *snōwan).
The claim that strong verbs of Class VI influenced sá is under-
mined by the fact that the preterite of sá, the highly abnormal redupli-
cated preterite 1sg. sera, etc., was not affected. It ought to have been
more pressing to replace those forms by more regular strong forms
than to get rid of the j-present *sæja. Moreover, the existence of
the j-present 1sg. hlæ, 2,3sg. hlær of hlæja 'to laugh' (< PGmc.
*hlahjan).

=== page 69 ===
69
umlauted j-presents in Class VI (deyja ‘to die,' geyja ‘to bark,' hlæja ‘to
laugh') is a strong objection to Lindeman's explanation. It is not easy to
understand why slá ousted the j-present *sæja, with which it only shared
the structure of the forms of the 2,3sg. and 2pl. present, when it left the
Class VI j-present hlæja intact; Lindeman's analogy would have made
the inflection of slá and hlæja identical. Lindeman's account of gróa
and róa does not have a firm basis, as the reconstruction Pr.-N.
*snōwan for ON snúa is uncertain (cf. section 7.1.3).24
These complications are sidestepped by the assumption of the
proposed rule of Proto-Germanic j-loss, which can derive the Old
Norse paradigm of the verba pura from the Germanic j-present. Hiatus
forms were created in Proto-Germanic, and no phonological or ana-
logical loss of intervocalic *j is needed for Proto-Norse. The hiatus
was preserved until Norse contraction rules took effect.

and fló(a)ði, respectively, and from very early on the verb sá also had

the weak preterite sáði, which ultimately ousted the reduplicated pret-

also had the weak preterite knáði (cf. Cleasby/Vigfusson 1969, 345;
