ment of the weak forms has any consequences for the question of the
treatment of intervocalic *j.
The weak preterite of the Old Norse verba pura has been used to
support the view that the Old High German dental preterite (Cl. I 3sg.
pret. ind. sāta) was originally formed without the connecting vowel *-i-
(Matzel 1987a, 182-83; 1987b, 214-16, cf. section 4.1.3 below). Matzel
followed those earlier scholars who had regarded the Indo-European to-
participle as the source of the dental preterites, the preterite participle
type OHG gisāt and ON sáðr deriving directly from a Proto-Germanic
participle *sēða- (Matzel 1987a, 188-98).28 To create the preterite
Matzel used the analogy of other dental preterites made to participles
without the connecting vowel *-i-, viz., the preterito-presents and the
type *wurkjan of Class I, which provided the following model:
inf. *skulan
: ptc. *skulða-
inf. *wurkjan : ptc. *wurhta-
inf. *sējan

: 3sg. pret. X; X = *sēdē
27 Cf. Noreen 1970, 340, 342.
28 Cf. Mahlow (1879, 20), Kögel (1884a, 512-13), Collitz (1912, 88-

=== page 72 ===
72
An obvious objection to this scheme was already raised by Paul
(1880, 158-59, criticizing Mahlow 1879, 20), namely, the lack of
evidence for to-participles to the verba pura in Germanic; Gothic, Old
Norse, and Old English show no-participles (Go. saians, ON sáinn, OE
sawen), and Old High German has preserved such forms as well (e.g.,
zaplahanner). It seems indeed safe to assume that the verba pura did not
have living to-participles (in participle function) at the time when their
weak preterites were created. When Matzel claimed that to-participles
could be demonstrated for over half of the verba pura (1987a, 194), he
counted in adjectives such as *mōða- (ON móðr 'tired' to *mōjan 'to
wear out'), and nouns which were such adjectives substantivized, e.g.,
*blōða- n. (ON blóð, OHG bluot, OE, OS, OFr. blōd ‘blood' to *blōjan
'to bloom'). Many of these words are unlikely to have been directly
associated with the cognate verbs--let alone functioning as their parti-
ciples--at the time when a part of the West Germanic branch created a
dental preterite.29 The creation of the dental preterite of the verba pura
based on such "participles" ("Verbaladjektive") does not seem directly
parallel to the derivation of the dental preterites of the preterito-
presents or the type *wurkjan (ptc. *skulða-, *wurhta- giving rise to
3sg. pret. *skulỗē, *wurhtē), where the Germanic languages agree on
having to-participles only. It is much less likely that participles of the
type *sēða- could have given rise to weak preterite forms.
According to Matzel's hypothesis, ON 3sg. pret. sáði was the
direct outcome of PGmc. *sēdē, which was built on the preterite

conjugation of Class I and thus do not provide any evidence for a Proto-

not have any overt signs of a preterite with the connecting vowel *-i-.

The two verba pura with ō-vocalism and weak forms only, viz.,

glóaði, 3sg. pres. ind. flóar and glóar) and Class III (pret. flóði and
glóði, pres. not *flóir, but glóir, cf. Noreen 1970, 342). As no strong
forms are attested of these verbs, some scholars have separated them
from the reduplicated verba pura and reconstructed weak present
forms. The reconstructions PGmc. inf. *flōwēn (De Vries 1961, 132)
and *flowōn (Gutenbrunner 1951, 144) are presumably based on the
assumption that the root PIE *pley- showed actual reflexes of the root-
final *ų in Germanic (cf. OE flōwan).36 On the other hand, Lindeman
(1968, 68-70), operated with the root form PGmc. *flō- (< PIE *plō-
without *ụ, cf. Gk. nhóɛiv, ënλwv) and assumed for the West Germanic
languages the verba pura PGmc. *flōjan (OE flōwan, MHG vlüejen) and
*glōjan (OE 3lōwan, OHG gluoen, OS glōian). Such j-presents would,
in his opinion, have preserved the intervocalic *j and resulted in ON
*fløja and *gløja, 37 and he instead derived flóa and glóa from PGmc.
*flō-ēn and *glō-ēn, respectively.
The rule of intervocalic j-loss in Proto-Germanic makes it un-
necessary to separate the Old Norse verbs from their West Germanic
cognates, for PGmc. *flōjan and *glōjan would have led, via late PGmc.
*flō'an and *glō'an, to ON flóa and glóa. These verbs could have
acquired a weak inflection in the way described above for the verb sá,
namely, by the analogical creation of weak present forms based on the
36 De Vries reconstructed ON glóa as PGmc. *glōwan, as if it were a
strong verb with etymological *ụ, cf. OE glōwan (1961, 175). In
principle, there is no reason to treat ON glóa and flóa in different ways.
37 Cf. the discussion of Lindeman's views in section 3.2.2 above.

=== page 76 ===
76
preterites flóði and glóði, which had been formed by adding the dental
suffix directly to the roots. 38 The absence of present forms with
i-umlaut (3sg. pres. ind. *flør and *glør) and reduplicated preterites
(analogically remodeled 1sg. pret. ind. *flera and *glera) would in this
case merely be a historical coincidence.
This solution, however, ignores the possibility--very strong in the
case of flóa--that the roots in question contained an etymological PGmc.
*w.39 If this was the case, then flóa and glóa might derive from strong
verbs of the structure PGmc. *flōwan and *glōwan, which in Proto-
Norse lost an intervocalic *w after the vowel *ō (cf. Noreen 1970, 170,
who assumed w-loss in róa as well). The creation of the dental pret-
erites flóði and glóði to replace inconvenient strong forms (Cl. VII 3sg.
pret. ind. ON *flé and *glé, hiatus forms in pl. *fléum, -uð, -u) is
immediately understandable; 40 the weak forms of Classes II and III
could have arisen as described above. It is therefore unnecessary to
assume Proto-Germanic weak verbs of the type *flōwēn or *flōwōn on
the basis of flóa and glóa, as their weak forms might be a Nordic
innovation.
38 Hiatus verbs in Class III, such as trúa 'to believe, trust' (< PGmc.
*trū-ēn), could provide the model: pret. trúði: pres. trúir, flóði : X;
X = flóir. The preterite participle and the infinitive formed a bridge
between Class II and Class III, for pret. ptc. flóaðr and inf. flóa
matched both patterns and thus could have triggered analogical forms of
Class II (cf. Lindeman 1968, 69); hence, pret. flóaði, pres. flóar.
39 Cf. section 3.1.1 above.
40 The hypothetical preterites ON *flé and *glé are based on the
assumption that flóa and glóa would have had a Class VII pattern of
the type blóta - blét 'to sacrifice.'

=== page 77 ===
77
The history of the weak inflection of the Old Norse verba pura is
therefore not without ambiguities, but the verbs are most likely to have
created a dental preterite by adding the dental suffix to the vowel-final
roots directly. The verbs show no signs of potentially original or
secondarily created j-presents, nor any interference from the weak
Class I; hence, they strongly support the view that the verba pura lost
intervocalic *j already in Proto-Germanic.
3.2.4 Excursus: ON sóa‘to sacrifice'
The Old Norse verb sóa 'to sacrifice' has not been consistently
included in the group of verba pura, although its structure would, from
a synchronic point of view, have invited that assumption.41 The verb is
classified as belonging to the strong Class VII, based on poetical forms
of the infinitive sóa and the preterite participle sóinn (cf. Noreen 1970,
340). In fact, Noreen listed the verb with its synonym blóta, clearly
assuming that the unattested preterite forms were 3sg. *sé, 3pl. *séu,
but a reduplicating verb of the type róa, 3sg. pret. reri, could equally
well have been expected. No strong preterite forms are attested; the
verb underwent an early transfer to the weak Class II (3sg. pret. sóaði).
The verb sóa does not have exact cognates in the other Germanic
languages, and its etymological connections are debated. Some scholars
have compared this verb and the noun ON Són f. (a poetic word for
'blood,' also the name of one of the containers for the scaldic mead) to
41 The verb is, e.g., not listed in Lindeman's (1968, 67-71) and
Matzel's (1987a, 153-68) compilations of the Germanic verba pura.

=== page 78 ===
78
the noun OHG suona f., MLG swōne f. ‘reconciliation.' Falk and Torp
(1909, 556) thus claimed that the verb, PGmc. *swōan, had the original
meaning ‘to quiet, reconcile,' whereas Loewenthal (1923, 284) con-
nected PGmc. *swōan to PIE *su- 'good' assuming the semantic devel-
opment **to set right, *finish, kill.' The reconstruction PGmc. *swōan
meets the description of a verbum purum as a verb from a root ending
in a long vowel. On the other hand, neither account of the semantic
development is entirely convincing, nor is the formation of the verb
explained, the only Germanic word of those mentioned that does not
have an n-suffix.42
A different reconstruction, PGmc. *swōhan, is based on the
comparison with OE āswōzan 'to choke,' zeswōzen 'swooned, dead' and
assumes that the cognate ON sóa specifically came to be used of sacri-
ficial killings.43 This reconstruction makes the root consonant-final and
the verb therefore a verbum impurum. The semantic development
assumed here is not ideal either, and does not adequately account for the
meaning ‘blood' of the noun ON Són. This comparison has the addi-
tional weakness that the etymology of OE āswōzan is not well known.44
42 The original meaning 'reconciliation drink' of ON Són was rejected
by Hilmarsson (1988-89, 34), who observed that nothing in the story of
the scaldic mead, nor in the poetic use of the word Són, suggested that
sense.
43 According to De Vries (1961, 528), ON sóa denotes "die beschwich-
tigung des opfertieres."
44 Falk and Torp (1909, 556) hesitantly suggested that OE āswōzan
derived from an extended form of the root of PGmc. *swōan ("germ.
svôg, svog" = ig. svôq?" connected to Gk. ownów, oranάw 'to be quiet,
be silent'). Holthausen (1934, 339) and Seebold (1970, 497) stated that
the origin is unknown.

=== page 79 ===
79
A new etymology of the noun Són was proposed by Jörundur
Hilmarsson (1988-89, 33-41). Taking the meaning 'blood' at face
value, Hilmarsson suggested a derivation from the amphikinetic collec-
tive neuter PIE *h‚ésh₂ōr, gen. sg. *h,sh₂n-és ‘blood' (cf. the latter stem
in Hitt. iš(h)an-, Lat. sanguis). The development of Són was shown to
be to a large extent parallel to that of the noun Go. fōn 'fire' from PIE
*péh₂u̟ōr ‘id.' The zero grade of the root was generalized (*h,sh₂ōr) and
later the suffix form *-n-; hence, (PGmc.) nom.-acc. sg. *son, which
was transferred to the class of feminine consonant-stems.45
Hilmarsson's derivation of Són as the immediate replacement of
*sōr, the root vowel *ō being from the suffix PIE *-ōr, cannot easily
account for the verb sóa.46 The noun PIE *h₁ésh₂ōr is not derived from
a known Indo-European verbal root which sóa might represent. If the
two are related at all, it seems obvious that sóa would have to be a Ger-
manic creation. Extracting PGmc. *sō- from the noun and creating a
verbum purum PGmc. *sōjan ‘to shed blood'(?) would be an un-
paralleled formation; there are no denominative verbs among the verba
pura. It is thus hardly possible to connect the verb ON sóa 'to sacrifice'
to the noun Són 'blood' under Hilmarsson's etymology.
Under the reconstruction PGmc. *swōan (or, perhaps rather a
j-present *swōjan) the verb ON sóa does not have verbal cognates in
Germanic, and the connection with OHG suona 'reconciliation' is no
longer supported by the noun Són. Although the comparison with OE
āswōzan 'to choke' does not give sóa an established Indo-European
45 On the other hand, the Gothic noun fōn was kept as a neuter n-stem
with the highly irregular paradigm nom.-acc. sg. fōn, gen. sg. funins.
46 Hilmarsson did not discuss the verb sóa in his article on Són.

=== page 80 ===
80
etymology, at least it provides a Germanic cognate verb of a similar
meaning. There is no evidence that allows ON sóa to be included in the
group of verba pura.
3.3 Conclusion
It was argued in this chapter that the verba pura, verbs in root-
final PGmc. *ē and *ō, had j-presents at the Proto-Germanic stage, and
that every present stem form and the preterite participle underwent the
loss of intervocalic *j in Proto-Germanic. The above discussion has
made it clear that these forms of the verba pura in Gothic and Old
Norse can be derived from forms with the hiatus that was the result of
the loss of Proto-Germanic intervocalic *j. The presents in Gothic and
Old Norse developed phonologically from the hiatus forms; the hiatus is
preserved in Gothic, but a late contraction has occurred in some Norse
forms of the present stem.
inf. *sējan > *se'an
inf. *sējan > *sē'an > *sā'an
> Go. saian
> ON sá
[se:an]
[sa:]
Some of the Old Norse verba pura were transferred from the
class of reduplicating verbs to the weak conjugation (e.g., ON inf. sá,
3sg. pres. sá(i)r, 3sg. pret. sáði), but the weak forms show no signs of a
j-present inflection nor any interference from the weak Class I which
might have suggested the preservation of PGmc. *j.

=== page 81 ===
81
We must now turn to West Germanic. Compared to East and
North Germanic, the West Germanic languages not only have the
largest number of verba pura, but also present a greater variety of
forms than the other branches. Phonological and morphological
changes in the West Germanic dialects have produced a quite com-
plicated picture which will be clarified in the next two chapters. The
evidence of the dialects which show strong indications of a j-present of
the verba pura, viz., Old High German (inf. säen 'to sow'), Old Saxon
(sāian), along with Middle Low German and Middle Dutch, will be
discussed in Chapter 4. Old English and Old Frisian, which seem to
have undergone a separate development resulting in present stem forms
with *w (OE sāwan, pre-OFr. *sēwa(n)), will be treated in Chapter 5.

=== page 82 ===
CHAPTER 4
THE VERBA PURA IN OLD HIGH GERMAN
AND ITS MOST CLOSELY RELATED DIALECTS
4.1 The Old High German evidence
The Old High German verba pura present an unusual wealth of
forms with a complicated history. The clarification of the Old High
German facts is very important for our understanding of the develop-
ment of intervocalic PGmc. *j; this makes it necessary to discuss in
detail the Old High German evidence and various hypotheses which
have been proposed to account for it.
The Proto-Germanic inflection of the verba pura was not pre-
served in West Germanic to the extent witnessed in the other Germanic
branches. The truly reduplicated preterite attested in Gothic and Old
Norse (PGmc. 1sg. *sezō > Go. saisō, ON sera) was replaced by--or
developed into--a strong preterite of Class VII (cf. OE 3sg. sēow, pl.
seowun, OS 3sg. -seu, MDu. sieu). The evidence indicates a common
West Germanic pattern inf. *sa'an - 3sg. pret. *seu(w) - 3pl. pret.
*seu(w)un - pret. ptc. *sā'an-, where the infinitive and the preterite
participle continue the hiatus resulting from the Proto-Germanic j-loss
(inf. PGmc. *sējan > *sē'an > WGmc. *sā'an). This common pattern
was short-lived, and its is not even certain that a full-fledged preterite
82

=== page 83 ===
83
inflection of this type had been established before Old High German and
its most closely related dialects transferred the verba pura to the weak
conjugation.
The Old High German verba pura are generally classified as
belonging to Class I of weak verbs (cf. Braune 1987, 290), thus having
the following standard paradigm of the present indicative:
inf. sāen 'to sow’
1sg. sā(i)u
2sg. sais
1pl. sāemēs
2pl. säet
3sg. säit
3pl. säent
On the other hand, the Old High German inventory of forms
of the verba pura also contains forms which do not show a trace of *j
but have a hiatus and endings which look like--and have been inter-
preted as--normal thematic strong endings (the type inf. säan, 3pl. pres.
ind. sāant, pres. ptc. sāanti, etc.). Contracted forms also occur, inf. sān,
etc., as well as forms with intervocalic h, w, and i, apparently serving
as hiatus fillers. Such forms are attested with Class I endings (inf.
sähen, 3pl. pres. sāwent, and pres. ptc. cluoienti (of PGmc. *glōjan))
and the seemingly strong endings (3pl. pres. arplāhant, pres. ptc.
arplāhanti (of *blējan)).
The weak preterite and the preterite participle also belong to
Class I, showing byforms with and without the connecting vowel -i-
(3sg. pret. sāta/säita, ptc. gisāt/gisāit). No strong preterite forms are

=== page 84 ===
84
attested, but there are early attestations of the strong preterite participle
(zaplāhanner, ziplāner).¹
4.1.1 OHG sāen vs. sāan: Earlier theories
In the literature on the development of the verba pura, the
standard view holds that the Old High German verbs of this type show
present stem forms of two origins. On the one hand, there are the
normal forms of the type inf. säen, which point to a j-suffix (as if from
WGmc. *sājan) and are analyzed as belonging to Class I of weak verbs.
Earlier scholars who set up je/o-presents for the verba pura have de-
rived such forms phonologically from j-presents of the type PGmc.
*sējan (e.g., Kluge 1910, 106-7), preserving the PGmc. *j into West
Germanic. It is then assumed that WGmc. inf. *sājan (< PGmc. *sējan)
yielded OHG säen with the phonological change of *-ja- to OHG -e-, as
in the j-presents of Class I weak verbs (cf. hōren 'to hear' < PGmc.
*hauzijan).² But it is also claimed that beside the j-present inflection
there are attested j-less forms of the type OHG saan, i.e., forms with
"strong" endings. It has proved difficult to reconcile this latter type
with the opinion that the verba pura inherited j-presents.
1 Examples of the Old High German forms in this section and the
following discussion, as well as information on the attestations, are
taken from Bremer's collection of the forms of the verba pura (Bremer
1886, 61-69), Raven (1963-67) and Karg-Gasterstädt/Frings (1968-91).
2 At first glance, this inflection might seem to accord perfectly with the
view that intervocalic *j was preserved in Proto-Germanic, and OHG
säen was one of Cowgill's examples of a preserved PGmc. *j (1960a,
494, cf. section 1.4).

=== page 85 ===
85
The seemingly strong forms have not posed a problem for the
view that the verba pura never had je/o-presents; they have simply been
considered phonological (PGmc. inf. *sē-an > OHG sãan) (so Bremer
1886, 62, 71; Baesecke 1918, 231). This is consistent with the fact that
the "strong" forms appear already in the earliest Old High German
texts. The forms of the type OHG säen then require a separate ex-
planation and are considered analogical to verbs of the weak Class I (the
type OHG hōren).
On the other hand, those scholars who have held that the Ger-
manic verba pura formed j-presents (partly or exclusively) have
invoked the analogical influence of j-less, strong verbs to explain the
forms of the type sāan.
Van Helten (1909, 278-79) seems to have been of the opinion that
the verba pura did not generalize j-presents completely in Proto-
Germanic and that individual verbs with and without PGmc. *j influ-
enced each other--the point of contact being the forms of the 2,3sg.
present indicative, where both types had the endings *-is, *-ið. In this
way *j could be eliminated from the paradigm. Thus, van Helten con-
sidered the forms 3pl. pres. arplāhant and nāant to have been j-less from
the start, but the forms inf. sāan and 3pl. pres. tāant to have lost *j by
analogy. It is, however, highly implausible that there were any genu-
inely j-less verba pura of the structure *se-an in Proto-Germanic (cf.
section 3.1), which makes this explanation unattractive.³
3 Matzel (1987b, 219) rejected van Helten's explanation, as he would
have expected such influence from the strong verbs to have affected all
weak verbs of Class I. Although it is conceivable that it had not reached
beyond the verba pura, the objection mentioned above is a sufficient
reason to turn it down.

=== page 86 ===
86
Lindeman (1968, 66, n. 1) argued that the weak preterite gave
rise to analogical j-less present forms.4 Since OHG sāta, a weak pret-
erite without a connecting vowel, could be interpreted as belonging to
either a j-present or a strong present, secondary strong forms could be
formed (pret. *brūhta: inf. brūchan, pret. sāta : inf. X; X = sāan). The
weakness of this scenario lies in the scarcity of verbs with a strong
present and a weak preterite. Lindeman's example brūchan 'to need is
somewhat unfortunate, for it is isolated and its weak preterite brūchte is
not attested until the 12th century.5 The verbs bringan ‘to bring,' pret.
brāhta, gi-rīsan 'to be fitting,' pret. gi-rista, and biginnan ‘to begin,'
pret. bigonda, have different stems in the present and the preterite and
therefore would not make good models either (examples from Braune
1987, 255, 274, 276-77).
In a different analogical explanation, Matzel (1987a, 185-86;
1987b, 220-21) pointed out the similarities between the preterite plural
indicative forms and the preterite optative forms of strong verbs of

Classes V and VI and of the verba pura. For instance, 3pl. pret. ind.

structure as the corresponding forms of the verba pura, pre-OHG
*sēðun 'they sowed' (of the verb PGmc. *sējan) and pre-OHG *glōðun
'they glowed' (of the verb PGmc. *glōjan). Matzel apparently believed
that the preterites of the verba pura were interpreted as strong and thus
gave rise to strong present forms. This explanation must be considered
extremely implausible. Interpreting the preterite of the verba pura as
strong entails assuming that the dental preterite suffix *-ð- belonged to
the verbal root, which means that the new strong present forms should
have contained a root-final dental as well. Strictly speaking, the ana-
logical pattern should have looked as follows:

=== page 88 ===

imagine that the preterite plural forms, if interpreted as strong, could
have given rise to strong present forms without affecting the forms of
the preterite singular. It is clear that once the weak preterite of the
verba pura had been formed in the prehistory of Old High German, it
stayed that way, and no secondary strong preterite singular forms were
created.
None of the above attempts at explaining the Old High German
"strong" hiatus forms as analogical has therefore been completely
successful, and Bremer's phonological derivation of the "strong" forms
has the disadvantage of sacrificing their j-present origin.
In fact, the "strong" forms seem to be easily explained under the
assumption of a Proto-Germanic loss of intervocalic *j. The j-loss rule
allows the derivation of the verba pura from Proto-Germanic j-presents
which developed phonologically to Old High German hiatus forms with
"strong" endings.

4.1.2 OHG sāen vs. sāan: The philological evidence
The above discussion has described the consequences of the
assumption (of Bremer, Baesecke, Lindeman, Matzel, cf. above) that the
Old High German verba pura show present stem forms of two origins.
The existence of forms of the type OHG säen, i.e., a j-present of the
weak Class I, is not surprising. In fact, it seems a priori consistent that
Old High German, which has provided the verba pura with a secondary
weak preterite (3sg. pret. ind. sāta 'sowed'), would have created a
secondary weak present as well. On the other hand, in view of the fact
that not a single example of the older strong preterite is attested, only
rare strong preterite participle forms (e.g., zaplahanner 8th cent., dat.
sg. inbláhenen 11th cent., cf. Bremer 1886, 62, 68), it is quite re-
markable that the language preserved a considerable number of strong
present tense forms beside the weak forms of Class I that got the upper
hand.
The Old High German present tense inflection shows considerable
variation, and the distinction between the inflections of the strong verbs
and those of the weak Class I was to a large extent leveled out, as shown
by the paradigms below (cf. Braune 1987, 256-62). The claim that
strong present forms of the verba pura occur--phonological or ana-
logical--cannot be proved unless there is evidence of unambiguously
strong forms attested in texts which make a sharp distinction between
these two conjugations.

First of all, some forms of the verba pura are obviously

belong to either class. Second, contracted forms (e.g., pres. ptc. thrandi
Gl.K., 1sg. pres. ind. muo fragm. X of Hraban. Gl., 3pl. pichnant Can.
10) cannot count as evidence, for it has not been proved that they
developed from strong forms rather than weak ones. Third, forms with
the ending 1sg. pres. ind. -u (incnahu Tatian, giblao Gl. IV, 47, 50,
12th cent.) are not unequivocally strong.7 Even the potential appear-

between strong verbs with the ending -u and weak Class I verbs in -iu

would not be an absolute proof. There is an important structural
difference between the verba pura and Class I verbs of the type hōren,
in that the *j of a present form of the type 1sg. pres. *sāju was inter-
vocalic. It is by no means certain that the change of *sāju to său would
have begun at the same time as the change of hōriu to hōru and not
earlier.
Finally, the evidence of the distribution of the vowels -a- (strong)
and -e- (Cl. I weak) in the forms of the 3pl. present indicative (-ant vs.
-ent), the infinitive (-an vs. -en), and the present participle (-anti vs.
-enti) must be treated with caution. The difference was leveled out
quite early; -a- was favored in Upper German and commonly sub-
stituted for -e- in the corresponding forms of weak Class 1.8 In fact, the
philological evidence does not prove the existence of unambiguously
strong forms spelled with -a-, as the following collection of a-spellings
demonstrates.
8 Cf. Braune/Eggers on 3pl. pres. ind.: "Im 9. Jh. (T) ist dieser Unter-
schied bereits ausgeglichen" (Braune 1987, 256), ". im übrigen treten

umgekehrt -ent auch bei den stV. erscheint. Das erste ist obd. der Fall"
(op. cit., 261); on the infinitive: "In einer Ausgleichsbewegung erhalten
die swV. I von den stV. die Endung -an, . . . Dieser Ausgleich ist
hauptsächlich obd. und nimmt dort schon im 9. Jh. einen breiten Raum
ein" (op. cit., 265), and on the present participle: "In swV. I erscheint
besonders im Obd. auch häufig -anti" (op. cit., 266).
The Upper German a-spellings have thus been considered ana-
logical to strong verbs (Baesecke 1918, 203; Braune 1987, 112, 265),
but Schatz (1907, 99) seems to have assumed a special Upper German
sound change; at least he speculated that "das aus e (für ja) entstandene a
zwischen a und e lag" since the forms of verbs of Class I are commonly
spelled -en, -ent, -enti as well.

=== page 93 ===
93
It is certainly striking that most of the forms listed above appear
to be Upper German and thus cannot be considered unambiguously
strong. Even the oldest examples, 3pl. pres. ind. arplahant, pres. ptc.
arplahanti, arplahandi (Glossarium abrogans, 8th century), which in
Bremer's opinion supported the original strong inflection of the Old
High German present tense of the verba pura (Bremer 1886, 62), occur
in a text which shows a-spellings in verbs of Class I (cf. Kögel 1879,
184, 186-87).11 Middle German examples are insignificant; pres. ptc.
EFranc. druoanti and LFranc. thráandian are attested in texts in which
weak verbs of Class I both appear with -anti and -enti (cf. above).
The lack of evidence for genuine strong present tense forms of
the verba pura points to the conclusion that Old High German only
displays j-present forms belonging to Class I of weak verbs, with inf.
*sājan being the immediate preform of OHG säen. 12 In addition to the
philological evidence, the complete absence of strong preterite forms
makes it unlikely that any strong present forms survived; it is now clear
that the only relics of the older strong inflection of the verba pura in
Old High German are the isolated strong preterite participles (e.g.,
zaplahanner).
11 Kögel actually listed these forms under forms of j-presents spelled
with a (1879, 184, 187).
12 It is quite likely that the misconception of the genuinely "strong"
present forms originated in Bremer's article (Bremer 1886). Bremer
claimed that the verba pura never had ie/o-presents and accordingly
interpreted the relevant forms (the types OHG sāan, sāhan, sān) as evi-
dence for a Proto-Germanic hiatus. Bremer's work provides a good
collection of attestations of the verba pura and is frequently cited.
Later scholars probably simply accepted Bremer's idea of two inde-
pendent inflections, a j-present and relics of a strong present, existing
side by side in Old High German.

=== page 94 ===
94
The Old High German attestations of the verba pura thus make it
necessary to assume that in the prehistory of that language inconvenient
hiatus forms caused by the loss of PGmc. *j were eliminated. This was
done by remaking a weak j-present which fitted the weak preterite and
completely ousted the older "strong" forms. The development to the
Old High German weak present can be described as follows:
inf. *sējan > *sē'an > NWGmc. *sā'an → *sājan > OHG sāen (> sān)
The a-spellings of the type OHG inf. sāan are, therefore, not
relics of NWGmc. *sa'an directly descended from Proto-Germanic
hiatus forms, but rather an Upper German innovation. Their creation
--by analogy to strong verbs or as a phonologically developed dialect
feature (cf. above)--was not a process limited to the verba pura; the
verba pura simply participated in the general development of the j-
presents of weak verbs of Class I.
The discovery that the allegedly strong present forms of the Old
High German verba pura are an illusion renders superfluous the ana-
logical scenarios constructed for the development of strong forms in
Old High German. The explanations by van Helten, Lindeman, and
Matzel, scholars who reconstructed Proto-Germanic j-presents and
considered OHG säen phonological, were unconvincing (cf. section
4.1.1). It is not surprising that it has proved difficult to account for a
type of forms which never existed.

=== page 95 ===
95
4.1.3 The Old High German dental preterite
The only relics of the West Germanic pattern *sā'an - *seu(w) -
*seu(w)un - *sā'an- in Old High German are a few preterite participle
forms (e.g., zaplahanner, dat. sg. inbláhenen, cf. section 4.1); otherwise,
the forms of the verba pura belong to the weak inflection of Class I,
OHG sãen - pret. sāta (byform säita)- pret. ptc. gisāit (byform gisāt).
The creation of the dental preterite can be justified by a wish to elimi-
nate the inconvenient strong forms, cf. 3pl. pret. *seu(w)un. 13 As was
made clear in the discussion of the Old Norse verba pura (cf. section
3.2.3 above), the creation of the dental preterite of the verba pura does
not necessarily presuppose a pre-existing weak present. The Old High
German weak inflection differs from that of Old Norse, however, in
showing a Class I j-present as well as unambiguous preterite forms of
that class (pret. sāta (byform sāita) and pret. ptc. gisāit (byform gisāt)).
It must be investigated whether the creation of the weak preterite has
any implications for the view presented here of the present type OHG
sāen having been created as a secondary j-present.
It is a common view that the weak preterite of the verba pura was
created to the "weak" j-present (Paul 1880, 159, 161; van Helten 1909,
278-79; Kluge 1910, 106-7; Krüer 1914, 244-45; Flasdieck 1936, 308;
13 The incentive, according to Matzel (1987a, 192-93), was the
preterite plural, viz., the types 1pl. *Cē²um, *Ceu'um with hiatus
forms in need of remodeling. It is not certain, however, that any of the
verba pura had preterites with *ē² or that there was a real hiatus in
*Ceu'um when the dental preterite was created.

=== page 96 ===
96
Lindeman 1968, 65-67). There has not, however, been agreement on
whether the verba pura created a weak preterite with or without the
connecting vowel *-i-, or how early influence from weak verbs of
Class I was exerted. Subgroups of Class I have been invoked as direct
models; thus Krüer pointed to verbs of the root structure Gmc. *CŴh-
as being the closest parallels to the vowel-final verba pura. 14 Ana-
logical influence from long-stem verbs of Class I presupposes a con-
traction from pre-OHG *sa-i-da to OHG sāta, if the verba pura acquired
a weak preterite no later than at the stage when Class I preterites had
the shape *wih-i-da, *hōr-i-da, but Krüer maintained that the weak
preterites were not created until the -i- had been syncopated in the long-
stem preterites of Class I. On the other hand, Lindeman (1968, 67)
derived sāta from Proto-OHG *sæw-i-ða assuming the contraction
process of the Class I preterites 3pl. gehīton (inf. hiwen 'to marry') and
3sg. opt. gilāti (inf. *gilāwen 'to betray'). 15
Matzel (1987a, 1987b) observed that the Old High German verba
pura did not pattern exactly like Class I verbs of the type hōren,
14 "Weiterhin standen den vokalischen Wurzeln die einsilbigen
schwachen mit Langvokal + germanischem ham nächsten. Man ver-
gleiche Formen wie die 3. sg. *glôid, *sâid mit *wîhid, *smâhiď u. s.
w., in denen bekanntlich das h früh bereits zum Hauchlaute geworden
war. Stand aber einem oberdeutschen Praes. wîhu, wîhis, wîhit nach
der Synkope gewöhnlich das Praet. wîhta gegenüber, so mußten beim
Übertritt der sâjan-Sippe analog Praet. wie sâta, glôta u. s. w. gebildet
werden." (Krüer 1914, 245).
15 Lindeman assumed that the Old High German weak present devel-
oped from the mixed present paradigm 1sg. *sæ-ju, 2sg. *sæw-iz, 3sg.
*sæw-ið, etc., with the stem *saw- appearing in prevocalic position.
This reconstruction of the dental preterite would only be possible if it
could be shown that the present stem contained *w at this early stage,
cf. the discussion of Lindeman's hypothesis in section 5.1.1.

=== page 97 ===
97
notably, 1) that i-less forms of the uninflected preterite participle were
more common in verba pura (gisāt) than in the type hōren (gihōrt), 2)
that preterite forms of verba pura without the connecting vowel (sāta)
were much more frequent than those with -i- (sāita), and 3) that i-less
preterite forms of verba pura occurred in texts which did not show
syncopated forms of the hōren type. He concluded that the i-less forms
were original and that the verba pura later acquired secondary forms
with -i- by influence from the long-stem verbs of Class I, thereby re-
viving an old view represented (in different guises) by Mahlow (1879,
20), Möller (1880, 469), Bremer (1886, 69-71), Collitz (1912, 88-91),
Baesecke (1918, 232), Sverdrup (1929, 70), and Wagner (1950, 48,
n. 1). Matzel sided with the scholars who regarded the Indo-European
to-participle as the source of the dental preterites, the preterite parti-
ciple type OHG gisāt and ON sáðr being the direct outcome of a Proto-
Germanic participle *sēða- (Matzel 1987a, 188-98).16 As demonstrated
above (section 3.2.3), this opinion must be rejected; it is extremely un-
likely that the verba pura had living to-participles of the type *sēða-
when their weak preterite was formed.
16 Mahlow and Sverdrup had explicitly invoked the influence of the
preterite participle, and so had Collitz (1912, 90) in the case of the verb
*sējan. Möller actually reconstructed a pre-Germanic preterite *sē-
tấm, which is out of the question in view of the reduplicated preterites
of Gothic and Old Norse and the type *seu(w) in Old English, etc.
Bremer stated that pret. sāta and pret. ptc. gisät (gisān) were formed
"ohne zweifel unmittelbar vom stamme sâ-" (1886, 69). The theory of
an originally i-less preterite and participle was especially welcome to
Bremer, who assumed genuine "strong" presents of the verba pura and
was thus unable to create a secondary weak preterite of Class I with the
aid of an already existing j-present.

=== page 98 ===
98
Matzel's observation that the preterites and uninflected preterite
participles of the Old High German verba pura more frequently oc-
curred without the connecting vowel -i- than was the case with Class I
verbs of the type hōren is not a sufficient reason for demanding an
originally i-less weak preterite. The fact should not be ignored that the
structure of the verba pura differed from that of the type hōren in a
significant way, namely, in having a vowel-final root which could lead
to unstable hiatus forms. As a consequence, the question of whether the
preterite was formed with or without *-i- is probably undecidable. A
preterite *sā-i-da would have begun to contract to OHG sāta quite
early, and not necessarily hand-in-hand with the syncope of -i- in the
preterite of the type hōren. An early contraction of pret. *sā-i-da to
OHG sāta seems more plausible, if anything, than the contraction of
*CVw-i-da to OHG *CVta in the Class I preterites 3pl. gehīton (inf.
hiwen 'to marry') and 3sg. opt. gilāti (inf. *gilāwen 'to betray'). It
would therefore be difficult to prove that the syncope of the -i- in a
pre-Old High German preterite *să-i-da should have taken place at the
exact time when -i- was syncopated in *hōr-i-da and not earlier.17 In
the same way, the difference in phonological structure makes it im-
possible to require that the change of, e.g., 1sg. pres. ind. *sāju to sāu
or inf. *sājan to sãen was "the same" process as the change of 1sg. pres.
ind. hōriu to hōru and inf. *hōrjan to hōren.
Therefore, Matzel's arguments do not rule out the possibility of a
pre-Old High German Class I preterite *sā-i-da. This preterite would
17 Of course, the same argument applies against Krüer's (1914, 244-45)
solution of having sāta created after or during the time of the syncope
in hōrta, cf. above.

=== page 99 ===
99
most easily be created on the basis of the secondary pre-Old High
German j-present discussed in section 4.1.2 (and further explained in
section 4.1.4 below).
inf. *hōrjan

3sg. pret. *hōr-i-da
inf. *sājan

On the other hand, the possibility of an originally i-less weak
preterite also remains, for it is perfectly plausible that it was formed by
adding the dental suffix directly to the root (3sg. pret. *sē+dē), cf. the
discussion of the Old Norse preterite sáði and OE būde in section 3.2.3
above. The role of the to-participle in its creation would probably have
been insignificant. If this was the case, the preterite could in principle
have been formed before the West Germanic hiatus present *sa'an
acquired the secondary j-suffix.
The formation of the dental preterite of the verba pura therefore
does not turn out to have important consequences for this discussion of
the j-present. It can be shown that its creation does not depend on the
pre-existence of an original j-present with the Proto-Germanic *j pre-
served; a secondary j-present can easily have had the same effect, and
the preterite may (if i-less) have been completely independent of the
structure of the present.

4.1.4 The Old High German present
To account for the attested Old High German forms of the verba
pura it is necessary to assume that this language--the other West Ger-
manic dialects will be discussed later--created a secondary j-present. Its
creation from the West Germanic pattern *sā'an - *seu(w) - *seu(w)un
- *sā'an- --or, from the pattern pre-OHG *sā'an - *sāda - *sā'an- if a
weak preterite had already been formed--may have been brought about
by a simple analogy to the presents of the weak Class I. Both groups
shared the i-initial endings of the present after the West Germanic rule
of j-loss before i had applied to the j-presents.18

analogical pattern suggested by Cowgill (1959, 8-9, cf. section 1.3.1)

ence of the presents of Class I (2sg. pres. *dōmi-s: 1sg. pres. *dōmiju,
2sg. pres. *laþoō-s: 1sg. pres. X; X = *laþōju). The verbs of Class II
had undergone contraction following the loss of PGmc. *j, but the
verba pura had preserved the hiatus after an accented vowel (2sg. pres.
*sā-is) into West Germanic. Classes I and II further had a weak pret-
erite in common (*dōmida, *laþōda), which was not the case with the
verba pura (unless the i-less preterite had arisen before the creation of

Of course, the verba pura were not the only strong verbs with the
endings 2sg. pres. ind. *-is, 3sg. *-id, 2pl. *-id. These were the normal
thematic strong endings, but as consonant-final roots kept the stem and
the personal endings clearly distinct (e.g., 1sg. *biud-u), an additional j-
suffix would not have been of any advantage to such verbs and the in-
fluence of Class I j-presents did not affect them. The secondary j-
present, on the other hand, was an asset to the verba pura, which had
vowel-final roots and therefore hiatus forms in danger of contracting.
The result was the following j-present paradigm:

of *ja to e had taken place in the verba pura and the weak verbs of Class
I before the time of the earliest texts (inf. *sājan > sāen, *hōrjan >
hōren, likewise in other forms with *-ja-). Upper German texts have
j-less forms with -a- (inf. säan, hōran, etc., cf. section 4.1.2). The
verba pura must also have lost the secondary *j before u-endings very

Forms with w are attested very early; pret. ptc. gesauuit is the

the Old High German evidence, i.e., w-forms attested beside forms
with the hiatus fillers h and j, recommends the view that w arose as a
phonetic glide as well (cf. Bremer 1886, 72; van Helten 1909, 281).
The original locus of the w in the Old High German verba pura is,
therefore, the hiatus before u in the endings of the 1sg. and 1pl. present

Lat. leo without w) is best explained as having obtained a hiatus-filling
w before the u of acc. sg., nom., acc. pl. leun, perhaps also before o (cf.
Bremer 1886, 72).

It is at least clear from the attested forms that the various dialects
favored different hiatus fillers and must have leveled them through the
paradigms quite early. h is thus attested early before different vowels,
e.g., in 3sg. pres. ind. lohit, and is occasionally found outside of the

'trust, believe,' Braune 1987, 145). Several w-forms occurring in
Tatian (ca. 830) indicate that the w had been generalized in East Fran-
conian at that time (3sg. pres. ind. sauuit, 3pl. sauuent, dat. inf. zi
sauuenne, pres. ptc. gen. sg. sauuentes, pret. ptc. gisauuit). The form
1sg. pres. ind. sauuiu, in particular, shows that the w had been inter-
preted as belonging to the verbal root (*sāwu obtaining the Class I
ending -iu by analogy: 2sg. *hōris: 1sg. *hōriu, 2sg. *sāwis : X;
X = 1sg. *sawiu).
As of the 11th century the hiatus breaker j was quite common and
had been extended beyond its original domain (e.g., 1sg. pres. ind. naio
"Münchener glossen," 3sg. uuâiet, 3pl. blûoient in Williram), and forms
with i-umlaut appear as well (1sg. pres. ind. dreio "Münchener
glossen"). j-forms with umlaut were continued in Middle High German
(saejen, müejen) with remnants of contracted forms beside them (wān,
muon). The umlaut of the j-forms could be extended to contracted
forms (waen) and forms with w (saewen, müewen) and h (saehen,
mühen).28
28 For the Middle High German evidence, cf. van Helten 1909, 280-81,
who, moreover, stated that "wēhen, saehen, sēhen, sēen, mühen, müen
etc. können formen mit oder ohne -j- repräsentieren."

=== page 107 ===
107
The results of the above discussion of the changes involving *j in
present tense forms of the verba pura from Proto-Germanic to Old
High German can be summarized briefly as follows:
1. PGmc. inf. *sējan> *sē'an (loss of intervocalic *j) > WGmc. *sä'an
2. WGmc. *sa'an → pre-OHG *sājan (insertion of secondary *j,
analogical to Class I)29
3. Pre-OHG *sājan > OHG sāen (phonological change of *ja > e;
dialectal variant säan phonological or analogical)
4. OHG sāen → OHG sāien (hiatus breaker j extended from forms
with i-initial endings)30
This analysis differs significantly from earlier opinions. First of
all, the standard opinion assumes a direct development PGmc. *sējan
> WGmc. *sājan > OHG *sāen; a less common view considers the
development PGmc. *sē-an > OHG sāan phonological. It is a new
suggestion that the Proto-Germanic loss of intervocalic *j created hiatus
forms which were replaced by the analogical pre-Old High German j-
present *sājan. This process is easily explainable by the influence of the
weak verbs of Class I, which lent j-forms to the present tense of the
verba pura and perhaps were the model for their dental preterite as
well. The "secondary" *j is thus a morphological phenomenon.
Second, our analysis of the philological evidence makes it likely that no
29 In the following discussion this will be referred to as a "secondary
j-present."
30 In the discussion below this will be referred to as a "tertiary j-
present" with "tertiary j."

=== page 108 ===
108
"strong" forms (WGmc. *sā'an) were left behind after the creation of
pre-OHG *sājan, nor were such forms created later by analogy; forms
of the type saan attested in Old High German were dialectal variants of
the secondary j-present. As is generally believed, the j spelled out in
OHG sāien originated late as a hiatus filler; the "tertiary" j is therefore
a phonological phenomenon completely independent of the "primary"
*j of the Proto-Germanic j-present *sējan and the "secondary" *j of
pre-OHG *sājan.
The development illustrated in the four steps above from the loss
of Proto-Germanic *j to the insertion of the "tertiary" j was a long
process. Many centuries may have elapsed between the "primary"
j-present PGmc. *sējan and the creation of the "secondary" pre-OHG
*sājan. Likewise, the hiatus fillers had ample time to develop after the
prehistoric loss of "secondary" *j (pre-OHG *sājan > OHG säen). Old
High German forms with "tertiary" j (OHG säien) do not become
common until the 11th century--an obvious and long-recognized indi-
cation that they do not directly continue prehistoric j-present forms.
4.2 Other dialects of Continental West Germanic
No West Germanic language kept the pattern *sa'an - *seu(w) -
*seu(w)un - *sa'an- for long. The weak inflection of the verba pura
*sājan - *sā(i)da - *gisād was not a strictly pre-Old High German
creation, for other West Germanic dialects on the Continent show signs
of having participated in this innovation. In fact, some dialectal forms
are difficult to interpret, and it is not possible to decide with certainty

=== page 109 ===
109
in every instance whether a form represents the "secondary" j-present
or contains the "tertiary" hiatus filler j. On the other hand, there is no
evidence which proves the survival of a Proto-Germanic *j.
4.2.1 Present tense forms in Old Saxon,
Middle Low German, and Middle Dutch
-
Attested examples in Old Saxon are not numerous, but inf. sāian
pret. sāida seems to have been the basic pattern. In view of the develop-
ment of the Old High German forms described above, the infinitive OS
sāian can a priori be interpreted in one of two ways, namely, as the
secondary j-present *sājan with *j preserved or as having the "tertiary"
hiatus filler j.31 If OS sāian represents the secondary j-present, it is
comparable to the long-stem verbs of Class I, which did not undergo
j-loss in Old Saxon and still ended in -ian (OS hōrian and sāian, cf. OHG
hōren and sāen). If săian, on the other hand, contains the hiatus filler j
(cf. OHG säien), one must assume that pre-Old Saxon lost intervocalic
*j and developed a "tertiary" hiatus filler j, which was generalized
earlier in Old Saxon than the corresponding j in Old High German, for
such j-forms are attested in the Heliand (1sg. pres. sāiu, inf. sāian, cf.
Sehrt 1925, 441). This chronological factor makes it more plausible
that OS sāian contains "secondary" *j.32 The variants 3pl. blōat (C.
31 Cf. van Helten 1909, 282, who allowed for two possibilities, viz.,
original *j and the hiatus filler.
32 In principle, one could also imagine that the inherited hiatus forms
WGmc. inf. *sa'an, etc., were not replaced by a secondary j-present in
pre-Old Saxon, but that this language developed a phonological hiatus-

4340) and blōiat (M. 4340) have been interpreted as showing a loss of

beside dōian 'to die' < PGmc. *dawjan, Schlüter in Dieter 1898-1900,
1:280). These forms do not make it absolutely clear whether a "second-
ary" or "tertiary" j was lost, although the first choice seems preferable,
but at least it is evident that blōat does not reflect an inherited Proto-
Germanic hiatus. The infinitive sēhan (M. 2389) with i-umlaut was by
Schlüter interpreted as having a hiatus filler h after the loss of j.33
According to van Helten (1909, 281), sēhan could either be a spelling
for sēian or a compromise between sāhan and sēian. The latter choice
would presumably presuppose that pre-Old Saxon *sājan actually lost its
secondary *j and that both h and j could break the resulting hiatus.
Middle Low German has, likewise, mostly j-forms (sēien, mōien),
but there are also rare occurrences of j-less forms (blōen). The type
MLG blōen might stand for relics of the secondary j-present with the
hiatus preserved (comparable to OHG säen, OS 3pl. bloat(?)). The
existence of rare preterite forms without -i-, e.g., MLG mōde, would
support this interpretation, for one could compare an older pattern
MLG blōen - *blōde to the standard forms OHG sãen - sāta and a later
MLG blōyen to OHG säien with the "tertiary" hiatus filler.34 Yet, the
breaking *j before i-initial endings in these forms directly (WGmc. 2sg.
pres. *sā'is> *sājis; hence, inf. *sājan). It is more plausible, however,
that Old Saxon participated in the creation of the Old High German
secondary j-present.
33 A comparable example of hiatus-filling h after j-loss is OS friehan
‘to love' (cf. Go. frijōn), see Schlüter in Dieter 1898-1900, 1:280.
34 Van Helten considered at least the preterite type MLG mōde older
than the type blōyede/blēide: "selten als rest regelrechter entwicklung

111
Middle Low German j-forms can be understood as continuing the j-
forms of Old Saxon (sāian) directly; hence, it is also conceivable that
they represent the secondary j-present.
Middle Dutch is said to be ambiguous in the same way as Old
Saxon, 35 which means that MDu. saeyen could, under the analysis
proposed above, continue the secondary "Continental West Germanic"
*sājan with the secondary *j preserved or have the "tertiary" hiatus
filler. There are also attestations of j-less forms, e.g., inf. roen
(< *rō'an). Such forms do not necessarily bear witness to the pre-
servation of "strong" forms (the hiatus following the Proto-Germanic
j-loss). Forms with i-initial endings had no j in the paradigm of the
secondary j-present *rōjan; hence, the form 3sg. pres. ind. *rō'it was
ambiguous and could be interpreted as belonging to a strong verb, and
as j-presents shared the ending *-it with strong verbs, an analogical
"strong" present *rō'an could be created.36
4.3 Conclusion
The above discussion has shown that the hiatus that resulted from
the loss of intervocalic *j in Proto-Germanic was not preserved in Old
High German. A secondary morphological j-present was created, and
forms spelled with i show a "tertiary" phonological hiatus breaker. It
35 Again, cf. van Helten 1909, 283.
36 This seems to have been the interpretation of van Helten: "Roen . . .
voor het gebruikelijke roeyen, was natuurlijk ontstaan onder den
invloed van hi roet" (1887, 273).

=== page 112 ===
112
appears that Old Saxon, Middle Low German, and Middle Dutch parti-
cipated in the development of the secondary j-present; thus, they share
the first two steps in the development to Old High German. On the
other hand, as certain forms are difficult to interpret, it is not known
with certainty whether the secondary j-present survives in the forms OS
sāian, MLG sēien, and MDu. saeyen, or whether these dialects also
formed a "tertiary" j-present with a hiatus filler.
1. PGmc. inf. *sējan> *sē'an (loss of intervocalic *j) > WGmc. *sā'an
2. WGmc. *sa'an → pre-OHG/OS/MLG/MDu. *sājan (secondary *j,
analogical to Class I)
3. Pre-OHG/OS/MLG/MDu. *sājan > OHG säen; OS sāian, 3pl. blōat,
MLG seien, blōen, MDu. saeyen
4. OHG 3sg. *sā'it> *sājit (the hiatus breaker j) → OHG säien 37
In spite of these uncertainties it is clear that Old High German and
its most closely related dialects do not present forms of the verba pura
which unequivocally attest the preservation of the hiatus resulting from
the Proto-Germanic j-loss, let alone the Proto-Germanic *j itself.
Contrary to the standard belief, present stem forms of the verba pura

CHAPTER 5
THE W-FORMS OF THE VERBA PURA
AND THE ANGLO-FRISIAN EVIDENCE
5.1 Forms with w
West Germanic present tense forms of the verba pura spelled with
w have been a controversial problem. The details of their derivation
from stems of the type PGmc. *sēji/a- are not immediately obvious, and
scholars have debated whether forms with win the various dialects have
a unitary explanation. In the previous chapter (section 4.1.4) it was
maintained that the w in the Old High German forms of the type säwen
is an inner-German phenomenon, having arisen as a hiatus-filling glide.
Present tense forms with w are not found in Old Saxon, Middle Dutch
or Middle Low German (cf. van Helten 1909, 283). On the other hand,
Old English has w consistently in every form of the verb (inf. sāwan,
3sg. pres. ind. sæwð, 3sg. pret. sēow, pret. ptc. sāwen),¹ and Old
Frisian has forms that indicate preforms with w (3sg. pres. grēth
< pre-OFr. *grēwith ‘grows,' mēth < *mēwith ‘mows,' pret. ptc. esēn
< *gisēwin).
1 Contraction can take place in the preterite plural, e.g., poet. rēon
<rēowun (of rōwan 'to row' (Brunner 1965, 309)).
114

=== page 115 ===
115
Explanations of the w in the West Germanic present tense forms
range from its being etymological, inherited from Proto-Indo-Euro-
pean, to its being a West Germanic, even dialectal, phenomenon. It is
often connected to the w which appears in the strong preterite forms of
the verba pura in Old English, where strong preterite forms of Class
VII (3sg. pret. sēow) are the regular pattern,² as well as the Old Saxon
hapax 3sg. pret. obarseu 'superseminavit' and comparable strong forms
in Middle Dutch (pret. sieu). It is a common view that w was trans-
ferred from the preterite to the present forms, but opinions differ
on the details of the transfer and the ultimate origin of the w in the
preterite.
5.1.1 Earlier theories
The opinion was expressed early that the West Germanic w was
of Indo-European origin. Möller (1880, 469) connected the Germanic
reduplicated preterite forms to Latin vī-perfects, comparing, e.g., Lat.
sēvīto Go. saisō, OE sēow, OS (obar)seu (< pre-Gmc. 3sg. *sesówe).
He therefore assumed that a Proto-Germanic *w, directly inherited
from Proto-Indo-European, had been used to form the perfect/preterite
of vowel-final roots. The hypothesis of an Indo-European u-perfect,
augmented by examples from other languages,³ has been a popular
2 Weak preterite forms are attested in Northumbrian (sēawde, säude),
cf. Brunner 1965, 309. Campbell (1977, 320) mentioned early West
Saxon weak forms of the verb 'to sow' but did not list them.
3 Sanskrit perfects of the type jajñáu (equated with Lat. gnōvī, cf. Fick
1883, 594), the 1sg. endings Hittite -un and Tocharian -wā, Armenian

=== page 116 ===
116
explanation of the West Germanic w-forms, supported by such scholars
as, e.g., Noreen (1894, 67), Trautmann (1906, 20-21), Hirt (1915, 142-
47), Karstien (1968, 80), Wagner (1950, 33-34), Sonderegger (1959,
152), and Meid (1971, 81). However, the existence of this Indo-Euro-
pean perfect type has hardly been proved beyond doubt,4 and it is not
quite clear either how the reconstructed Proto-Indo-European forms
could have developed into the Germanic attestations. It may be possible
to derive Go. 1,3sg. pret. saisō and ON 1sg. sera through PGmc. *sezō
from pre-Gmc. 1sg. *sesówa, 3sg. *sesówe, although the details of a
pre- or Proto-Germanic loss of intervocalic *w remain to be demon-
strated. On the other hand, the only Germanic preterite forms which
have an actual w on the surface (and thus were the reason for suspecting
preforms with PIE *ụ at all), i.e., OE sēow, OS (obar)seu, etc., indicate
a preform WGmc. *seu(w) which can hardly have developed directly
from 1sg. *sesówa, 3sg. *sesówe (cf. Lindeman 1968, 56-57). Further-
med.-pass. aorist in -w (3sg. keraw ‘ate'), and Balto-Slavic verbs with a
root extension -v- attributed to the perfect (Lith. stovéti ‘to stand,'
dévéti 'to wear (clothes)').
4 Concerning the evidence outside of Germanic for a Proto-Indo-
European origin of this type, Bammesberger (1980, 14-15) pointed out
that 3sg. perf. PIE *ĝeĝnowe should not have resulted in Skt. jajñáu
(rather *jajñáva) and preferred to think of the Sanskrit forms as an
Indic innovation, as equivalent forms are not attested in Iranian. He
also expressed doubts as to the significance of the Latin forms and
suggested a secondary origin of the -v- in the Balto-Slavic verbs. And,
as Watkins (1969, 53) observed: If it is maintained that this PIE *-ụ-
arose as a root extension (*gnō-u vs. the unextended root *gnō- in Gk.
Eyvw), it remains to be justified how it came to be associated with the
perfect (or the Armenian middle aorist) and why it was limited to 1sg.
forms in Hittite and Tocharian. Moreover, it turns out that the Arme-
nian middle aorist in -w is irrelevant for this question; the ending does
not go back to a form with PIE *u at all (-w< PIE *-to).

more, the proponents of this view have not had a consensus on the range
of the generalization of the w. Thus Möller (1880, 469) stated that the

Sonderegger (1959, 152) assumed a pre-Old High German general-
ization; he mentioned that the w was for the most part lost in Old High
German in intervocalic position. The Indo-European μ-perfect is thus
not an ideal solution; there is not adequate evidence on its Indo-Euro-

cerning the w-forms in the West Germanic preterite and present.

A better phonological explanation of the West Germanic w was
proposed by Bremer (1886, 72-73). His account of the Old High
German w-forms (cf. section 4.1.4) covers the win the other West
Germanic dialects as well, namely, w arose as a phonetic hiatus filler in
forms where u followed the long root vowel. The forms in question
are the 1sg. and 1pl. present indicative (confirmed by OHG 1sg. krāuu,
lōuu) and the plural forms of the reduplicated preterite with the endings
*-um, *-uþ, *-un; from there the w could spread to other forms in
different ways in the individual dialects. Van Helten (1909, 283-84)
brought this idea, as one of several contributing factors, into his account
of the w-forms, but it has not been widely accepted. 10 This explanation
obviously presupposes that the forms in question had a hiatus that
needed to be filled. It therefore fitted in well with Bremer's view that
the present of the verba pura never had a je/o-suffix (PGmc. inf. *sē-
an), but it was not as plainly convenient for the scholars who assumed
that the j-present suffix was preserved into West Germanic. By the

stem *seww-, which was extended to the singular on the analogy of the
Class VII verb 'to hew' (3sg. pret. *heww, 3pl. *hewwun). Bammes-
berger (1980, 17 with n. 53) rightly pointed out that a direct develop-
ment of 1pl. *se-z-um to OE *seron (cf. ON serum) would have been
preferable. However, the same can be said of his own suggestion, pre-
Gmc. 3pl. *se-sa-nt> PGmc. *sezǝ-un > *sezǝ-wun (with glide in-
sertion) > *sezwun; one would likewise have expected PIE *se-sh,-nt to
result in PGmc. *se-z-un > OE *seron. Bammesberger later proposed
(1986, 61-62) that the form 1du. *se-sǝ-we> *se-z(ə)-we> *seww had,
after the dual was extinct, provided a new preterite stem *seww-, to
which personal endings were added. It is, however, not at all plausible
that the dual, a dying category, would have had this effect, and one
wonders why the stem *seww- would have been preferred to the
existing *sez-.

=== page 122 ===
122
j-loss before *i operated. 12 It was inserted in the hiatus forms of the
paradigm 1sg. pres. ind. *sæ-ju, 2sg. *sæ-iz, 3sg. *sæ-ið, etc.,13
resulting in a mixed paradigm of forms with *j and *w, 1sg. *sæ-ju,
2sg. *sæw-iz, 3sg. *sæw-ið, etc., from which the *w was free to spread
in different ways in the dialects. 14 This suggestion can be immediately
rejected with the argument that an analogical extension of *w from the
preterite would have been more likely to affect the whole paradigm at
once. One would probably have expected a completely generalized
present stem with *w (1sg. *sæw-ju, 2sg. *sæw-iz, 3sg. *sæw-ið, etc.)
rather than a new anomalous paradigm.15
Lindeman's mixed paradigm provided one common West
Germanic source for the w-forms in Old English, Old Frisian, and Old
High German, and the j-forms in Old High German (inf. sāen), Old
Saxon (inf. sāian), and Middle Dutch (saeyen) as well. This is neither
necessary nor plausible. The secondary origin of the West Germanic
j-present (explained in section 4.1.4) argues against a common West
Germanic paradigm of this kind. It was demonstrated above that the
original *j was lost already in Proto-Germanic and that the j-present

could spread (cf. above). Lindeman (1968, 64, n. 3) rejected this with
the argument that a West Germanic loss of *w before *j could not be

attested in some West Germanic dialects (i.e., in OHG, OS, MLG, and

that innovation, for the Old English verba pura are strong verbs of
Class VII with w in every form of the present, preterite, and preterite
participle (sāwan - sæwð - sēow - sawen), and no sign of a j-present.17
It must also be stressed that it is by no means necessary to regard the
completely generalized win the Old English verba pura and the
scattered w-forms in Old High German (East Franconian) as having the
same origin. 18 It has been shown how w appeared as a phonetic glide in
Old High German, filling the hiatus after the loss of *j in the "second-
ary" j-present, and spread beyond its original domain. The w in Old
English is likely to be an older phenomenon.

5.2 The Old English and Old Frisian evidence
Starting from the j-less West Germanic pattern *sā'an - *seu(w) -
*seu(w)un- *sā'an- it is relatively easy to derive the attested Old
English structure of the verba pura with *w as the only hiatus-breaking
device. The Old Frisian material is more difficult to interpret, but this
language appears to have followed Old English in generalizing the
hiatus filler *w.
5.2.1 The Old English w-forms
The Old English preterite of the verba pura belongs to Class VII
of strong verbs, and there is no distinction between verbs with ē- and ō-
vocalism, e.g., 3sg. sēow, pl. sēowun (of sāwan < PGmc. *sējan), and
3sg. rēow, pl. rēowun (of rōwan < PGmc. *rōjan). This pattern can be
immediately reconstructed as WGmc. 3sg. *seu(w), 3pl. *seu(w)un (cf.
Lindeman 1968, 64), under the assumption that West Germanic as a
whole replaced the Proto-Germanic reduplicated preterite of the verba
pura by--or. developed them into--monosyllabic stems of Class VII.
The Old Saxon hapax 3sg. pret. obarseu and Middle Dutch preterites of
the type sieu can likewise be derived directly from *seu(w) (cf.
Lindeman op. cit., 56). The evidence for common West Germanic
forms in *-eu(w) is not unambiguous, however, as there are indications
of the vocalism PGmc. *ē₂ as well. The Old Frisian preterite blē has

=== page 125 ===
125
been reconstructed as *blēu (op. cit., 65), and Old English displays
variant forms with a monophthong ē (e.g., oncnēw, pl. oncnēwon Cura
past.). In addition, OS -seu and MDu. sieu have also been read as *sēu
(e.g., by van Helten 1896, 453-54; 1908-9, 107; Karstien 1968, 82).
If one starts from a preterite PGmc. 1sg. *se-zō (< PIE *se-sóh₁-
he, cf. Go. 1sg. saisō, ON sera), there is no compelling reason to
regard the West Germanic forms with ē as original and to prefer an
underlying vocalism *ē₂ (or *ē) to *eu. 19 It is easy to imagine that the
pattern WGmc. 3sg. *seu(w), 3pl. *seu(w)un could have been influ-
enced by Class VII verbs with the preterite vowel *ē 2. One can there-
fore assume (with Lindeman 1968, 56, 65) that all the West Germanic
strong preterites of the verba pura go back to this structure except for
the Old Frisian type blē and Old English forms like oncnēw, which got
an analogical *ē₂ from the type *hē₂t.20
19 The assumption of a Proto-Indo-European origin of the *w invites
different reconstructions (PGmc. *se-sōwa or *se-sēwa). Karstien
(1968, 81-83) thus proposed the derivation PGmc. *sésē,wa > PWGmc.
*sé-sē₂wa > *sē₂wa > POE *sēw > OE *sēo. 1pl. PGmc. *sésēwm
yielded sēwon, from which the sequence -ēw- was generalized, but
1,3sg. *seo with w secondarily added gave the standard forms with
-ēow-. OS -seu and MDu. sieu reflected *sē ₂, (< *sé-sē₂wa), likewise
with w generalized, but OFr. blē was phonologically derived from
*blēu< *beblē₂wa. There is no reason to reconstruct these preterites
with e-grade, and, moreover, the preform *sésē,wa is contradicted by
the o-grade of Go. saisō. The comparison with Lat. sēvī, a root aorist
with a secondary ų, does not prove the existence of a Proto-Indo-Euro-
pean e-grade perfect of this verb.
20 The pattern would simply be as follows as far as verbs with the pres-
ent vocalism *ē are concerned: OE inf. hātan: pret. hēt, inf. sāwan:
pret. X; X = *sew. It is not as direct for verbs with *ō (OE blōwan -
blēow with pret. pl. blēwan attested, cf. Brunner 1965, 309, and OFr.
ble), for the corresponding consonant-final stems had preterites with the
diphthong OE ēo (hrōpan - hrēop). One must, therefore, assume a

126
One might perhaps have expected to find two distinct preterite
types of the verba pura, but the verba pura with e-vocalism evidently
did not follow the verba impura in taking *ē2 in the preterite (cf. *lētan
- *lē₂t), whereas the verba pura with ō-vocalism agree with the corre-
sponding verba impura in having preterites with *eu (cf. *weup of
PGmc. *wōpijan). It is at least clear from the verb OE wēpan, pret.
wēop ‘to weep,' that reduplicated preterites of the type 3sg. PGmc.
*Ce-CōC (*we-wōp) could end up with the Old English diphthong ēo.
Both types of verba pura had the o-grade vocalism *ō in the redupli-
cated preterite, and it is perhaps understandable that they were given
*eu regardless of the vocalism of the present, that is, as *we-wōp
became *weup, so *se-zō and *re-rō became *seu and *reu, respective-
ly. The development to the stem *seuw is most likely to have been the
simple insertion of a hiatus-breaking *w before the *u of the plural
endings (e.g., 3pl. *seu-un> *seuwun), which was generalized in the
preterite paradigm.
The pre-Old English pattern can thus be assumed to have been
inf. *sā'an - 3sg. pret. *seuw - 3pl. pret. *seuwun - pret. ptc. *sā'an-,
*rō'an - *reuw - *reuwun - *rō'an-, with the following present indic-
ative paradigms, which had preserved the hiatus resulting from the loss
of Proto-Germanic *j:
spread of -ēw- from the remade type *sew to *bleuw. This is only
natural; after all, there was no distinction between the preterites of
sāwan and blōwan, which means that a byform *sēw of sēow could give
rise to *blew beside blēow.

The glide w was a natural hiatus filler before the u of the endings
of the 1st person singular and plural, in particular in verbs with the
vowel *ō; hence, 1sg. *rōwu, 1pl. *rōwum. The *w of the preterite
probably would have assisted in extending the new w to all forms of the
present paradigm of the verba pura and to the preterite participle. In
addition, the existence of verbs with an etymological *w, at least OE
flōwan (the root PIE *pley-), may have played an important role. 21

easily have been analogically retained or reintroduced in this para-

in the preterite plural and 1sg. and 1pl. present indicative forms was

Helten also brought in the etymological *w of flōwan, rōwan and
others, but his description of the process is not identical to the view
presented here; his idea of j-presents and j-less thematic presents

forms by the glide *w at the same time as the verba pura.23
5.2.2 The Old Frisian present
Although the Old Frisian material is limited, there are clear
indications that this was the only language on the Continent to follow
Old English in breaking the Proto-Germanic hiatus by *w and extend-
ing it throughout the present paradigm. First of all, the *w can be
detected in nouns such as OFr. fliueswerp ‘cast in shallow water'
(< *flōwisa-) and grōwinge 'sprouting out.' Second, present tense
forms are attested which are likely to have lost *w before *i with
subsequent contraction, e.g., 3sg. grēth < *grēwiþ< *grōwiþ, mēth
22 The loss of Proto-Germanic *w before *u is exemplified by the
derivation PIE *hju-hṇ-kó-> PGmc. *juwuŋgaz > Go. juggs ‘young'
(cf. Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 1:96; also Trautmann 1906, 60-61 with older
lit.). The West Germanic rule supposedly accounts for such examples
as dat. pl. WGmc. *knewum> *kneum > OE cneom ‘knees' (cf. Paul
1880, 162-64).
Baesecke (1918, 134) and Flasdieck (1936, 305) had pointed out
that the hiatus-filler solution conflicted with the w-loss, but Flasdieck
himself (op. cit., 308) and Lindeman (1968, 61), who assumed that this
rule was West Germanic, operated with a retained/restored *w before
u-endings. Such generalizations of *w are also assumed for other para-
digms, e.g., the feminine wō-stem nom. sg. clawu 'claw,' with w from
the oblique cases (clawe, etc.) (Brunner 1965, 110, n. 2, 142, n. 3).
23 Of course, this influence of the verb PGmc. *flōwan rests on the
assumption that it did not join the group of the verba pura until the *w
was generalized in pre-Old English, i.e., that a verbum purum *flōjan
(> *flō'an) had not been created in Proto-Germanic.

< *mēwiþ < *māwiþ. Third, even forms with y on the surface have
been thought to have lost *w before *i and created a j in hiatus (3sg.
blōy(e)t < *blōjiþ < *blōwiþ).24
On the other hand, Old Frisian also displays forms reminiscent of
those occurring in Old High German and the other Continental West
Germanic languages. The weak preterite weide has been taken to imply
a j-present *wēia.25 The isolated present participle gliände 'glowing'

present tense forms 3sg. grēth and meth belonged to the same forma-
tion, but had never acquired a *w.26

said to indicate hiatus forms (OEFr. *kriā(n) < *krē'an, etc., cf. van
Helten 1907, 154-55; Flasdieck 1936, 301).27

opinion (of Bremer 1886) that the Germanic verba pura were a uni-

It would have obvious advantages if all three types of forms could
be combined; the close ties between Old Frisian and Old English recom-
mend the w-formation. Hiatus forms or j-presents are less plausible
sources. If Bremer's j-present had been the only type in pre-Old
Frisian, the nominal derivatives with *w (fliueswerp, grōwinge) would
need a special explanation. It would be even less desirable to interpret
forms of the type OEFr. *kriā(n) < *krē'an as having preserved the
hiatus that arose from the loss of intervocalic PGmc. *j and having
spawned secondary forms with *w and *j. That assumption would
imply that Old Frisian was the only West Germanic language to pre-
serve the Proto-Germanic hiatus. One would therefore prefer an
account of the Old Frisian verba pura which could derive all the present
tense forms from an Anglo-Frisian inflection with the hiatus breaker
*w generalized throughout the paradigm. 28
The most economical scenario thus derives the Old Frisian
structure of the verba pura from the pre-Old English pattern *sāwan
*seuw - *seuwun. One must assume that pre-Old Frisian deviated early
from this pattern by creating an analogical preterite on the model of the
Class VII type 3sg. pres. *hēt (< PGmc. *hē₂t, hence, *sēw, cf. section
5.2.1). The preterite vocalism *ē₂ was productive in Old Frisian and
spread to Class VII verbs with earlier *eu, not only to the verba pura,
cf. inf. hlāpa ‘to run' - pret. hlēp. According to van Helten (1890), a
phonological loss of *w yielded most of the attested Old East Frisian
*mæō, *kræō. As we shall see below, the reconstruction *krē'an does
not necessarily presuppose a preserved Proto-Germanic hiatus.
28 This seems to have been the opinion of van Helten in his Altost-

derived from the Anglo-Frisian w-type. Starting from the pre-Old
Frisian pattern inf. *sēwa(n) - 3sg. pres. ind. *sewip - 3sg. pret. *sēw -
pl. pret. *sēwun - pret. ptc. *gisēwin, it is possible to account for the j
of the alleged *wēia as a generalized hiatus-filler. 34 After a phono-
logical loss of *w (3sg. pres. ind. *wēwiþ> *we'ip), one of two things
could happen. On the one hand, as explained above, *we'ip could
undergo contraction to 3sg. pres. *wēth (and may have generated
secondary hiatus forms (inf. *wē'an, e.g., the preform *sē'an behind
ziähn) if their loss of *w was not phonological). On the other hand,
*we'ip could develop a glide in hiatus (> *wējiþ), which was extended
to other present forms (inf. *wēia). In other words, whatever one
thinks of the plausibility of the creation of pret. *wēi+de to inf. *wēi+a,
there is no need to consider *wēia an original Proto-Germanic j-
present.
The Old Frisian material therefore presents a complicated
picture. As the exact conditions for some of the sound changes involved
in the development of the verba pura are not fully understood, it is
difficult to make definite statements about the details of the history of
various forms. Nevertheless, it appears that the alleged Frisian descen-
dants of genuine j-presents with the PGmc. *j intact (OEFr. gliānde,
wēide) and the Frisian candidates for a preserved hiatus (ModEFr. kriō
< OEFr. *kriā(n) < *krē'an, etc.) can be explained quite convincingly
on the basis of the Anglo-Frisian type of present with *w in every
*win
34 This solution is parallel to the one which van Helten proposed for the
Old West Frisian type with surface j (cf. above).

=== page 135 ===
135
form. The Old Frisian verba pura do not provide evidence that inter-
feres with the proposed Proto-Germanic loss of intervocalic *j.
5.3 Conclusion
The discussion of the verba pura in Chapters 3, 4, and 5 has made
it clear that their present tense forms in all the Germanic branches can
be derived from forms with the hiatus that was the result of the loss of
intervocalic *j in Proto-Germanic.
The present in Gothic developed phonologically from the Proto-
Germanic hiatus forms, and the hiatus is even preserved; the Proto-
Germanic j-loss rule makes it unnecessary to assume a special Gothic
rule of intervocalic j-loss after the vowel *ē. The strong present of the
verba pura in Old Norse was likewise shown to be phonologically de-
rived from the inherited hiatus forms. Analogical explanations of its
development from a Proto-Norse j-present *sājan with PGmc. *j
preserved are no longer needed.
inf. *sējan > *sē'an
inf. *sējan > *se'an > *sā'an
> Go, saian
[sɛ:an]
> ON sá
[sa:]
The development was more complicated in the West Germanic
dialects. It was shown in Chapter 4 that Old High German did not
preserve the Proto-Germanic hiatus forms. A secondary j-present (pre-
OHG *sājan) was created through the influence of the weak verbs of
Class I--a morphological innovation quite comparable to the analogical

=== page 136 ===
136
remodeling of Class II presents in Ingveonic (the type in *-ōjan). The
"secondary" *j was later lost, and attested forms spelled with -i- show a
phonologically developed "tertiary" hiatus breaker. Although certain
forms in the other continental dialects are difficult to interpret, it
appears that Old Saxon, Middle Low German, and Middle Dutch
participated in the Old High German development of the secondary
j-present; "tertiary" j-forms were possibly also created.
1. PGmc. inf. *sējan> *sē'an (loss of intervocalic *j) > WGmc. *sā'an
2. WGmc. *sa'an → pre-OHG *sājan ("secondary" *j)
3. Pre-OHG *sājan > OHG säen (phonological change of *ja > e;
dialectal variant säan phonological or analogical)
4. OHG sāen → OHG säien ("tertiary" hiatus breaker j)
Old English underwent a different development, for the Old
English verba pura show a strong inflection with w in every form. It
was argued above that the ultimate origin of this wis not to be sought in
a morphological phenomenon inherited from Proto-Indo-European; it
rather has an inner-Germanic phonological explanation. The Proto-
Germanic hiatus was broken by *w in the position before *u, which was
generalized and resulted in a structure pre-OE inf. *sāwan - 3sg. pret.
*seuw - 3pl. pret. *seuwun - pret. ptc. *sāwan- (OE sāwan, etc.). Old
Frisian shared in the generalization of *w; hence, pre-OFr. inf.
*sēwa(n) (with loss of *w in ModEFr. ziāhn). There does not seem to
be any reason to assume that hiatus forms or j-present forms survived
in Old Frisian beside the forms with *w.

=== page 137 ===
137
It is therefore evident that present stem forms of the verba pura,
providing the majority of examples of the Proto-Germanic sequences
*-éjV- and *-ójV-, accord perfectly with the view that Proto-Germanic
*j was generally lost in intervocalic position. As we shall see in the
next chapter, nominal derivations of the verba pura do not conflict with
these results.

=== page 138 ===
CHAPTER 6
PGMC. *-éjV- AND *-ójV-
NOMINAL FORMS
6.1 Nominal derivatives of the verba pura
The roots of the verba pura, ending in PGmc. *ē and *ō, might
have been expected to form j-suffixed derivatives with the sequences
PGmc. *-éj V- and *-ójV-. According to standard opinions, such words
should preserve the intervocalic *j into the dialectal period, but the
proposed rule predicts j-loss. The following sections will discuss some
nominal derivatives of the verba pura in Old Norse, Old High German,
and Old English.¹
6.1.1 North Germanic nouns with umlaut
Old Norse does not preserve the verb PGmc. *blējan ‘to blow,'
but the noun Olcel. blær m. ‘breeze' is assumed to be derived from the
same root (PIE *bhleh₂-(?) ‘to blow up, inflate,' cf. section 3.1.1). The
1 What follows is not an exhaustive list of nominal derivatives of the
Germanic verba pura, but these examples serve to illustrate the
material.
138

=== page 139 ===
139
word is usually reconstructed as a ja-stem PGmc. *blēja-z, which
implies that the intervocalic *j was preserved into Proto-Norse and
caused umlaut of *ā to ON æ (PGmc. *blēja-z > Pr.-N. *blāja-R>
Olcel. blær, cf. Falk/Torp 1909, 283; Magnússon 1989, 67; PGmc.
*blājaz, De Vries 1961, 46).
This Proto-Germanic preform does not make the word a counter-
example to the j-loss rule, however, for an early loss of *j can also lead
to ON blær. The development would be PGmc. *blēja-z> *blē'a-z
> *blā'a-z> *blãr > OIcel. blær, the last stage being the so-called R-
umlaut of *ā to æ. In fact, blær is one of the examples of R-umlaut in
Noreen's handbook (1970, 66).²
The other verb of the form PGmc. *blējan, namely, 'to bleat,'
which is not itself attested in North Germanic, has left a trace in Old
Icelandic, likewise in a noun blær (m. ‘ram'). In this case there is
reason to reconstruct a form containing *w (PGmc. *blēwi-) because of
such forms as the place-name element Blæva- (gen. pl.) and ModIcel.
blævir m. ‘ram' (< PGmc. *blēwija-, cf. Magnússon 1989, 67).³
