6.1.2 OHG sā(i)o 'sower' and krā(i)a ‘crow'
Agent nouns and nouns denoting individuals made with the suffix
*-jan-, both deverbative and denominative formations, were an estab-
lished type in Germanic, e.g., Go. fiskja ‘fisher,' ON skyti, OE scytta,
OHG scuzzio 'shot' (cf. Krahe/Meid 1967-69, 3:96-98). Such an agent
noun from the verb PGmc. *sējan 'to sow' is attested in Old High
German. OHG são m. 'sower' can be derived phonologically from
PGmc. *sē-jan- assuming Proto-Germanic i-loss and preserved hiatus
(PGmc. *sē-jan-> *sē'an-> WGmc. *sa'an-). The byform saio, how-
ever, is attested already in the late 8th century (cf. Bremer 1886, 62).
An example of the corresponding feminine formation is probably to be
seen in OHG krāia f. 'crow' (beside kräa, krā, krāwa, krēia; PGmc.
*krē-jōn- from the verbum purum PGmc. *krējan 'to crow').5
Bremer accounted for the variant saio by the insertion of j before
i-initial endings (gen., dat. sg. *sājin < *sāin) with subsequent leveling,
that is, as being on a par with verb forms with the "tertiary" hiatus
breaker (1886, 71, cf. section 4.1.4 above). However, that explanation
cannot apply to all nominal derivatives of the verba pura. The form
5 One might think of PGmc. *krē-jōn- as based on a masculine jan-stem
PGmc. *krē-jan-. The w appearing in the variant krāwa is a hiatus
filler, but cannot prove that the root had an etymological *w (cf. PGmc.
*krēwijan assumed by Paul 1882, 221-22, see sections 3.1.1 and 5.1.1).

=== page 142 ===
142
krāia (an ō- or ōn-stem paradigm with no i-initial endings) was seen by
Franck as a counterargument to the claim that i-initial endings were a
necessary condition for the hiatus breaker j, and he seems to have
allowed for the possibility that j could have developed after a palatalized
root vowel whichever vowel followed.6
A simple extension of the j from "tertiary" j-forms of the verbs
sāen and krāen is a conceivable explanation of the byforms saio and
krāia. The fact that the glide j spread fairly late in the verbal paradigm
of the verba pura might appear to argue against this account of early
occurrences of the j-forms saio and krāia. However, the chronological
factor is hardly an absolute argument against this explanation, for the
earliest j-forms of the verba pura (pres. ptc. plugentiu, pluogentiu
"Monseer glossen," 9th cent.) are not much later than the first instances
of saio ("Junius'sche glossen," late 8th cent., and "Murbacher hymnen,"
early 9th cent.), and the aforementioned plugentiu and gen. pl. chraiono
of krāia are, in fact, attested in the same source (Ms, cf. Graff 1834-42,
3:240, 4:587). This possibility can therefore not be ignored.
On the other hand, the drawbacks of these two hypotheses can
be avoided by adopting a morphological explanation of the variants são
and sāio. Several jan-stem agent nouns appear ending in -io in older
sources, e.g., scephio beside scepfo 'creator,' cempheo beside kempho
6 Franck (1909, 92-93): "Der alte Halbvokal war also als selbständiger
Laut verstummt, und der später wieder häufiger vorhandene j-Laut hat
sich als Zwischenlaut neu entwickelt. Diese lautliche Entwicklung kann
sich nicht auf die Fälle beschränkt haben, in denen eine Flexionssilbe
mit i folgte wie sā-it, das beweist ein Subst. wie craia Krähe. Die
Mouillierung des Wurzelvokals muß also genügt haben, den Zwischen-
laut zu erzeugen (vgl. Wilm. 1 §154)."

=== page 143 ===
143
'fighter' (Braune 1987, 206-7). The forms in -io can be understood to
be original, while those in -o would seem to have eliminated -i- by
analogy to those forms of the paradigm which had lost *j before *i
(cf. dat. sg. *-jin> *-in, Kluge 1886, 10). A jan-stem formed from
a vowel-final root (e.g., PGmc. *sējan-) would have undergone the
regular loss of intervocalic *j, resulting in a completely j-less paradigm.
Thus, a form like OHG são would be completely regular; the byform
sāio must have arisen through the influence of jan-stems with con-
sonant-final roots (the type scepfo, scephio above).7 Such a secondary
*j is also to be found in OHG krāia (cf. gen. pl. chraiono and the j still
appearing in jō-stem forms, e.g., sunt(e)ōno, also attested suntiōno,
Braune 1987, 111).
The development of these nominal forms is quite important for
the evaluation of Old High German forms of the verba pura of the type
sāien. It is clear that sāio and krāia are no hindrance to the assumption
that j-spellings in the verba pura originated as a hiatus-filling phenom-
enon in the position before i. The age of the attestations of sãio and
krāia is also irrelevant for that discussion, as these forms with j can be
explained by a morphological innovation independent of the develop-
ment of the hiatus filler j. Through the influence of jan- and jōn-stems
with consonant-final roots a secondary *j was inserted in forms with the
hiatus that was created by the Proto-Germanic j-loss. The origin of
these j-spellings (sāio and krāia) is thus quite comparable to the creation
7 Schatz also preferred to call the j of saio a suffix, not a hiatus-filling
glide: "Suffixales j kann in säio Sämann H T vorliegen. Sonst ist j,
geschrieben i, g Übergangslaut," (1927, 201), but he did not make it
clear whether the j-suffix was the *j of PGmc. *sējan- retained or the
result of a later remodeling.

of the secondary j-presents of the verba pura on the analogy of Class I
verbs with consonant-final roots. Therefore, it is obvious that the
forms sāio and krāia have no consequences for the postulation of a
general Proto-Germanic loss of intervocalic *j.
6.1.3 i/ja-stem adjectives
Three of the verba pura are attested with deverbative i/ja-stem
adjectives, an adjective formation originally of i-stem inflection, which
has joined ja/jō-stems in North and West Germanic. Old Norse pro-
vides two words, heið-sær ‘gift-sowing, generous' (if correctly con-
nected to *sējan) and snemm-grør ‘early-cropping' (to *grōjan).8 The
third example comes from Old English: OE 3e-cnawe ‘knowing, con-
scious, acknowledging,' on-cn@we ‘known, recognized,' or-cnawe
(-cnāwe) 'recognizable, evident' (to *knējan).
Again these words probably shed no light on the question of the
treatment of intervocalic *j in Proto-Germanic. Deverbative i/ja-stem
adjectives were a productive formation in the Germanic dialects,
especially in North Germanic, cf. Matzel's collection of words of this
type (Matzel 1974, 94-107). The antiquity of the words above is not
8 In fact, heiðsær is a hapax, occurring in scaldic poetry where it
describes a king. The interpretation 'gift-sowing, generous' reads the
first element as heið f./n. ‘gift, reward.' The word has also been
translated as 'looked on with honor, honored' (and the noun heiðsæi f.
as ‘honor,' Egilsson 1931, 236) as a compound of heiðr m. 'honor' and
-sær ‘seen,' the attested i/ja-stem adjective to the verb sjá ‘to see.' By
the latter reading this word does not constitute an example of an adjec-
tive made to a verbum purum.

=== page 145 ===
145
known with certainty, but it is clear that the umlaut effect is not to be
understood as triggered by a preserved Proto-Germanic *j.
In Old Norse, umlaut might have been expected in an original
formation PGmc. *sē-i- (nom. sg. m. PGmc. *sē-i-z> *sā-i-R > ON
sær), PGmc. *grō-i- (PGmc. *grō-i-z> *grō-i-R > ON grør), but it is
just as likely that these adjectives were formed late. The strong verbs
of Classes IV and V made such adjectives with ē-grade (adj. bærr ‘due,
entitled to' to bera ‘to bear,' mætr ‘valuable' to meta ‘to value'), and
those of Class VI had an ō-vocalism (adj. førr 'capable, passable' to fara
'to go'). It would have been quite natural to form such adjectives to the
verba pura sá and gróa with the umlauted stems sæ- and grø-.
OE -cnawe (-cnāwe) can also be interpreted without invoking
PGmc. *j. A preform PGmc. *knē-i- would not, of course, explain the
presence of win Old English, but even an ancient adjective would
probably have been influenced by the verb cnawan after it acquired the
w (cf. section 5.2.1). A late formation is also easily justifiable. The
strong verbs of Classes IV and V show the vowel & in such adjectives
(adj. bære 'producing, bearing' to beran 'to bear, bring,' mæte 'moder-
ate' to metan 'to measure'), and it would have been fitting for cnawan to
form such an adjective with ☎ (-cnāwe).

6.2 Nominal forms unrelated to the verba pura
The sequences PGmc. *-éjV- and *-ójV- also appear outside of
the verba pura and their derivatives. The following sections discuss
examples of different kinds. OE bozen 'both' is a form commonly
assumed to preserve a Proto-Germanic *j, and ON fró f. ‘relief'
exemplifies how the assumption of Proto-Germanic j-loss can open
the possibility of a new reconstruction.
6.2.1 OE bezen/bazen ‘both'
The traditional interpretation of OE bezen (byform bozen) m. pl.
'both' considers the form to be a compound of PGmc. *bō and the
demonstrative pronoun PGmc. *jena- ‘that one.” The preform has
been reconstructed as *bō-jīnō (*jī- < PIE *iei-, Holthausen 1888, 372),
*bō-junō (*ju-< *ja-, van Helten 1905-6, 92), and *bō-jenō (Holthausen
1934, 29).10 This Old English formation would thus stand isolated
among the other Germanic forms of ‘both,' the uncompounded *bai
(Go. bai) and compounds with the demonstrative pronoun PGmc. *þa-
9 These Old English forms are commonly believed to have had long
vowels (bēzen/bozen), cf. Brunner 1965, 252-53; see also section 2.2.3
above.
10 The author of this explanation was Sievers (1885, 495-96, n.1), who
did not, however, reconstruct the Proto-Germanic preform.

=== page 147 ===
147
as the second element (ON báðir, OHG beide, etc.).11 Furthermore, this
reconstruction assumes the preservation of *j in the structure PGmc.
*-ójV-, at least if it is to be understood that the compound was created
before the loss of Proto-Germanic intervocalic *j.
An investigation of the philological and metrical evidence for the
forms of OE bezen/bozen brought Seebold (1968, 417-36) to the con-
clusion that the reconstruction *bō-jenō should be rejected, as the
nominative forms in the oldest sources showed spellings which did not
reveal bazen (< *bō-j-). Seebold preferred to regard the history of
bezen/bozen as parallel to that of OE twezen/twæzen m. pl. 'two.'12
The philological evidence allowed, in Seebold's opinion, the
assumption that the genitive forms boeza and twoeza had a genuine
vowel ã, which could be interpreted as a remodeling of the Germanic
preforms. According to Seebold, the Proto-Germanic gen. pl. *bajjōn
and *twajjō” (cf. ON beggja, tveggja, Go. twaddjē) would have taken
the vowel *ō over from (the neuter nom.-acc. pl. (from du.)) *bō and
*twō. This claim is plausible; it takes OE bæ3V- by umlaut from a
preform *bōjV-. Moreover, the derivation does not conflict with the
rule of Proto-Germanic j-loss, as the proposed form *bōjV- had not
been created when the rule applied. It would be tempting to regard the
nominative forms bezen/bozen (and twezen/twozen) as having taken
over the sequence *bōjV- (*twōj V-) of the remade genitive. But if it is
correct that the early attestations unequivocally indicate a nominative
11 Cf. the discussion of the origin of the Germanic words for 'both' in
the context of Go. bajõþs in section 2.2.2.
12 Cf. the discussion of Seebold's derivation of OE twezen from PGmc.
*twajinaz in section 2.2.3 above.

preform with be- (and likewise twe-), which is to be interpreted as an
original short vocalism (*ba-, *twa-), a different solution must be
sought.
In any case, it is clear that the vowel œ of OE bozen does not
demonstrate the umlaut effect of the preserved Proto-Germanic *j of a
preform PGmc. *bōjV-; the *j of bozen is likely to have arisen through
a pre-Old English paradigmatic remodeling. The relevance of OE
bezen/bozen and twezen/twozen for the question of Proto-Germanic
j-loss is uncertain, as their origin has not yet been explained satis-
factorily.
6.2.2 ON fró ‘relief'
The noun ON fró f. ‘relief' is commonly thought to be a loan-
word from Low German (MLG vrō, cf. OHG frao, frō ‘glad, happy,'
De Vries 1961, 143; Magnússon 1989, 210). The German adjective is
derived from PGmc. *frawa- (cf. Skt. právate ‘jumps, hastens' of the
root PIE *preu- 'to jump, leap,' Pokorny 1959, 845-46), which has a
direct equivalent in the Old Norse adjective frár ‘quick,' appearing also
in the Runic Norse name Frawarādar.13
Holthausen (1948, 73) compared ON fró with Gk. лpāúç ‘mild'
(< *лpāúç) and Skt. á-prayu- 'incessant' without explaining the forma-
tion or reconstructing preforms. Holthausen's suggestion has not been

been easy to explain the lack of umlaut in ON fró, had it been closely
related to the words suggested.

apparently PIE *preh₂-i-, *prh₂-i-, *prih₂-) with such further cognates

gically from an o-grade formation of the type of Gk. τoµóç ‘sharp,' i.e.,
PIE *prohi̟-ó-s (> *prōį-o- > PGmc. *frōja-> *frō'a- (> *frōwa-(?))
> ON frór). On the other hand, this adjective is more likely to be a late
creation made to the noun fró under the influence of ON ró f. ‘rest'
with the adjective ON rór 'calm.'

=== page 150 ===
150
borrowed from Middle Low German, but this etymology is an
instructive example of the way the rule of Proto-Germanic j-loss can
open possibilities for new reconstructions.
6.3 Conclusion
The examples discussed above of different types of nominal
derivatives of the verba pura do not conflict with the evidence of the
verbal forms for a Proto-Germanic loss of intervocalic *j.
The Old Norse forms blær and glær are commonly believed to
derive from Pr.-N. *-āja- < PGmc. *-ēja- and to show the umlaut reflex
of the preserved *j, but the umlaut effect may be caused by a following
*r (PGmc. *-ēja-z> *-ē'a-z> *-ā'a-z> *-ār > Olcel. -ær). Alternative
reconstructions which do not involve intervocalic *j are also possible.
The umlaut of the i/ja-stem adjectives ON heið-sær and snemm-grør and
OE -cnawe is not the result of a Proto-Germanic intervocalic *j. These
words are likely to be late creations with analogical vocalism.
The j spelled out in the Old High German agent nouns sāio and
kraia is not of Proto-Germanic date. As shown above, it is probably
independent of the phonetic hiatus filler of present tense forms of the
type OHG sāien. This is most likely to be a "secondary" *j, the result of
morphological innovations quite comparable to the process that created
the secondary *j of the verba pura.
As for OE bazen, a nominal form unrelated to the verba pura,
the traditional derivation from PGmc. *bō-jenō was shown to be too
uncertain to allow any judgements about the treatment of PGmc. *j. It

=== page 151 ===
151
is quite plausible that a stem pre-OE *bōjV- was created when the loss
of PGmc. *j was no longer active.
These examples, representative of nominal derivatives of the
verba pura and other nominal forms which might indicate PGmc.
*-éjV- and *-ójV-, thus do not provide counterevidence to the rule of
intervocalic j-loss in Proto-Germanic. In addition, ON fró demon-
strates how the assumption of Proto-Germanic j-loss can lead to a new
reconstruction.

=== page 152 ===
CHAPTER 7
THE SEQUENCE PGMC. *-újV-
7.1 Verbs in PGmc. *-ūjan
Verbs whose roots ended in PGmc. *ū have sometimes been
classified with the verba pura (e.g., by Flasdieck 1936, 295; Meid 1971,
71). This classification was rejected by Matzel (1987a, 146-47, n. 3;
1987b, 202, n. 4) on the assumption that verb stems in *ū belonged to
roots ending in resonants (u-diphthongs), not in long vowels. Classifi-
cations aside, there undeniably existed verbs in the ancient Germanic
languages with hiatus forms on the surface and the apparent root
vocalism PGmc. *ū. The proposed rule of Proto-Germanic intervocalic
j-loss would, of course, predict j-loss in the sequence PGmc. *-ūjV-; it
must be investigated whether these verbs contained an intervocalic *j.
7.1.1 PGmc. *būjan 'to live'
The Indo-European root *bhuh,- 'to come into being, become, be,
live, dwell' is well represented in Germanic with descendants attested in
all the daughter languages. Traditionally, this root has been believed to
show ablaut, cf. Pokorny's rendering "bheu-, bheuǝ- (bhu-, bhụē-):
152

=== page 153 ===
153
bhou-: bhū-" (1959, 146), but there is little evidence for anything but
the zero grade *bhuh,-, at least in the verbal forms made from this
root. Thus, the root aorist shows the zero grade (PIE 3sg. *é-bhuh,-t in
Skt. ábhūt, Gk. ếυ), as does the perfect (PIE *bhe-bhuh,- in Skt. 1sg.
babhūva, Gk. лéʊкα). The zero grade is also well documented in the
category which immediately concerns us here, viz., the je/o-present PIE
*bhuh,-je/o- (Lat. fiō, fierī ‘to become, come into being, be done, come
to pass,' Olr. bīu ‘usually am,' Gk. þúοµαι 'become, grow').1
There is every reason to believe that the Germanic verb (Go.
bauan 'to live,' Olcel. búa 'to live, dwell, prepare, inhabit,' OHG
bū(w)en ‘to live, inhabit,' OE, OS būan 'to live, dwell') likewise de-
rived from a form with zero grade, namely, the Indo-European i̟e/o-
present *bhuh,-je/o-. This opinion--firmly supported by comparative
evidence--is by no means new. The ū-vocalism in the Germanic present
was recognized by Paul already (1880, 155), and the je/o-suffix by
Osthoff (1881, 27), who reconstructed 1sg. *būjō, etc.²
1 The exact formulation of this root is debated, and scholars disagree
on the question of ablaut and the color of the laryngeal. There is no
reason to specify the laryngeal, unless one accepts the alleged evidence
of the Latin imperfect in -bam, etc., for *h₂. Cf. Rix's reconstruction
of the root aorist as 3sg. *e-bhuh₂-t, in his opinion a replacement of
*e-bhueh₂-t (1976, 214), and the root reconstructed as *bheu̟h₂- in
Cowgill/Mayrhofer (1986, 186). Among modern proponents of the
zero grade, one may mention Meid (1971, 85), who regarded PIE
*bhuh,- as the "primäre Wurzelgestalt" of this verb.
2 Meid (1971, 85) does not seem to have assumed a je/o-present, for he
reconstructed PGmc. *bu-an beside the regular verba pura with *j (e.g.,
*sējan). Cf. also Jasanoff (1978a, 82), who derived ON búa from
*bhuh₂-je/o-.
Lühr (1984, 30-31, 57) claimed that the rule that derived Lat.
fiō from PIE *bhuh,-ie/o- (PIE *-uh,-je/o-> *-uiie/o-> *-īie/o-) was
common to West Indo-European languages; it thus accounted for Olr.

=== page 154 ===
154
The alternative view, a preform with the lengthened ō-grade
*bhōu̟h,-, is not as well founded (appearing, e.g., in Persson 1912, 677;
von Friesen 1932, 88; Flasdieck 1935, 89-93; 1936, 295-99; Pokorny
1959, 149; Seebold 1970, 128).³ The ō-grade of the alleged PGmc.
*bōwan supposedly existed in the causatives Skt. bhāvayati ‘brings into
being, cares for, refreshes' and OCS izbaviti ‘to release' from PIE
*bhōuh,-eje/o-, but as causatives are a productive category in both
languages, these forms cannot be proved to be of Proto-Indo-European
date. In Slavic there are several analogous pairs of causatives in -aviti
beside verbs in -yti (here, OCS -baviti beside byti ‘to become, be'). The
fact that Skt. bhāvayati is not attested in the Rig Veda, only from the
bīu and also the b-initial forms of the verb 'to be' in West Germanic
(North. bīom, bist, bið, etc.). Lühr did not provide a convincing
number of Germanic examples of this development (only Go. infeinan
'to be touched,' understood as derived from an adjective *fija-, an
alleged cognate of Lat. pius). As a consequence of this assumption,
Lühr had to consider a number of Germanic j-presents which appar-
ently are derived from PGmc. *-ūjan to be secondary. Lühr's account
of one such example, PGmc. *dūjan ‘to shake,' will be discussed in
section 7.1.6. The lack of support in Germanic makes it very im-
plausible that this development was West Indo-European. The evidence
is strongest in the Italic languages, though hardly absolutely conclusive,
and the rule may have been limited to that branch. It is even uncertain
that the rule was at work in Lat. fiō; under a different interpretation,
Lat. fīō did not derive from PIE *bhuh,-ie/o-, but rather from a
metathesized form *bhuih,- from a zero-grade i-present PIE *bhuh,-i-
(Jay Jasanoff, p.c.). Therefore, this hypothesis does not contradict the
opinion expressed here, namely, that PIE *bhuh,-je/o- yielded PGmc.
*būjan.
3 Von Friesen was of the opinion that the two ablaut grades existed side

*būwēn (op. cit., 90). Flasdieck also assumed both ablaut grades.

Atharva Veda on (Whitney 1885, 113), and not in Avestan, also indi-

The reconstruction PGmc. *bōwan is thus not directly supported

on the assumption that the spelling au of Go. bauan represented the

reconstruction *bōwan (e.g., by Sievers 1879, 566-70).6

7.1.2 Dialectal development of *būjan
The Proto-Germanic descendant of PIE *bhuh,-je/o-, the strong
verb PGmc. *būjan, was later transferred to the weak conjugation in
most dialects through different channels. Earlier scholars have recon-
structed for Proto-Germanic several weak verbs from this root. Von
Friesen, for instance, set up a Class III verb PGmc. *būwēn and a
causative *bow(w)éjan (1932, 90), and Flasdieck a weak verb of Class
III and the denominative PGmc. *bōzōjan (1935, 92-93). Seebold's
dictionary offers a confusing picture of *bōwwjan, *bōwwōn, and a
Class III weak verb beside the strong *bōwwan (1970, 125). This is
unnecessary, for, as was the case with the verba pura, the transfer of
*būjan to the weak conjugation is likely to have taken place after the
separation of the Germanic dialects.
The late Proto-Germanic hiatus form *bū'an yielded Go. bauan
[bo:an] by the rule of lowering of *ū to [ɔ:] in hiatus (Braune 1882, 13).
The limited Gothic evidence indicates a relic of strong inflection in the
form 3sg. pres. bauiþ beside a Class III preterite bauaida (hapax); this

=== page 157 ===
157
transfer to Class III was probably conditioned by the stative, intransitive
meaning of the verb.
The form *bu'an gave ON (WN) búa directly, and the strong
inflection with a reduplicated preterite was preserved (OIcel. búa - bjó -
bjoggu - búinn).7 The East Norse vocalism ō (OSw. bōa) does not pre-
suppose a Proto-Germanic ō-grade. It has been satisfactorily explained
as having arisen through a dialectal lowering of ū to East Norse ō in
prevocalic position--a rule also seen in such further examples as the
verbs WN trúa vs. EN trōa ‘to believe' and snúa vs. snōa ‘to turn’
(Erlandsson 1972, 81). East Norse shows strong relics (OSw. pres. bør,
pret. ptc. bōin), but has otherwise taken up weak inflection (OSw. pret.
bōpe) with a preterite of the same type as that of the verba pura (OSw.
pret. gröþe), cf. Noreen 1904, 449-50. As for West Norse, Old Nor-
wegian developed weak byforms in the preterite (búði), which have
been interpreted as being of Class III (Noreen 1970, 338); direct models
for the innovation can be found in that class (trúa, pret. trúði).
The relationship of ON búa and ON byggva, -ja ‘to inhabit,
settle,' a transitive weak verb of Class I, has not been satisfactorily

available hiatus breakers, w was the most natural choice after the vowel
ū, hence, inf. būwen. No forms with hiatus-filling h or j are attested;

logically.

ence in meaning can be detected which would require būzian and bōzian
to have separate origins. Cf. also comments on Flasdieck's views in
Seebold 1970, 127.

7.1.3 ON snúa 'to turn, twist'
164
From a strictly formal point of view, the verb ON snúa ‘to turn,
twist' might have undergone the same phonological development as ON
búa and thus might represent PGmc. *snu'an (< *snūjan) unchanged.23
As should have become clear from the discussion above, the history of
ON búa can, by the evidence of other Indo-European languages, safely
be reconstructed as PGmc. *bū'an via *būjan from PIE *bhuh,-je/o-,
but in the case of snúa comparative evidence for a zero-grade je/o-
present is not available.
ON snúa (a reduplicating verb, 3sg. pret. sneri/snøri - 3pl. pret.
sneru/snøru - pret. ptc. snúinn) is traditionally connected to the verbs
Go. sniwan ‘to hasten,' OE snēowan, snōwan ‘id.'24 Pokorny cited the
root of the Germanic verbs as "snēu- : snū- und sněu-" (1959, 977), and
PIE *sneuh,- can be inferred; in Germanic there is evidence for a
laryngeal in the Verschärfung of ON snoggr (< *snawwu-) ‘quick, short
(esp. of hair),' and, supposedly, OE snēowan (if from *snewwan). The
23 The verb snúa has indeed been reconstructed as PGmc. *snu'an by
some scholars, e.g., by van Helten (1908-9, 116).
24 Suggestions for more distant cognates include the Indo-European

Germanic verbs cannot be equated with exactly parallel verbs in other

Many scholars equate the Old Norse verb snúa with OE snōwan
and reconstruct both as PGmc. *snōwan (e.g., von Friesen 1932, 91;
Brunner 1965, 295; Lindeman 1968, 51, n. 1). The phonological basis
for this assumption is the old belief that PGmc. *ōw gave *ū before
vowels in West Norse and West Germanic, which, in turn, is based on
examples such as Go. bauan vs. OIcel. búa, OE, OS, OHG būan (Sievers
1879, 566). There is no phonological reason to prefer PGmc. *snōwan
to PGmc. *snū'an (perhaps < PGmc. *snūjan) as the preform of ON
snúa. An argument against the preform PGmc. *snōwan is the verb ON
flóa ‘to flow,' derived from PGmc. *flōwan (the root PIE *pleụ-),
which makes it clear that the outcome of PGmc. *-ōwan was not OIcel.
* úa.
The equation of ON snúa with OE snōwan is also made difficult
by the fact that the relationship of the variants OE snōwan and snēowan
is far from clear. Pointing to PGmc. *snowan and *snewwan, respec-
tively, they are often interpreted as independent verbs and placed in
different inflectional classes. 26 The form snēowan is connected to the e-

7.1.4 PGmc. *gnūjan (?) and *bnūjan (?) ‘to rub'

the Germanic languages do not show traces of an original strong in-

7.1.6 Old Norse verbs in -ýja

kept the intervocalic *j in North Germanic, which caused umlaut and
resulted in ON *-ýja. Several Old Norse verbs in -ýja have hitherto

As for the North Germanic development, it is generally assumed
that i-umlaut was phonological in those forms of the strong present
paradigm which had an ending containing *-i-, i.e., 2sg. *briutis/z(i),
3sg., 2pl. *hriutiþ/ŏ(i). The umlaut was then leveled through the
singular (extended to the 1sg.) and eliminated in the plural (eliminated
from the 2pl.) to yield the standard Old Norse present tense paradigm
inf. brjóta, 1sg. brýt, 2,3sg. brýtr (with the innovation -r in the 3sg.),
1pl. brjótum, 2pl. brjótið, 3pl. brjóta. In all probability, verbs which
had lost an intervocalic *j underwent an analogous development; thus,
Proto-Norse 2sg. *bū'ir (< *bū'is/z(i)) yielded ON býr phonologi-

It can also be argued that ON rýja 'to pluck wool (off sheep)' is a

If the traditional connection of ON lýja ‘to beat, exhaust' with

to continue a je/o-present of Proto-Indo-European date. The set root

indicates a je/o-present. In fact, the Latin present might be interpreted

stem might have been expected to yield Lat. 1sg. *liō.52 Although the
original formation cannot be determined with certainty, one could
instead conceivably propose an original athematic present or perhaps
a zero-grade thematic present (tudáti present) which the daughter
languages replaced with different formations. A tudáti present PIE

assumption much more feasible that this verb (and any other verb in
-ýja which might have derived from an Indo-European tudáti present)
had been given a je/o-present in Proto-Germanic already.
The reconstruction of j-presents of the type PGmc. *-ūjan for
verbs with the outcome ON -ýja does not conflict with the rule of
intervocalic j-loss in Proto-Germanic. The j appearing in ON dýja,
lýja, and rýja can be explained as secondary, namely, as having arisen
when analogical j-presents replaced older hiatus forms. At the end of
the Proto-Germanic period the present of lýja (to take that verb as an
example) was inf. *lū'an, etc., with a hiatus in every form after the loss
of intervocalic *j (cf. the development of *būjan above). Instead of
remaking a preterite of Class VII on the model of the highly anomalous
*bū'an - *beu or an analogical ri-preterite with an outcome like snúa -
sneri, the verb acquired a weak preterite. 53 It could either have been
made by adding the dental suffix directly to the root (*lū-ðē> ON lúði)
or, conceivably, with the connecting vowel *-i- of Class I (*lū-iðē> ON
lúði, perhaps through the intermediate stage *luwiðē).54 Either method

7.2 Nominal forms
179
A few nouns in Old Norse appearing with the umlaut vowel ý
have been understood as derived from preforms with PGmc. *-ūjV-,
with the implication that the intervocalic *j had been preserved long
enough to trigger umlaut in Proto-Norse. Two examples will be
discussed in the following section.
7.2.1 ON gnýr 'noise' and trýju-soðull ‘type of saddle'
The noun ON gnýr m. ‘clash, din' is derived from the verb gnýja
'to sound.' The connection with gnúa 'to rub' might lead to the assump-
tion of an underlying ū-vocalism in all three words; hence, the deriva-
tions gnýja < PGmc. *gnūjan, ja-stem gnýr < PGmc. *gnūja-z.56 This
is, however, usually recognized as a fairly clear case of an e-grade
formation; the preterite form 3sg. gníði of gnýja indicates the pattern
PGmc. inf. *gniwjan, 3sg. pret. *gniwiðē. The noun gnýr can accord-
ingly be reconstructed as *gniwja-z and thus is not an example of the
Old Norse outcome of PGmc. *-ūja-.57
56 Cf. Osthoff's attempt at deriving gnúa and gnýja from one para-
digm (PGmc. *gnūjan, 1881, 28), and Seebold's reconstruction gnýja
< *nū-ja- (without specifying the initial consonantism, 1970, 124).
Noreen (1970, 64) used gnýja as an example of the j-umlaut of *ū to
ON Ý.
57 Among scholars in favor of e-grade are Pipping 1912, 146; De Vries
1961, 180; Magnússon 1989, 264. The reason for reconstructing

=== page 180 ===
180
The evaluation of the original root vocalism of the noun ON
trýju-soðull m. 'trough-formed saddle' does not get support from a
cognate verb. This word is a byform of treyju-sọðull, in which the first
element treyja (< PGmc. *trawjōn-) is compared to OE trīe3 n. 'tray'
(< PGmc. *trawja-) with the ablaut grade PIE *drou̟- of the root of the
Indo-European word for 'tree' (Pokorny 1959, 214-16). ON trýju-
soðull is sometimes reconstructed as reflecting the corresponding zero
grade PGmc. *trūjōn- (De Vries 1961, 598; Magnússon 1989, 1066).58
On the other hand, nothing prevents the reconstruction PGmc. *triwjōn-
(cf. Noreen 1970, 142); the e-grade is also represented, e.g., in ON
trjóna f. ‘pole' (< PGmc. *trewnōn-) and the Germanic word for 'tree'
(PGmc. *trewa-, cf. Go. triu, ON tré, etc.).
7.3 Conclusion
Examples of the sequence PGmc. *-újV- are not numerous. The
prime example of a j-present is the verb PGmc. *būjan, which can by
comparative evidence be derived directly from an Indo-European i̟e/o-
present PIE *bhuh,-je/o- (or its morphological predecessor *bhuh,-i-).
As was the case with the verba pura, which are comparable in having a
root-final long vowel, it can be argued that this verb lost the inter-
vocalic *j in Proto-Germanic. Moreover, it can be demonstrated how
*gniwjan and not *gnewjan or *gneujan will be made clear in section
8.3.1.
58 In fact, the long *ū presupposes the presence of a laryngeal; one
would have to assume the ablaut grades PIE *drouh,- and PIE *druh,-.

=== page 181 ===
181
the attested forms in the Germanic daughter languages developed by
phonological and analogical processes from the resulting hiatus forms
(late PGmc. inf. *bū'an, etc.). Given the productivity of j-presents in
Germanic, it is quite likely that other Germanic presents showing a
hiatus after the vowel *ū (e.g., ON snúa) derive from PGmc. *-ūjan as
well.
Forms which have hitherto been thought to demonstrate the
preservation of intervocalic *j into the dialectal period do not turn out
to have consequences for this argument. The Old Norse j-presents in
-ýja discussed above (ON lýja) were shown to be secondary creations,
and the examples of nominal forms with umlaut (ON gnýr) do not
require reconstructions containing PGmc. *-ūjV-.

CHAPTER 8
THE SEQUENCE PGMC. *-VwjV-
8.1 "Intervocalic" *j after u-diphthongs
The fate of Proto-Germanic *j after u-diphthongs is relevant to
the discussion of intervocalic *j, as the formulation of the conditions of
j-loss may have to take into consideration the treatment of sequences in
which PGmc. *j was preceded by a diphthong and followed by a vowel.
Preceding i-diphthongs can in general be treated in connection with the
sequences of PGmc. *-ījV- (cf. section 1.4.2).
The exact phonetic realization in Proto-Germanic of u-diphthongs
followed by *-jV- is not known with certainty. The reconstruction of
actual diphthongs is frequently encountered (e.g., PGmc. *-aujV-,
*-iujV-). The reason is probably, at least in part, to be sought in the
Gothic spelling of such sequences, e.g., inf. taujan ‘to do, make.' At
first glance, some statements in handbooks on the West Germanic
languages are difficult to reconcile with this assumption; a West
Germanic gemination of *w before *j (in sequences such as *-awja-
> *-awwja-) is sometimes described without explaining how, where, or
when the *w in *-awja- had arisen (cf. Brunner 1965, 142; Braune
1987, 106-7). There is no general consensus on the proper way to
reconstruct Proto-Germanic sequences of u-diphthongs followed by
182

=== page 183 ===
183
*-jV-. Handbooks use PGmc. *-VujV-, *-VwjV-, and *-VwijV-, and
one can even find more than one of these variants in the same
handbook.1
8.1.1
Indo-European background
and Proto-Germanic outcome
Sequences of Proto-Germanic short u-diphthongs followed by
*-jV- can derive from various Indo-European sources. The simplest
sequence is naturally PIE *-VuiV-, typically, of course, encountered in
a word with a root in *-Vụ- and a suffix *-iV-. The presence of a root-
final laryngeal should not make a difference for the Proto-Germanic
result. A laryngeal is expected to be lost very early from the sequence
PIE *-Vuh,iV-, as it has been shown that interconsonantal laryngeals
were lost before *i already in Proto-Indo-European. 2 Therefore, PIE

from *þau-ja-. The preforms PGmc. *taw-ija- and PGmc. *tau-ja- both

* -VuiV- and *-Vu̟hiV- should have yielded the same Proto-Germanic

for the loss of the laryngeal would lead to *-VuiV-. Furthermore,
forms with suffixes of the shape PIE *-VIV- play a role, as the sequence
PIE *-Vų-ViV- (notably, forms in which anit roots in short u-diph-

stances undergo the converse of Sievers' Law and ultimately fall
together with the outcome of PIE *-VuiV-. If, on the other hand, the
loss of a laryngeal left a hiatus which was subsequently filled by a glide,
Verschärfung followed; hence, the sequence PIE *-Vuh, ViV- is not
among the possible direct preforms of pre-Gmc. *-VuiV-.3
There is more than one possible interpretation of the Proto-
Germanic realization of pre-Gmc. *-VuiV-, whatever its source.
First of all, it has been assumed that the Gothic spelling (-auja-
and -iuja-) actually reflects the Proto-Germanic state of affairs, a "true
diphthong" followed by *j and a vowel. This approach was, e.g., taken
by Noreen (1970, 142), who spoke of the alternations *-auj- : *-awi-
and *-euj- : *-ewi- as Proto-Germanic. This assumption requires that
sequences of PGmc. * VujV- be investigated in the light of the treat-
ment of intervocalic *j.

=== page 185 ===

The second possible interpretation is as PGmc. *-VwjV-. This
presupposes that a truly consonantal *w preceded the Proto-Germanic
*j, in which case such structures would be irrelevant to the formulation
of sound changes involving *j in intervocalic position. This opinion is
represented by Brugmann (1897-1916, 1:336-37) and Kieckers (1928,
43), who took the apparent u-diphthongs of Gothic (taujan) and North
Germanic (ON heyja ‘to perform, carry out' < *haujan) to be second-
ary, but assumed that West Germanic had preserved the inherited
sequence and undergone a gemination of *-Vwja- to *-Vwwja- (OE
strēzan, OHG strouwen 'to strew' < WGmc. *strawwjan).
The evidence of all the Germanic languages supports the
traditional opinion that Proto-Germanic *j was preserved into the
dialectal period after apparent u-diphthongs. Taking j-presents as
examples, it is held that the Gothic infinitive taujan preserves inherited
*j. There is no ambiguity in the Gothic spelling j of the kind encoun-
tered in the verba pura (saian 'to sow' < *sējan). In Old Norse, the
inherited *j had caused umlaut and j was likewise actually spelled out,
cf. ON heyja (< *haujan). By contrast, the verba pura did not appear in
Old Norse with j, nor was there a sign of umlaut in the infinitive (ON
sá ‘to sow' < *sējan, róa ‘to row' < *rōjan). The West Germanic
gemination seen in verbs like OE strēzan, OHG strouwen 'to strew'
(< WGmc. *strawwjan) is thought to have been brought about by the
inherited *j (cf. the derivation PIE *neujo-> PGmc. *niuia-> WGmc.
*niuuia-> OHG niuwi 'new' in Brugmann 1897-1916, 1:336-37). As
might be expected, the verbs which go back to PGmc. "*-aujan" and
"*-eujan" inflect like normal j-presents.

=== page 186 ===
186
For phonetic reasons, it might seem natural that a general loss of
intervocalic *j also applied when *j was preceded by a diphthong. It is
understandable that preceding i-diphthongs were treated like the vowel
*ī, and it is known that the structures *-ījV- and *-aijV- both resulted in
Verschärfung (cf. section 1.4.2). On the other hand, it would be some-
what surprising that intervocalic *j had been lost following the vowel
*ū (PGmc. *būjan> *bū'an), but not following a u-diphthong (of a
hypothetical PGmc. *taujan). Therefore, our findings about the loss of
intervocalic *j after monophthongs (e.g., PGmc. *sējan> *sē'an, *rōjan
> *rō'an, *būjan > *bū'an) recommend the assumption that the inherited
sequences of u-diphthongs followed by *j had the form *-VwjV- in
Proto-Germanic. That view gives a very simple account of the develop-
ment into the Germanic daughter languages. Proto-Germanic *-VwjV-
was realized as *-VujV- in East and North Germanic. The development
in West Germanic actually favors the assumption of PGmc. *-VwjV-;
the West Germanic languages simply kept the consonantal *w of the
sequence *-VwjV- unchanged and later lengthened it by the West
Germanic gemination.
Examples of the development of the various Indo-European
structures in question are given below; etymologies and dialectal
developments will be explained in the following sections.
a) PIE *-Vụ-jV- (PIE *neu-io- > PGmc. *niwja-
> Go. niujis, ON nýr, OE nłowe, OHG niuwi ‘new')
b) PIE *-Vụh,-¡V- (PIE *kou̟h₂-io-> PGmc. *hawja-
> Go. hawi, ON hey, OE hie3, OHG houwi ‘hay')

d) PIE *-Vụ-VịV- (PIE *kjou-eje/o- > PGmc. *hawjan
> ON heyja, OE hēzan ‘to carry out')
This list does not exhaust the possible sources of Germanic forms which
appear to derive from Proto-Germanic *-iwjV- and *-awjV-. Although
Verschärfung is expected in the sequence PIE *-Vuh, ViV-, the geminate

of set roots in PIE *-Vuh,- with the suffix PIE *-eie/o- appear to have
been degeminated on the analogy of verbs without Verschärfung.4 In

hlywan 'to protect' < PGmc. *hliwjan, derived from the noun PGmc.

It is simplest to assume that inherited sequences of long and short
u-diphthongs followed by suffixes containing *i (and sequences of the
same kind created within Germanic) were realized in Proto-Germanic
as having a consonantal *w which was treated like a consonant with
respect to the converse of Sievers' Law. It is therefore most natural to
reconstruct Proto-Germanic sequences with short vocalism as *-VwjV-
(PGmc. *tawjan ‘to do, make,' cf. *nazjan > Go. nasjan ‘to save') and

8.2.1 The Gothic outcome of PGmc. *-awjV- and *-iwjV-
The structure PGmc. *-VwjV- yielded Gothic -VujV-. A u-
diphthong (subsequently monophthongized) was thus formed before j
(PGmc. *tawjan > Go. taujan ‘to do, make'), but *w remained con-
sonantal before i. This resulted in paradigmatic alternations in the weak
verbs of Class I (inf. taujan, 3sg. pret. tawida < PGmc. *tawjan,
*tawioē, cf. OHG zouwen 'to prepare') and nominal paradigms (neuter
ja-stem nom.-acc. sg. hawi, gen. sg. haujis ‘hay' < PGmc. *hawja,
*hawjisa, cf. ON hey, OHG houwi, OE hīez ‘id.'). In the Gothic of
Wulfila's time the diphthong *au had most likely been monophthongized
to a long open [ɔ:].5
The structures *-ewjV- and *-iwjV- were, in all probability, no
longer distinguished in Proto-Germanic, but had fallen together as
PGmc. *-iwjV-. The Gothic diphthong iu emerged in the position
before j; unlike the digraphs ai and au, which represented monoph-

Gothic au (a monophthong [ɔ:] or a diphthong [au]), see Braune 1973,

thongs, Go. iu is likely to have been a falling diphthong.6 The adjective
Go. niujis ‘new' is an example of the outcome of an original e-vocalism

assume that PIE *sih,u- underwent laryngeal metathesis before con-
sonant to yield PIE *siuh,-, which is continued in the Latin and Balto-

the gemination of *w before *j in the West Germanic present of this
verb (OHG siuwen, OE siowian) has been thought to indicate a short
vocalism, a pre-Germanic shortening of *siu-ie/o- to PGmc. *siwjan has
been postulated (Rasmussen 1989, 118). However, it is unnecessary to
derive PGmc. *siwjan from the abnormally syllabified *sīu-ie/o- (< PIE
*sih,u-je/o-) that appears to lie behind Skt. sívyati. It is more appro-
priate to regard the Germanic verb as continuing PIE *sih,u-je/o-, the
regular reflex of PIE *sih,u- before *i, which lost its laryngeal and
yielded PGmc. *siwjan phonologically (PIE *sih,u-je/o-> *sių-je/o-
> PGmc. *siwjan).
8.2.2 The Gothic outcome of PGmc. *-ōwijV-
The Gothic outcome of PGmc. *-ōwij V- is fairly clear. Under
the interpretation that Gothic au represented a monophthong, a general
loss of medial *w after *ō can be assumed (Go. sauil [sɔ:il] n. ‘sun' <
PGmc. *sōwil-a- < PIE *seh₂u̟el-, Go. staua [stɔ:a] f. 'judgement' <
PGmc. *stōwo); the spellings ow and auw are not encountered in Gothic
words. As a result of this change, PGmc. *-ōwijV- yielded Go. -ōjV-,
Slavic presents. The Sanskrit and Germanic presents have chosen the
unmetathesized variant PIE *sih,u-.
9 The variant spelling widowo (L. 7, 12) for widuwo 'widow' is, of
course, insignificant. This wide formulation, a general loss of medial
*w after *ō, is not consistently stated in the standard handbooks (yet, cf.
Bethge in Dieter 1898-1900, 1:208). Of course, those who believe in
a diphthongal pronunciation of Go. au describe the development of
PGmc. *ōw to Go. [au] before vowels (Kieckers 1928, 21, 44). Others
discuss a loss of *w in the sequence *-ōwijV- or *-ōwV- (e.g., Brug-

=== page 192 ===
192
e.g., in weak verbs of Class I (PGmc. *stōwijan > Go. stōjan ‘to judge')
and ija-stems (PGmc. *tōwija-> Go. taui, gen. tōjis ‘work, deed').10
One possible choice for the relative chronology of the changes involved
is PGmc. *stōwijan> *stōwjan> Go. stōjan (loss of *w after *ō); this
order does not cause a conflict between Go. stōjan and sequences in
which *ō was prevocalic after w-loss and was lowered (PGmc. 3sg.
pret. *stöwiðē> *stō'iða > Go. stauida). An apparent argument for
the order *-ōwijV-> *-ō'ijV-> Go. -ōjV- comes from the sequence
*-ōwiji-. 2sg. pres. stōjis thus indicates the development *-ōwiji->
*-ō'ii-> Go. -ōji-; *stōwijiz> *stowīs would have yielded Go. *staueis

preserved *j, could easily have been generalized in the present para-

10 The development PGmc. *-ōwijV-> Go. -ōjV- is also assumed by

Go. stōjan is usually understood as deriving from the root PIE
*steh₂- 'to stand.' This etymology will be discussed in connection with
the cognate OHG stouwen 'to bring to a standstill, accuse' in section
8.4.2 below.
The etymology of Go. taujan and taui n. is not well established.
Feist (1909, 263-64) connected the Germanic words to Gk. (Dor.)
dãλoç ‘slave' (of uncertain origin), mentioning, e.g., Skt. dūras ‘far' and
Gk. Súw 'cause to sink' among less certain connections. Pokorny (1959,

OLat. duenos 'good.' None of these choices is quite convincing.

8.2.3 The Gothic outcome of PGmc. *-ēwijV-
The outcome of PGmc. *-ēwijV- in Gothic cannot be verified by
many examples, but *w appears to have been preserved after the vowel
*ē. Three verbs of the weak Class I suggest the outcome Go. -ēwjV-.
Go. lēwjan ‘to give over, betray' together with 3sg. pret. opt.
OHG fir-lāti, gi-lāti ‘betrayed' and OE lawan 'to betray' suggests the
presence of a verb PGmc. *lēwijan (Falk/Torp 1909, 364). Its etymo-
logical connections are not well established. Pokorny listed the verb
under a root PIE *lēu- 'to slacken' represented only in Germanic and
Balto-Slavic (Lith. liáujuos ‘cease,' etc.) with a further reference to
*(s)leu- ‘slack' (1959, 682). This connection was rejected by Feist
(1909, 179), who regarded PGmc. *lēw- as a lengthened grade of the
root PIE *leu- 'to cut off, separate, loosen' (Skt. lunáti 'cuts (off),' Gk.
λúw 'release,' etc.), which is thought to lie behind Go. fra-liusan ‘to
lose' (Pokorny 1959, 681-82). Feist's analysis is the more plausible of
the two, but the verb PGmc. *lēwijan cannot be derived directly from a
known Indo-European ancestor. It could be a denominative formation;
the noun Go. lēw n.(?) ‘opportunity' lends itself as the basis in spite of a
semantic difference.
The evidence of the verb Go. ga-tëwjan 'to appoint' (< PGmc.
*ga-tēwijan) for the Gothic outcome of PGmc. *-ēwijV- is limited, as
present stem forms are not attested (only pret. ptc. ga-tewips k 8,19,
nom. pl. ungatewidai th 3,7). On the other hand, the ija-stem adj.
taihun-tëweis ‘decimal' (dat. pl. taihuntewjam K 15,6) provides the

=== page 194 ===
194
information.11 The words derive from Go. tēwa f. ‘order,' which is
most likely to be an ē-grade cognate of Go. taujan ‘to do, make' and taui
n., gen. sg. tōjis ‘work, deed' (hesitantly, Feist 1909, 263-64; Pokorny
1959, 218), cf. section 8.2.2 above.12
The third verb, Go. skēwjan 'to go' (< PGmc. *skēwijan), does
not have a truly convincing etymology. It is traditionally equated with
the Old Norse verb skæva 'to go, hasten' (cf. section 8.3.3 below) and
further connected to EFr. schōjen, MDu. schooyen ‘to go, hurry' (with
a different ablaut grade) (De Vries 1961, 511). Some scholars have
assumed that the underlying root contained a (labio-)velar (as if from
PGmc. *skēhwijan(?)). Feist connected these to OHG (gi)-scehan, OE
scēon, OFr. skia 'to take place,' under a root PIE *skěk"-, cf. Av. subj.
sačāitē ‘that he go by,' OIr. scuchim 'go away' (1909, 237), but their
relationship is uncertain. Others have proposed roots in u-diphthongs
without offering very successful scenarios. Torp (Falk/Torp 1909,

466), e.g., suggested PIE *(s)kēu- : *sku- and compared the Germanic

8.2.4 The Gothic outcome of PGmc. *-īwijV- and *-ūwijV-
The Gothic outcome of PGmc. *-īwijV- is apparently unknown.
One might have expected the retention of *w after the front unrounded
vowel *ī, as seems to have been the case after *ē (in Go. -ēwjV-<
PGmc. *-ēwij V-). Gothic words with the spelling *-eiwjV- do not
occur (neither, e.g., Class I verbs of the type Go. *-eiwjan nor ija-stems
in Go. *-eiwi n. or Go. *-eiweis m.). 13 Words in other Germanic
languages which indicate preforms in PGmc. *-īwV- or *-īwijV- do not

*-īwij V- (Hirt 1896, 159-61; Braune 1973, 17).

The Gothic result of the sequence PGmc. *-ūwijV- is also un-

8.3.1 The Old Norse outcome
of PGmc. *-awjV- and *-iwjV-
According to standard views, the phonological outcome of PGmc.
*-awjV- was ON -eyjV-, as demonstrated by inf. heyja weak Cl. I 'to
perform, carry out' (< PGmc. *hawjan, cf. OE hēzan ‘to carry out,
complete') and hey n. ja-stem ‘hay' (with loss of the final syllable
< PGmc. *hawja-, cf. Go. hawi, OHG houwi/hewi, OE hiez ‘id.'). It is
indeed likely that PGmc. *-aw- became a diphthong *-au- before *j and
that this inherited *j caused the regular umlaut of *au to ON ey (cf.
Noreen 1970, 64). 16 The preterite of Class I verbs in PGmc. *-awjan
was ON 3sg. -áði (e.g., háði), which is commonly believed to be
phonologically derived from PGmc. *-awiðē, although opinions have
differed on the exact details.17
16 Noreen actually assumed that the Proto-Germanic structure was
*-auj-, presumably based on the Gothic structure -auj V- and ON -eyjV-
(1970, 142). The view represented here takes PGmc. *-awjV- to
EGmc. and NGmc. *-aujV-, cf. section 8.1.1 above, but both methods
result in a phonological ON -eyjV-.
17 Noreen assumed a loss of *i and a later change of *a-u to *ā (PGmc.
*-awiỗē> *-a-uðē> -áði) (1903, 64, 70; 1970, 77). By this method the
outcome -áði is phonological with no umlaut effect from the *i or the
*w. Pipping (1912, 142, 147, 167) proposed a rule by which *w was
lost, e.g., before *i which was later lost; he thus assumed the develop-
ment PGmc. *-awiðē> *-ā-iðē and an analogical lack of i-umlaut in
-áði.

forth,' Gk. σɛúw 'set in quick, violent motion.' PIE *ki̟ou-eje/o- yielded
PGmc. *hawjan by the Germanic rule of j-loss after initial consonants
(cf. section 8.2.1).18

The preterites in ON -éði to verbs in -ýja can be understood as
analogical. In Pipping's opinion (1912, 147), hléði resisted i-umlaut
through the influence of pret. ptc. *hlewiðr and the noun *hlewa-
(> ON hlé n. ‘protection'). A more plausible account was proposed by
Sturtevant (1931, 155-57), who claimed that the preterites hléði and
séði arose by analogy to the verb týja ‘to help.' This verb had the
preterite téði (as a byform beside týði, tøði), and the proportion is
obvious (týja : téði, hlýja : X; X = hléði).
It can be concluded that the phonological outcome of PGmc. inf.
*-iwjan is ON -ýja (ON *sýja), and the corresponding preterite *-iwiðē
results in ON -íði (ON *síði). The same result is expected in verbs
which derive from an earlier e-grade (PGmc. *-iwjan< *-ewjan, in ON
knýja, pret. kníði). There is no reason to reconstruct inf. *-ewjan, pret.
*-ewidē in cases where the preterite is -éði (séði, hléði), as such forms
are analogical.25
8.3.2 The Old Norse outcome of PGmc. *-ōwijV-
The phonological outcome in Old Norse of the sequence PGmc.
*-ōwijV- is not known with certainty. So much is clear that PGmc.
*-ōwijV- did not turn out like structures of PGmc. *-ōCijV-; in that
case, the retention of *w, the syncope of *-ij-, and i-umlaut of the root
vowel would have been expected, giving a long-stem verb of Class I in
25 A preform *hlewjan does not necessarily follow from the assump-
tion that hlýja is a denominative of *hlewa-; a denominative verb would
presumably have conformed to the pattern *-iwjan, *-iwiðē.

=== page 202 ===
202
ON inf. *-øva (cf. ON døma 'to judge' < PGmc. *dōmijan). There do
not appear to be examples of this treatment. By contrast, a loss of *w
in this environment is predicted by the well-known Proto-Norse rule of
loss of *w after *ō (e.g., ON flóa ‘to flow' < PGmc. *flōwan, cf.
Noreen 1970, 170). According to this rule, the expected outcome of
PGmc. *-ōwijV- is ON *-øjV- with *-ij- triggering the usual umlaut of
*ō to ON 0.26
Weak verbs of Class I might, therefore, be expected to appear
with infinitives in *-øja and (since the long-stem verbs of this class
have preterites with i-umlaut) with preterites in 3sg. *-øði (< PGmc.
*-ōwiðē, cf. ON dømði ‘judged' < PGmc. *dōmiðē). Examples of the
sequence PGmc. *-ōwijV-are not abundant in Old Norse, and no verb
is attested with the unambiguous pattern inf. -øja, pret. -øði. Scattered
preterite forms in -øði only occur as variants beside other preterite
types and seem to belong to verbs with infinitives in -ýja (hløði from
hlýja 'to make warm, protect,' søði from sýja ‘to sew'); the verbs flýja
'to flee' and týja 'to help' have variants with ø and ý in their preterite
and present forms (cf. Noreen 1970, 329).
An exceptional umlaut rule was formulated early this century to
account for related forms with ø and ý on the surface and an alleged
underlying PGmc. *ō. The rule states that *ō became ON ý before *j
instead of yielding ø, the usual outcome of i-umlaut. This is somewhat
surprising, as there is no difference between the effect of *j and *i on

*-ōwjV-> *-ōjV-> ON *-øjV-.

other vowels. 27 In fact, the examples provided in support of the rule
are open to suspicion.
1. The rule supposedly accounted for the variant forms of the

believed to have arisen in gen. sg. býjar. It is doubtful, however, that
the underlying root vowel of this derivative of PGmc. *būjan ‘to live'
was PGmc. *ō and not *ū.28

being equated with Go. frawrōhjan 'to accuse, suspect,' cf. Go. wrōhs
'complaint, accusation.' This etymology is uncertain. ON røgja ‘to
accuse, slander' continues a cognate form with the voiced fricative
variant produced by Verner's Law (< PGmc. *wrōyijan); it is im-

addition, there is little evidence for the prefix *fra- in Old Norse. 29 As

cognate of Go. taujan ‘to do, make,' cf. also Go. taui n., gen. sg. tōjis
‘work, deed' (< PGmc. *tōwija-) (Feist 1909, 263; Pokorny 1959, 218).
The verb tøja could therefore be interpreted as PGmc. *tōwijan, a
denominative formation of PGmc. *tōwija- (cf. Psilander 1913, 282,
who assumed the original meaning "ausrichten" and took tøja to be a
Nordic creation; Pokorny loc. cit.).

the j-umlaut rule described above, the vowel ý must have been extended
analogically from forms with *j (e.g., the phonological inf. týja), and
the infinitive tøja must be analogical to forms with phonological ø (2sg.
pres. tør < *tōwīz, 3sg. pret. tøði < *tōwiðē), cf. Noreen 1970, 329.
To complicate the picture, this verb seems to have been confused
with the verb tjóa 'to suffice, help' (< PGmc. *tiuhan 'to draw,' cf. Go.
tiuhan, OE tëon, OHG ziohan, OS tiohan; Lat. dūcō ‘lead,' etc.). ON
tjóa formerly belonged to the strong Class II (pres. 1sg. tjó, 2,3sg. týr,
etc., pret. ptc. toginn), but its preterite was replaced by the weak pret-
erite týði/tøði (as well as the forms tjóaði and tjóði, cf. Noreen 1970,
329).37 It is thus quite plausible that the forms with ý of the weak verb
týja/tója derive from the strong present of tjóa, i.e., that 2,3sg. týr gave
rise to the j-present forms inf. týja, etc., and subsequently the preterite
týði. By this view (cf. Sturtevant 1930, 62-63), the infinitive týja does
not constitute an example of the j-umlaut *ō> ý; moreover, tøja can be
understood as the phonological outcome of PGmc. *tōwijan.38
It emerges that all the proposed examples of the change *ō> ý
by j-umlaut are to some extent suspect. Although the material hardly
allows firm judgements on the development of PGmc. *-ōwijV-, it is
preferable to regard ON -øjV- (with loss of *w and the regular i-
umlaut effect, e.g., inf. tøja) as the most plausible result.

8.3.3 The Old Norse outcome of PGmc. *-ēwijV-
The phonological result of the sequence PGmc. *-ēwij V- in Old
Norse is not illustrated by many examples, but the evidence seems to
favor the outcome ON -ævV-, with the retention of *w and the expected
vowel changes of PGmc. *ē to NGmc. *ā with umlaut to ON æ. The
result thus matches the treatment of sequences of PGmc. *-ēCijV-, as in
the verb ON mæra ‘to praise' (< PGmc. *mērijan, cf. Go. mērjan 'to
proclaim').
Among examples of this development is the poetic verb ON skæva
'to go, hasten' (< PGmc. *skēwijan, equated with Go. skēwjan 'to go').
The verb has been transferred to the weak Class II in Old Norse, but
semantically the equation is well-founded.39 From the verb are derived
skævaðr m., a poetic word for 'horse,' and the poetic agent noun skævir
m. 'one who sets in motion'; skær m. ‘horse' is also a possible cognate
(perhaps < *skāwi- < PGmc. *skēwi-, cf. Magnússon 1989, 883). OSw.
nat-skæva f. ‘bat' belongs in the group as well (*skāwijōn-, Pipping
1912, 154).
According to Pipping's beliefs of the loss of *w in Proto-Norse,
the expected outcome of PGmc. *skēwijan would be *skæa, but *w
would have been preserved in forms which preserved a following *i
(2,3sg. pres. *skævir, pret. ptc. *skæviðr), from which it could have
been reinserted into the infinitive (Pipping 1912, 169). The assumption
39 The transfer to Class II was probably caused by the influence of such
Class II verbs as ON fræva ‘to fertilize.'

=== page 209 ===
209
of w-loss, e.g., before *i which was later lost, allowed him to derive the
nouns skær and skævir from one ija-stem paradigm. He expected the
phonological outcome nom. sg. *skāwijar > Olcel. skævir, but assumed
that the loss of *w in the nom. pl. *skāwijōr (> *skā-ijōr > OIcel.
*skæar) could have created a new nominative singular without *w
(nom. sg. skær) (op. cit., 148). Pipping also predicted w-loss in the
feminine ōn-stem OSw. nat-skæva (*skāwijōn-> *skā-ijōn-) and had to
assume analogical restoration of the *w (op. cit., 154).
Pipping's view of the development of PGmc. *skēwijan seems
counter-intuitive (PGmc. *skēwijan > NGmc. *skāwijan with loss of
*w to *skā'ijan and i-umlaut and syncope to a hiatus form *skæa
[ske:a]). Of course, long-stem verbs of Class I with consonant-final
roots lost *-ij- (NGmc. *mārijan > ON mæra), but it is by no means
certain that *-ij- would have been lost after a vowel (*skā'ijan > *skæa).
If *w had been lost at all, one would rather have expected inf. *skæja
(cf. tøja < PGmc. *tōwijan). The conditions of Pipping's w-loss are
also implausible. If *w was lost before syncope and a following *i pro-
vided the environment, it needs justification why only an "unaccented"
(i.e., later syncopated) *i triggered the loss (inf. *skāwijan> *skā'ijan),
but not an *i which was retained (nom. sg. *skāwijar > *skāwīr >
skævir).40
It was assumed above in the case of the sequence PGmc. *-ōwij V-
that the preceding vowel had conditioned the loss of *w. The rule
40 In fact, Pipping had an additional method of losing the *w of
*skāwijan, in the event that it had been protected from being lost before
the unaccented *i, i.e., by a rule which operated after syncope and
eliminated intervocalic *w before nasalized vowels (op. cit., 168-69).

=== page 210 ===
210
which eliminated *w after the rounded *ō (PGmc. *tōwijan> tøja,
*flōwan > flóa) is phonetically more plausible than w-loss triggered by
a preceding low unrounded *ā or the front unrounded PGmc. *ē. In
other words, PGmc. *tōwijan is a priori more likely to lose *w than
PGmc. *skēwijan or NGmc. *skāwijan, and it is a plausible assumption
that inf. skæva illustrates the phonological result of PGmc. *-ēwij V-.41
8.3.4 The Old Norse outcome of PGmc. *-īwij V-
Unambiguous examples of the outcome of the sequence PGmc.
*-īwijV- are scarce in Old Norse; as became clear in section 8.2.4,
Gothic does not provide material for comparison. According to the
standard opinion, *w triggered umlaut of an immediately preceding *ī
to ON ý, e.g., in ON blý n. ‘lead' (< PGmc. *blīwa-, cf. OHG blīo ‘id.')
and the name of the god Týr m. (< PGmc. *Tīwa-z, cf. OHG ZĨo, OE
Tīw).42 Similarly, the outcome of PGmc. *-īwijV- has been predicted
to be ON -ýjV-.

Gothic verbs in -ēwjan, it is not the only possible example of the

< *glaiwa-, Magnússon 1989, 261).

45 It is tempting to connect frýja to the root PIE *perk-, *prek- ‘to ask,”

8.3.5 The Old Norse outcome of PGmc. *-ūwijV-
The sequence PGmc. *-ūwijV- meets the conditions of Ver-
schärfung, and one would expect *-йww(i)jV- to yield ON *-yggv(V)-
with the usual umlaut effect of the j-suffix. Examples of this develop-
ment should thus be Verschärfung cases with genuine u-vocalism.
The verb ON byggva 'to inhabit' is most likely to be a causative
PIE *bhuh,-eje/o-, which gave *bŭwijan and, by an analogical length-
ening of *ŭ, PGmc. *būwijan yielded *buww(i)jan (cf. section 7.1.2).
The verb *bū'an was available to provide the lengthening, and ON
byggva thus provides an actual example of the development of PGmc.
*-ūwij V-.
ON skyggva 'to overshadow, polish' (< *skuww(i)jan) is not
likely to have developed entirely phonologically from a Proto-Indo-
European preform. The verb is a denominative formation of the noun
49 This analogical channel created the infinitive ON spýja ‘to spit' by
the influence of 2,3sg. spýr (< *spīwir). This verb did not have an
original j-present, however, cf. Go. speiwan, OE, OHG spīwan ‘id.'
(Noreen 1970, 329).

=== page 216 ===
216
*skuwwan- (Go. skuggwa 'mirror,' ON skuggi ‘shadow'). As sequences
of PIE *Cūh, V- cannot be established for the proto-language, Germanic
forms with the Verschärfung output *Cuww- and an original u-vocalism
must be analogical. It has thus been suggested that the word *skuwwan-
adopted the geminate *-ww- from the verb *skawwōn 'to look' (OHG
scouwōn) (Jasanoff 1978a, 86). By a different view, Rasmussen (1990,
440) found a locus for Verschärfung within the original paradigm of
*skuwwan-. He assumed that *skuww-was the result of paradigmatic
analogy, a blend of nom. sg. PIE *skéµh,-ōn> *skeww-ōn and loc. sg.
*skuh,-én-i > *skuw-én-i. It is therefore very unlikely that there ever
existed a verb PGmc. *skūwijan. The verb ON skyggva was probably
created within Germanic from the noun *skuwwan-, which had an
analogical geminate, not a sequence *skūw-.
8.4 Old High German
8.4.1 The Old High German outcome
of PGmc. *-awjV- and*-iwjV-
It is widely recognized that the semivowel *w participated in the
West Germanic gemination of consonants before *j. The structures
PGmc. *-awjV- and *-iwjV- (the latter from *-ewjV- and *-iwjV-)
yielded *-awwjV- and *-iwwjV-, respectively, and became a second
source of *-ww- beside the Proto-Germanic geminate *-ww- which had

=== page 217 ===
217
arisen in Verschärfung environments. u-diphthongs were formed with
the preceding vowels, and Old High German sequences of -auw- (later
-ouw-) and -iuw- emerged. Examples of Verschärfung cases are the
verb OHG scouwōn ‘to look' (< *skawwō- < PIE *skou̟h,-eh₂ie/o-, cf.
OE scēawian) and the adjective OHG triuwi 'true' (< *triwwija- < PIE
*dreuh,-ijo-, cf. Go. triggws, Olcel. tryggr).50
The development PGmc. *-awjV- through *-awwjV- to OHG
-auwV- (later -ouwV-) does not show the effects of umlaut caused by
*j. Thus, weak verbs of Class I indicate the development PGmc.
*-awjan > *-awwjan > OHG -auwen (-ouwen), e.g., OHG frauwen
(frouwen) 'to make glad, be glad.' Beside such forms there are variants
in -ewen (frewen), to a certain extent dialectally distributed: the type
frauwen (frouwen) is mainly Bavarian and forms with -ew- are the
more common type in Alemannic and Franconian (cf. Schatz 1907, 154;
Braune 1987, 291). The traditional explanation of these variants, which
is very likely correct, attributes them to a paradigmatic alternation of
forms with phonological i-umlaut before single *w (*-awi- > -ewi-)
and forms with gemination before *j but no umlaut effect (*-awj-
> *-awwj-> -auw-). The dialects are then assumed to have favored
different levelings; the present indicative paradigm had the following
phonological forms (cf. Kögel 1884b, 528):
1sg. *frawwju
2sg. *frawis
3sg. *frawit
A
^
frouwu
frewis
frewit, etc.
50 Cf. Schatz 1907, 96; 1927, 190-93; Braune 1987, 106-9.

=== page 218 ===
218
ja-stem nouns show analogous variant forms with -ouw- and -ew-,
which are also dialectally distributed and explained from a phonological
paradigm as follows:
nom. sg. *gawi >
gewin. 'district'
gen. sg. *gawwjes >
gouwes, etc.
This lack of umlaut in geminated forms is somewhat surprising in
view of the fact that words of the same inflectional types have gemi-
nated consonants and umlaut of *a to e throughout the paradigms (e.g.,
sezzen 'to place' < *satjan, gi-fremmen 'to promote' < *-framjan, nezzi
'net' < *natja). Some scholars have therefore wished to separate the
development of *-wj- from the West Germanic gemination. Thus,
Darms (1978, 461) claimed that the lack of umlaut could only be ex-
plained by assuming that *-wj- had been assimilated to *-ww-before the
West Germanic gemination, and hence before the rule of i-umlaut.51
However, Darms' argument does not carry much weight, for West
Germanic gemination of *-awj- to *-awwj- can be assumed without
claiming that the geminate *-ww- actually prevented umlaut. In all
probability, *-awwj- had become pre-OHG *-auwj- before umlaut, and
thus it is easily understandable why an early umlaut of *a to e did not
take place. The lack of "secondary umlaut" would rather have to be
explained as a failure of the sequence *-auw- to undergo umlaut.
The sequence PGmc. *-iwjV-, likewise, underwent gemination
51 Similarly, Dahl (1938, 107-9) had assumed a gradual transition of *j
to *w after the u-element of the diphthong (gen. sg. of neuter ja-stems:
*-aujes> *-auwes).

=== page 219 ===
219
(> *-iwwjV-) and yielded OHG -iuwV-. The adjective OHG niuwi
‘new' < PGmc. *niwja- (an original e-grade, PIE *neujo-) demonstrates
that *-iw-before *j merged with the outcome of PGmc. *-iww- (OHG
triuwi ‘true,' cf. Go. triggws). Accordingly, the j-present PGmc.
*siwjan (cf. Go. 3sg. pres. siujiþ) has the form OHG siuwen 'to sew'; as
expected, there is no gemination in the preterite 3sg. siwita (< PGmc.
*siwiðē).
8.4.2 The Old High German outcome of PGmc. *-ōwijV-
Discussion of the Old High German outcome of the sequence
PGmc. *-ōwijV- necessarily involves an evaluation of the Old High
German cognate of Go. stōjan 'to judge' (< PGmc. *stōwijan), i.e.,
OHG stouwen 'to bring to a standstill, accuse,' which is complicated by
the presence of forms with the root vocalism u(o) and a different
meaning, OHG stu(o)en 'to atone, be punished.'
It was suggested early that the loss of the *w of PGmc. *-ōwijV-
found in East and North Germanic (cf. Go. stōjan, ON tøja) was a
Proto-Germanic change. Thus, Mahlow (1879, 30) claimed that *w was
lost before *j in Proto-Germanic unless preceded by a short vowel, a
rule which included a change of PGmc. *-ōwj- to PGmc. *-ōj-. It was
accepted by Kögel (1884a, 510, n. 1, 513-15), who, wishing to demon-
strate that PGmc. *-ōw- became *-au- before vowels within Proto-
Germanic, derived the following pre-Old High German present para-
digm:

=== page 220 ===
inf.
*stōwjan

*stōwju

3sg. *stōw(j)iþ
V V V V

*stōjan

stau(u)it, etc.
Kögel assumed that the verb stauuen (stouwen) had generalized the
vocalism au and that the type stuowen (sic) derived from forms with *ō,
to which *w had been secondarily extended from those forms of the
paradigm which had retained *w (e.g., 2sg. *stōwis; hence, a secondary
inf. *stōwjan).52
Needless to say, the spelling au before vowels in Gothic (Go.
1sg. pret. stauida < *stōwiðō) does not provide evidence for a Proto-
Germanic change of *-ōw- to *-au-. In addition, the claim that PGmc.
*-ōw- yielded OHG -au- before vowels is contradicted by such examples
as OHG rōa, ruowa f. ‘rest' (< PGmc. *rōwō, Kluge 1989, 609). As for
the position before *j, the postulated change PGmc. *-ōwij-> *-ōwj-
> *-ōj- is undermined by the verb stouwen itself. Had the change
PGmc. *-ōwij-> *-ōwj-> *-ōj-, in fact, been Proto-Germanic, the verb
PGmc. *stōwijan would have become *stōjan. A verb of this structure
would be most likely to merge completely with the established group of
verba pura in Old High German, which acquired secondary j-presents
52 Kögel assumed that the Class I verb stouwen had variant forms of
the type stuowen. The forms in question are inf. stuouuan 'corripere'
Gl. II, 165, 36 and imperat. stuouo ‘dimica' Gl. II, 492, 14; these are by
Raven (1963-67, 2:148-49) assigned to a different verb, i.e., stouuon
Cl. II. Kögel rightly claimed that forms meaning 'to atone' belonged to
a different verb, in his opinion stūēn Cl. III (or stūōn Cl. II, because of
preterite forms of the type 3sg. -stuota).

(e.g., OHG bluoen 'to bloom' < *blōjan). The preform *stōjan would
make it quite remarkable that stouwen shows any forms of the stem
stouw- at all.
Mahlow's Proto-Germanic rule *-ōwj-> *-ōj- was immediately
refuted by Paul (1880, 157, 160), who assumed that the sequence had
the form *-ōwijV- in Proto-Germanic, and that *-ōwjV- did not arise
until the dialects were separated. Paul, on the other hand, believed that
a change of *-ōwj- to *-ōj- took place independently in Gothic and Old
High German. His Old High German evidence consisted of the verbs
*stōwijan> *stuojen > OHG stuen and *mōwijan> *muojen> OHG
muen 'to trouble.' The latter example can be ignored, as the verb is
more likely to be a verbum purum PGmc. *mōjan without an etymo-
logical *w.53 As for the verb *stōwijan, Paul assumed that forms of the
type stouwen were created on the model of the preterite *stōwida,
which, in his opinion, had the phonological outcome *stōuda > *stouda
> stouta; Kögel's change of *-ōw- to -au- before vowels was thus side-
stepped.
Van Helten (1891, 485-86) did not accept any loss of postvocalic
*w before *j in Old High German. He derived forms with -uow- from
preforms with *-ōwi-, but the variants with -ouw- by a phonological
53 The Gothic preterite participle af-dauips 'oppressed' of Go. *-dōjan
(< *-dōwijan, a causative of the root of PGmc. *dawjan, cf. ON deyja

presupposed a verb Go. *-mōjan (< *-mōwijan) and the same origin of
the cognate OHG muoen (Meyer 1869, 388; Paul 1880, 156). Nothing
prevents the interpretation that Go. af-mauips belonged to a verbum
purum Go. *-mauan < PGmc. *-mōjan (cf. section 3.1.1).

development from *-ōwj-. He proposed that the treatment of *-ōwj-
was analogous to that of *-wj- after *a and *i, i.e., *-ōwj-> *-ōuwj-
> *-ouw(j)-. Later, van Helten retracted the idea that *-ōwj- could
have yielded *-ouw(j)- with the argument that *-ōwij- had not become
*-ōwj- at the time of the West Germanic gemination (1893, 564-65).
He then preferred to regard -uow- as the phonological outcome of
*-ōwj- and interpret forms with -ouw- as analogical to verbs with
phonological gemination (*frawi-: *frawwjan, *stōwi- : X; X =
*stōwwjan).
Of these competing theories of the phonological outcome of
PGmc. *-ōwij V- in Old High German, the ones that predict the vocal-
ism *-u(o)- are not based on firm evidence from the verb stouwen.
First of all, the forms spelled with -u(o)- and meaning 'to atone,' rather
than 'to accuse,' belong to a separate verb (stūēn(?) stūōn(?) (Kögel
1884a, 514), stu(o)en Cl. I (Raven 1963-67, 1:210)). Second, forms of
stouwen with -uo- seem to have adopted this vocalism from yet another
verb, stouwōn/stuowōn Cl. II ‘to accuse, blame, attack' (listed in Raven
1963-67, 2:148-49). A verb PGmc. *stōwōn would have had the
phonological outcome OHG stuowōn (cf. ruowa f. 'rest' < PGmc.
*rōwō); byforms with stouw- of this verb are most easily explained
(with van Helten 1893, 565) as analogical to the Class I verb stouwen.
The evidence works in favor of the assumption that PGmc.
*-ōwij V- became OHG -ouw-. Van Helten's argument for rejecting this
idea, namely, the belief that the West Germanic gemination before *j
operated before the change of *-ij- to *-j-, does not contradict the claim
that PGmc. *-ōwij V- developed a geminate *-ww-. Consonant gemi-
nation in a long syllable appears to be limited to Old High German

=== page 223 ===
223
(mostly early Upper German) (cf. Braune 1987, 94-95), and it is
perfectly plausible that *-ōwj- (< PGmc. *-ōwij-) was geminated to
*-ōwwj- by a strictly pre-Old High German change. 54 To get to the
outcome -ouw- one could, in theory, either accept van Helten's diph-
thong shortening *-ōuwj-> *-ouw(j)- or, perhaps rather, assume a
vowel shortening which led to an early merger with forms with PGmc.
*-aw(w)j- (PGmc. *-ōwij-> *-ōwj-> *-ōwwj-> *-awwj-> -auw-
> -ouw-). Neither method predicts that every form of the present
paradigm of PGmc. *stōwijan developed phonologically to stouw-. The
outcome stuow- is expected in those forms which had lost *j before *i
(e.g., 2sg. pres. *stōwis> *stuowis), 55 but -ouw- has been leveled
through the present paradigm and extended to the preterite (3sg. pres.
stovuit, 3sg. pret. stouvita; forms from Raven 1963-67, 1:323).56
As the surface structure of OHG stouwen is identical to that of the
verbs from PGmc. *-awjan (e.g., frouwen 'to make glad, be glad'),
some scholars have chosen to place stouwen in that group and recon-

Since this verb is crucial for the evaluation of the Old High German
outcome of PGmc. *-ōwijV-, it must be investigated whether its
historical background allows a reconstruction with PGmc. *-awjV-.
The Gothic cognate stōjan ‘to judge' (< PGmc. *stōwijan) has
traditionally been derived from the root PIE *steh₂- 'to stand' with a ụ-
extension (< PIE *stohu̟-eje/o-, cf. Jasanoff 1978a, 79). Some scholars
have seen it as a causative formation comparable to OCS staviti ‘to
place,' as if the original meaning 'to make stand' had developed to the
meaning 'to judge' of stōjan and such divergent meanings as 'to stop,
bring to a standstill; reproach, accuse' of OHG stouwen (Kluge 1963,
741). Others have interpreted the verb as a denominative formation
from the noun PGmc. *stōwō f. ‘place' (< PIE *stoh₂u̟-eh₂) of OE stōw
f. 'place,' ON eld-stó f. 'fireplace.' Under this explanation the corre-
sponding noun in Gothic, staua f. 'judgement,' has been understood as
'court' (Falk/Torp 1909, 493; Feist 1909, 249). There is little doubt
that the noun meaning 'place,' viz., OE stōw, ON -stó f., is from this
root, as well as the verb OE stōwian 'to hold back' (< *stōwōjan, a
denominative verb of the productive Class II). The meaning 'to bring
to a standstill' of OHG stouwen is also easily connected as a denomina-
tive PGmc. *stōwijan. This etymology makes the reconstruction
*stawjan, which lacks direct cognates, highly implausible. Such a
formation would presuppose an inconvenient preform, PIE *sth₂u-eie/o-
or the like; the zero grade would not fit the presumed causative mean-
57 Schatz (1927, 32) seems to have assumed a preform PGmc.
*stawwjan, as if this were a case of Verschärfung.

=== page 225 ===
225
ing, and the development of PIE *sth₂u̟-eje/o- to PGmc. *stawji/a-
would be wholly unparalleled.
On the other hand, the meanings 'to judge' of Go. stōjan and 'to
accuse' of OHG stouwen are difficult to reconcile with the root 'to
stand,' especially since the noun Go. staua seems primarily to mean
‘judgement, court case,' rather than the site where the event took place.
This was pointed out by Gusmani (1978, 225-36), who for semantic
reasons preferred to connect these to the root PIE *steu- 'to praise
aloud' (Pokorny 1959, 1035).58 This root formed a present with
"Narten"-type ablaut (3sg. *stéy-ti, 3pl. *stéu̟-nti, cf. Skt. stáuti
'praises,' Gk. σteta 'promises (to do), boasts'), to which it is easy to
connect the lengthened grade *stōu- of the Germanic words. Gusmani
suggested that *stōų- might either be gotten from the lengthened-grade
perfect which goes with presents of this type or from an ā-stem action
noun (cf. Go. staua). The latter possibility is preferable; the verb
*stōwijan seems to be limited to Germanic, and the noun which for
formal and semantic reasons could serve as the basis is attested in that
branch.59 This etymology also makes the reconstruction *stawjan for
OHG stuowen a less plausible alternative than *stōwijan.60

8.4.3 Old High German verbs in -ouwen/-ewen
1. bouwen/bewen 'to use frequently, wear out by use, exhaust;
make' does not have a good etymology. It has been equated with Go.
bauan ‘to live' (Kögel 1884a, 515-16), which is formally impossible,
and also mistakenly confused with the verbum purum OHG bāen 'to
warm' (Graff 1834-42, 3:4; Raven 1963-67, 1:5, 285).61 An additional
1978, 1-13). However, a denominative formation is probably the most
realistic choice.
60 It goes without saying that OHG stu(o)en (stūēn(?)) ‘to atone, be
punished' belongs to this root and not to the root 'to stand.' From a
formal point of view, it might continue an Indo-European zero grade
(cf. the zero grade of Hitt. ištuwāri ‘becomes manifest'). It has also
been suggested that it was a late creation, a denominative formed from
the noun OHG *stūa (in the compound stūatago ‘last judgement'), which
was derived, in part analogically, from PGmc. *stōwō (van Helten
1893, 565).
61 Kögel seems to have wished to unite Go. bauan and OHG bouwen

suggested cognate is OE bīewan, bỹwan, bēowan ‘to rub, polish, adorn,'

tenuously connected to ON bygg n. ‘barley, grain' under a root PGmc.
*beww- ‘to rub,' which does not have known cognates in other branches
(Seebold 1970, 126). Conversely, Lloyd and Springer (loc. cit.)
assumed that the basic meaning of the verbs was 'to prepare,' which

prepare, adorn.' This etymology does not make it clear whether the
Proto-Germanic preform of OHG bouwen was *bōwijan or

the causative OCS izbaviti 'to save, set free' (< PIE *bhōuh,-). In
theory, one could take OHG bouwen from a lengthened-grade causative-

formation in Old English, however, would be difficult to explain, as
would the relationship of *bawwjan to *bewwjan (with e-grade). The
comparison of OHG bouwen with the Old English forms thus does not
give a satisfactory account of the verb.
