4. flouwen/flewen 'to rinse' is traditionally derived from the root
PIE *pleų- 'to run, flow, swim, etc.' It is interpreted as a causative and
equated with the o-grade causatives Skt. plaváyati ‘causes to swim, over-
flows' and Serb. ploviti 'to float, swim' as continuing PIE *ploy-éi̟e/o-
> PGmc. *flawjan (Pokorny 1959, 835-37; Meid 1971, 88). Germanic
cognates built with this ablaut grade are attested, e.g., ON fley n. ‘ship'
(< PGmc. *flawja- < PIE *plou̟-ijo-, cf. Gk. ñλoĩov ‘floating vessel'),
but a causative PGmc. *flawjan is, to the best of my knowledge, not
supported by cognate verbs in the other Germanic languages. On the
other hand, the root PIE *pley- is likely to have formed a "Narten"
present (PIE 3sg. *pléy-ti, 3pl. *pléu̟-nti),64 which is properly matched
by a causative with a lengthened ō-grade, PIE *plóu̟-ie/o- (cf. the ō-
grade causatives Skt. plāváyati ‘causes to swim,' OCS plaviti ‘to cause to
swim, float,' Lith. pláuju, pláuti ‘to wash, rinse').65 The ablaut grade
PIE *plōu- is well represented in Germanic; it lies, e.g., behind the
verb *flōwan (ON flóa, OE flōwan 'to flow'). Although it is possible
64 Evidence for this ablaut pattern is found in Toch. B 3sg. plyewäm
'floats' implying a preform PIE *plēų-ti.
65 Klingenschmitt (1978, 6), who discovered the connection between
"Narten" presents and ō-grade causatives, did not consider the Slavic
verb to be directly descended from this Indo-European type. Skt.
plāváyati might be derived from PIE *ploụ- with a later lengthening
explained by Brugmann's Law.

=== page 230 ===
230
that Old High German created a verb *flawjan independently, it is quite
likely that OHG flouwen continues PGmc. *flōwijan < PIE *ploy-ie/o-.
5. fouwen/fewen 'to sift.' This verb has been classified with the
root PIE *peu-, *peu̟ǝ-, *pй- ‘to clean, purify, sift,' cf. Skt. punáti
'cleans,' Lat. pūrus 'clean.' The German verb has then been equated
with Skt. paváyati 'cleans, purifies' under the reconstruction *fawjan
(Pokorny 1959, 827). A genuine causative-iterative of a set root, PIE
*pou̟h,-ei̟e/o-, would be expected to yield Verschärfung, hence, PGmc.
*fawwjan. That origin is, however, possible. Verbs with Verschärfung
could easily lose the geminate *ww by analogy to verbs with the West
Germanic gemination of *w before *j: 1sg. pres. ind. *prawwju: 2sg.
*prawis, 1sg. *fawwju : X; X = 2sg. *fawis. This verb does not have
cognates which recommend a reconstruction PGmc. *fōwijan.
6. frouwen/frewen 'to be glad, rejoice, make glad' was created
as a factitive verb from the adjective OHG frao, frō ‘glad,' OS frā ‘id.'
(< PGmc. *frawa-) (Kluge 1989, 232) and can without hesitation be
reconstructed as PGmc. *frawjan. This reconstruction gets support
from North Germanic; ON adj. frár ‘quick, merry' (< PGmc. *frawa-,
cf. the Runic Norse name frawaradar (Magnússon 1989, 205)). Further
connections are uncertain. The adjective PGmc. *frawa- has been con-
nected to Skt. právate ‘jumps up, leaps, hastens' (root PIE *preu-, cf.
Pokorny 1959, 845-46), but it could also be related to the family of Go.
frauja ‘lord,' Skt. pūrva- ‘the first, the earlier,' etc. (Falk/Torp 1909,
233).
7. gikewen 'to call, name' is equated with OE cī(e)zan 'to call'
and reconstructed as PGmc. *kawjan (Falk/Torp 1909, 45; Pokorny
1959, 403). Among further cognates are Skt. gavate ‘sounds,' Gk.

yó(F)oç 'groaning,' Lith. gaujà ‘pack of dogs, wolves' (Pokorny's root
PIE *gỗu-, *goчǝ-, *gū- ‘to call, cry' (1959, 403)).
Although it is not generally recognized, this root is also
represented in Old Norse in the infinitive køja (hapax) 'to call,
disturb(?),' which indicates a preform PGmc. *kōwijan (cf. tøja

OHG gikewen derives from an ō-grade as well. It is, however, con-
ceivable that the form køja is a variant spelling for *keyja, 67 which
would unite OHG gikewen, OE cī(e)zan, and ON køja under the
reconstruction PGmċ. *kawjan.

10. strouwen/strewen ‘to strew' is connected to Lat. struō

12. zouwen/(zewen) 'to prepare' is a cognate of Go. taujan,
Runic Norse 1sg. pret. tawido, and usually reconstructed accordingly as
having an o-grade PGmc. *tawjan (e.g., Pokorny 1959, 218). The

preferring an equation of OHG zouwen with ON tøja 'to help' to an
equation with Go. taujan 'to do, make.'
None of the above verbs can with absolute certainty be recon-

stouwen (< PGmc. *stōwijan), the best candidate is probably flouwen; if
derived directly from an Indo-European causative, it should continue
PGmc. *flōwijan (< PIE *ploy-ie/o-). Unlike stouwen, of which no
spellings with *stew- are attested, the verb flouwen has forms with
-ew-.73 These cannot be phonological from the sequence *flōwi-, but
can easily have arisen by analogy. A late direct influence from the
verbs of PGmc. *-awjan (e.g., frouwen/frewen) is one possibility. It is
perhaps more likely that the analogy took place earlier. When the verb

had reached the stage *flawwjan (< *flōwwjan < PGmc. *flōwijan), the
vowel *a was generalized and the relevant forms acquired phonological

It has thus been shown that Old High German may have merged
under the sequence -ouw- the outcomes of PGmc. *-ōwija- (*stōwijan,
*flōwijan) and PGmc. *-awja- (e.g., *strawjan).
8.4.4 The Old High German outcome of PGmc. *-ēwijV-
Evidence for the development of PGmc. *-ēwijV- in Old High
German is not abundant, but it is at least clear that the treatment of
PGmc. *-ōwij V- just described, namely, gemination and vowel short-
ening (*-VwijV-> *-ÑwwjV-> *-VwwjV-), cannot be demonstrated
for the sequence *-ēwij V-.
One of the Gothic examples of this structure, Go. lēwjan ‘to
betray,' has a cognate in Old High German. Unfortunately, no present
stem forms of the verb are attested, only the forms 3sg. pret. opt. firlati
and gilati (Raven 1963-67, 1:101). These are said to presuppose an
infinitive OHG *-lā(w)en 'to betray'; the loss of *w after long vowels
was sporadic in intervocalic position but regular before consonants, cf.
the verb hiwen, pret. hīta 'to marry' (Baesecke 1918, 134-35, 229;
Braune 1987, 105).
74 In van Helten's description of the phonological development, *-ōwj-
*-ōuwj-> -ouw- (1891, 486), there never was a stage with the vowel
*a; in this scenario the forms in -ew- would have to be directly taken
over from the verbs in PGmc. *-awjan.

=== page 236 ===
236
From a strictly formal point of view, the same phonology might
apply to the verb OHG klauuen ‘to scratch, itch' (Raven 1963-67, 1:92),
if it is to be read as *klāwen (< PGmc. *klēwijan) and not as *klouwen
(*klewen), the equivalent of ON kleyja (< PGmc. *klawjan). 75 The
existence of the North Germanic cognate and of MHG klöuwen 'to
scratch' makes it impossible to claim that klauuen is an unequivocal
example of the outcome of PGmc. *-ēwijan.
On the basis of the Old High German preterite form -lāti and
the cognate Go. lēwjan, it appears simplest to assume that the sequence
*-ēwij V-preserved *w into Old High German and that the vowel
PGmc. *ē developed in the regular way to OHG *ā; hence, OHG
*-aw V-. However, this is not universally accepted.
Darms (1978, 58, 461) assumed that *-wj- had been assimilated
to *-ww- before the West Germanic gemination (cf. section 8.4.1
above) and thus expected the development *-ēwjV-> *-āwwV-> OHG
*-auwV-. He did not provide an attestation of this development in Old
High German, however, for his potential example *hlēwja-> *hlāwwa-
only led to an unattested paradigm OHG nom. sg. m. *lau, gen. sg.
*lauwes 'tepid.'76 This explanation does not fit the earlier recon-
struction of inf. *läwen 'to betray' (< PGmc. *lēwijan). Darms' theory
75 Cf. section 8.4.3 above. In fact, Raven listed klauuen among long-
stem verbs of the weak Class I and has therefore presumably assumed a

ambiguated the matter (*klāta of *kläwen vs. *klouuita, *kleuuita, or
the like, of *klouwen), but none are attested.
76 On the other hand, Darms interpreted MHG gen. sg. lawes as
derived from the stem *hlēwa-.

=== page 237 ===

would predict the Old High German outcome inf. *lauwan/-en,77 3sg.
pres. *lāwit, 3sg. pret. *lāta. A verb of this unusual structure would
hardly have been left unchanged, and more than one method of regular-
izing the pattern was available. In this case, a generalization of the stem
*lauw- (and perhaps analogical influence from the verbs in -ouwen,
-ewen derived from PGmc. *-awjan) is unlikely to have taken place; at
least it has not affected pret. firlāti. On the other hand, it is thinkable
that a generalization of the stem *lāw- replaced inf. *lauwan/-en by the
unattested inf. *läwen analogically. On the basis of this meager evi-
dence, Darms' theory is probably unprovable.
8.4.5 The Old High German outcome
of PGmc. *-īwijV- and *-ūwijV-
As was the case with PGmc. *-ēwijV-, it seems a priori safest to
assume that PGmc. *-īwijV- preserved *w into Old High German and
that the long vowel was left unchanged; hence, OHG *-īwV-. An
example of this development is the verb OHG hīwen 'to marry'
(< PGmc. *hīwijan), pret. hīta (comparable to *lāwen, pret. *lāta).
The intervocalic *w was ultimately lost, and forms with hiatus fillers
appeared (pres. ptc. hiienti, 1sg. pres. kehihe, cf. Raven 1963-67, 1:71-
72). The etymology of this verb is well known, and no doubt exists as
to whether the *w was inherited; cognates such as Go. heiwa-frauja
77 Inf. PGmc. *lēwijan> *lēwjan > *lāwwan> *lauwan would
probably be the strictly phonological result, for one can hardly expect
the Class I development of *-jan > -en after the change *wj> *ww.

=== page 238 ===
238
'master of the house,' ON hý-býli 'home, homestead' (< PGmc. *hiwa-)
make that clear. OHG hīwen 'to marry' was most certainly denomina-
tive, and nominal cognates exist in that language (e.g., OHG hīwo m.,
hīwa f. ‘spouse,' hīwiski n. ‘family').
The Verschärfung environment PGmc. *-ūwij V- would be ex-
pected to yield *-uww(i)jV-, and from there most likely pre-OHG
*-ūwjV-, at least if *-uww-behaved before *j as it did in prevocalic
position (cf. OHG scuwo, scũ m. ‘shadow' < *skuwwan-, OHG ūwila f.
‘owl' < *uwwilōn- (Jasanoff 1978a, 86)).78
The verb ON skyggva 'to overshadow, polish' (< *skuww(i)jan),
an example of Verschärfung with a Germanic u-vocalism, is matched by
OHG inf. scuen, 3sg. pres. scuit ‘to sketch, imitate' (Raven 1963-67,
1:189). ON skyggva was probably created from *skuwwan- within
Germanic, and it is unlikely that there ever existed a form PGmc.
*skūwijan (cf. section 8.3.5 above).
It was argued above that the verb ON byggva ‘to inhabit' is a
genuine example of the outcome of PGmc. *-ūwijV- in that language
(< *buww(i)jan < PGmc. *būwijan, ultimately a causative PIE *bhuh,-
eie/o-, cf. sections 7.1.2 and 8.3.5). If this verb had been carried down
to Old High German, the outcome *būwen (*buen) might have been
expected. Such forms are identical to those of the weak verb of Class I
which arose as a secondary j-present built on the strong verb *bū'an
(OHG bū(w)en < *būjan, analogical from *bū'an < PGmc. *būjan). In
view of the fact that OHG bū(w)en is both intransitive ‘to live' and

certain. Some handbooks supply skūwo (Schatz 1927, 193).

transitive 'to inhabit,' it is at least theoretically possible that forms of
PGmc. *būjan and the old causative PGmc. *būwijan had merged in Old
High German. If this is the case, this verb provides indirect evidence
for the Old High German outcome of PGmc. *-ūwijV-.
8.5 Old English
8.5.1 The Old English outcome
of PGmc. *-awjV- and *-iwjV-
As stated above (section 8.4.1), the structures PGmc. *-awjV- and
*-iwjV- (the latter from *-ewjV- and *-iwjV-) became *-awwjV- and
*-iwwjV- through the West Germanic gemination of consonants before
*j, thus creating new occurrences of *-ww- beside the Proto-Germanic
geminate *-ww- which had arisen in Verschärfung environments. In
West Germanic, u-diphthongs were subsequently formed with the
preceding vowels. Examples of Verschärfung cases confirm this
development for Old English, e.g., the verbs OE scēawian ‘to look'
(< *skauwō-< *skawwō- < PIE *skou̟h,-ehje/o-, cf. OHG scouwōn)
and OE cēowan ‘to chew' (< *kewwan < PIE *ĝi̟euh,-e/o-, cf. Olcel.
tyggva, OHG kiuwan).79
One would, accordingly, expect PGmc. *-awjV- and *-iwjV- to
appear in Old English as reflexes of WGmc. *-awwjV- and *-iwwjV-,
i.e., forms with long diphthongs and *w preserved. Although such
79 So reconstructed by Jasanoff (1978a, 82-83).

=== page 240 ===
240
forms occur (e.g., Angl. niowe 'new,' cf. Go. niujis, ON nýr, OHG
niuwi (< PGmc. *niwja-)), this is not consistently the case. Forms of
weak verbs of Class I in PGmc. *-awjan and *-iwjan basically fall into
three groups. 80
1. Forms in which a long diphthong is followed by the preserved
suffixal *j, but *w does not appear on the surface. Examples of this
outcome are WS cīеzan/cīzan/cỹzan, non-WS cēzan 'to call' (< PGmc.
*kawjan, cf. OHG gikewen), poet. hēzan 'to carry out, complete'
(< PGmc. *hawjan, cf. ON heyja), and strēzan ‘to strew' (< PGmc.
*strawjan, cf. Go. straujan, OHG strouwen).81 The *j frequently
appears in forms where it is not historically justified; in West Saxon it
was completely generalized, e.g., pret. cī(e)3de, pret. ptc. zecï(e)zed.
2. Forms in which a long diphthong is followed by w. These
forms thus show the expected outcome of *-awwjan and *-iwwjan with
gemination. The *j of the suffix does not appear; this was regularly the
case in verbs of Class I where *j was lost after geminated consonants
(e.g., fremman 'to advance, perform' < PGmc. *framjan). Among
examples of this group are OE hlywan/hlēowan 'to make warm'
(< PGmc. *hliwjan, cf. ON hlýja) and pret. ptc. bisĭudi ‘embroidered'
of the verb 'to sew' (PGmc. *siwjan, cf. Go. *siujan, ON *sýja). Verbs
with a Proto-Germanic geminate (Verschärfung product) in this group
are WS iewan/ywan 'to show' (< PGmc. *awwjan, cf. OFr. awia, auwia
80 The three groups and the examples are taken from Brunner (1965,
323-25).
81 OE hlīzan 'to praise' can presumably be added to this group
(< PGmc. *hlawjan, cf. OHG lewen), cf. section 8.4.3 above.

'id.') 82 and WS зetrīewan 'to trust' (< PGmc. *-triwwjan, cf. Go. adj.
triggws, ON tryggr, OHG triuwi ‘true'). The *w also appears in forms

e.g., WS pret. hlÿwde, pret. ptc. zehlỹwde.
3. Forms in which a short root vocalism is followed by w. This
outcome is phonological in forms where *j did not follow *w at the
time of gemination (2,3sg. present, preterite, preterite participle).
Forms with Class I endings showing this treatment are scarce (e.g.,
pret. ptc. bisiuuidi ‘embroidered,' pret. strewede 'strewed,' pret. ptc.
3ebeowed 'rubbed'), for the verbs in question have for the most part
joined Class II (si(o)wian 'to sew,' stre(o)wian 'to strew,' etc.).
Patterns similar to those of the first and second group appear
in ja-stem nouns, the other primary source of the sequences PGmc.
*-awjV- and *-iwjV-. Neuter ja-stems with *j, but no *w (cf. group 1),
are WS hī(e)3, non-WS hēz ‘hay' (< PGmc. *hawja-, cf. Go. hawi, ON
hey, OHG houwi/hewi), WS 3lī3 ‘mirth' (< PGmc. *gliwja-, cf. ON
gly), Angl. trēz ‘tray' (< PGmc. *trawja-, cf. ON treyju-søðull ‘type of

rences of 3līz, e.g., nom. sg. 3līu, gen. sg. 3līwes (forms from Brunner
1965, 201-2).
82 OE Tewan has by some scholars been connected to the Indo-European
word for 'eye' (Falk/Torp 1909, 5; from PIE *ok"- ‘to see,' Pokorny

=== page 242 ===

This confusing evidence has led to doubts that West Germanic
gemination of *w was phonologically regular in every instance of
PGmc. *-\wjV-. Some scholars have given noncommittal statements
on this subject,83 others have proposed solutions which limited the effect
of gemination of *w or eliminated it altogether. To name a few alter-
native suggestions, Sievers (1884, 204) proposed that an old difference
in accentuation might be the reason for the alternation of OE w and 3,
*-íwj- yielding OE -īw- and *-iwj '- becoming OE -ī3-; yet he realized
that this claim was unprovable. 84 Van Helten (1892, 297-310; 1895,
507-8; 1903, 530-33) assumed gemination in the sequences PGmc.
*-awjV- and *-iwjV- in Old High German and Old Frisian; on the other
hand, Old English, as well as Old Saxon and the other Low German
dialects only underwent gemination of *w after *i and that gemination
was even analogical to the gemination of other consonants.85 Dahl
(1938, 104, 107-9) did not assume any gemination of *w, but in certain
forms with surface w he invoked dialectal assimilation processes, e.g.,
83 "Nicht ganz klar ist, ob die westgerm. Gemination vor j... auch in
der Lautfolge -wj- in allen Fällen anzunehmen ist." (Brunner 1965,
142); "Im Westgermanischen wurde aus dieser Folge in großem Um-
fange -uui-" (Luick 1921, 116). Campbell (1959, 46) stated the West
Germanic gemination as a general rule, but was vague on the further
development in Old English, stating that *w was "generally" lost in
*-auwj- (hence, -īez-, -ē3-) and *j in *-iuwj- (hence, -īew-, -īow-).
84 This solution is untenable. These variant forms with w and j are a
dialectal West Germanic phenomenon; their explanation would be much
more likely to lie within the dialectal period.
85 Van Helten's arguments are unconvincing. He attributed the
divergent treatments of PGmc. *-awjV- and *-iwjV- to different
developments of the diphthongs *au and *iu, assuming that these
sequences had been *-aujV- and *-iwjV-, respectively, at the time of
gemination (van Helten 1903, 531-32). Reflexes of *-iwjV- are not
attested in Old Frisian.

=== page 243 ===
243
gen. sg. *haujes> OHG hauwes, dat. sg. *glīujæ> *glīuu̟æ > OE
gliowe.
Of the scholars who accepted the rule of West Germanic
gemination of *w, Kögel (1884b, 524, 533-35) accounted for the Old
English forms with j (3) on the surface by a sound change of *w to j
through the palatalizing effect of a preceding *i or a following *i or *j
(inf. *strīezan ‘to strew' < *strauwjan). Of course, this rule created
problems for forms appearing with w, e.g., the type OE pret. strewede,
where Kögel predicted OE pret. *stre3de (< *strawida).86 Luick (1921,
117) and Girvan (1931, 42-43) accepted gemination, but were unable to
claim with confidence that it had applied in Old English in the sequence
*-awj-; cf. the w-less forms of the type *hawja (WS hī(e)3, non-WS
hē3).
As demonstrated above (section 8.4.1), the gemination of *w
before *j is evident in Old High German, and it would have obvious
advantages to be able to account for forms in the other West Germanic
dialects apparently deriving from *-awwjV- and *-iwwjV- as instances
of the same process. Gemination has certainly been accepted by many
scholars, but its exact distribution, dialectal and phonological, has
typically been vaguely formulated and not adequately justified. The
dialectal distribution assumed by van Helten, e.g., is quite surprising; it
is unusual to witness Old High German and Old Frisian undergo a sound
86 Kögel stated the rule vaguely, suggesting that *w became j under the
specified conditions "oder doch unter gewissen noch nicht näher be-
kannten bedingungen." The rule supposedly operated in Old English
and Old Saxon (because of Old Saxon j-forms such as inf. thregian ‘to
threaten,' cf. OHG drewen) or at least in certain dialects of these
languages.

244
change in which neither Old English nor Old Saxon took part. The
phonological constraints on gemination in Old English sometimes
suggested also need justification, for it is not obvious why gemination
would occur in the sequence *-iwjV-, but not in *-awjV-.
It is, however, possible to account for the Old English facts
without abandoning the idea of a genuine gemination of *w before *j in
the sequences *-awjV- and *-iwjV- by assuming the following phono-
logical development:
inf. *-awjan> *-awwjan> *-auwjan > *-ēawjan> *-īewjan
WS -īezan
inf. *-iwjan > *-iwwjan> *-iuwjan> *-ēowjan> *-īewjan
> WS *-īezan
The infinitives of the type WS ciezan (< *kawwjan < *kawjan; the first
group of Class I verbs above) would thus be considered phonological,
as would such ja-stem forms as gen. sg. WS 3līzes (< *gliwwjas <
*gliwjas). This account makes use of well-known sound changes, viz.,
the West Germanic gemination and development of u-diphthongs in
structures of *-Vww-, and the regular treatment of the diphthongs *au
and *iu by i-umlaut in Old English. A loss of *w is added, an under-
standable simplification of the sequence *-īewj-.87 In the first group of
verbs above (the cīezan type), -īez- was leveled through the paradigms;
87 This change is to be understood as limited to the sequence *-iewj-; it

-īez- was extended to such forms as WS pret. cī(e)3de, pret. ptc.
zecī(e)zed. The second group above (the hlywan type) generalized the
stem with a (long) diphthong and *w at a stage prior to the loss of *w,
cf. hlỹwan, WS pret. hlywde, pret. ptc. 3ehlywde.
The description of the treatment of those forms of the paradigms
which did not contain *j at the time of gemination depends on the
development of *w before *i (e.g., 3sg. pres. *-awiþ < PGmc.
*-awjiþ(i), 3sg. pret. PGmc. *-awiðē). It is not well understood to
what extent *w was lost in that environment (cf. Brunner 1965, 141-
42), but, in fact, the verbal forms with a short root vowel (the third
group above) can be accounted for whether or not *w was lost. By a
phonological preservation of *w, 3sg. pret. PGmc. *strawide would
have yielded OE strewede directly. If, on the other hand, the loss of
*w was phonological, such forms could have retained *w on the analogy
of the forms with *j, which kept *w quite long (cf. inf. *-auwjan>
*-ēawjan > *-īewjan > WS -īeзan).
The gemination of *w can hardly have taken place before the
syncope of the final syllable of nom.-acc. sg. *hawja, *gliwja, or until
these had the form *hawi, *gliwi, cf. the evidence of the ungeminated
form OHG hewi ‘hay.' Certain Old English forms indicate a loss of
*w before *i, e.g., compounds with the first elements nī- (< *niwi-
< PGmc. *niwja- 'new') and 3lī- (< *gliwi- < PGmc. *gliwja- ‘mirth').
As was the case with the Old High German byform houwi, the ungemi-
nated forms could easily be influenced by forms with gemination.88
88 This description of events, the loss of *w before *i and later influ-
ence from geminated forms, matches that of Brunner (1965, 141-42,

=== page 246 ===
246
These analogical changes could happen at more than one stage; hence,
one finds forms with 3 and forms with w, that is, the same outcomes as
in the first and second group of verb forms. Phonological forms with
-3-, e.g., gen. sg. *-auwjas > WS -ī(e)зes, *-iuwjas > WS -ī(e)зes, gave
rise to the analogical forms nom.-acc. sg. WS hī(e)3, 31ī3. The leveling
of w could also take place; this happened before the geminated forms
lost *w. In this case, gen. sg. *glīowjes triggered the replacement of
nom.-acc. sg. *glī by *glīow, which, in turn, is the reason for the
secondary gen. sg. 3līowes.
In this way it is possible to account for the Old English forms
without abandoning the idea of a phonological gemination of *w before
*j. In Kögel's attempt to do so, an unconvincing change of *w to j
under vaguely formulated conditions was proposed and the type OE
strewede was not accounted for (1884b, 533-35, cf. above). The only
special assumption needed in the solution proposed here is a phono-
logical loss of *w in the sequence *-īewj-.
The Old English forms above that have 3 but no w (group 1, the
ciezan type) have been used as arguments against calling the gemination
of *w before *j a West Germanic change. Comparable forms can be
found in the Continental West Germanic dialects, a fact which in van
Helten's opinion (1892, 297-310; 1895, 507-8) limits the gemination to
Old High German and Old Frisian. To the Old English forms with -īez-
(< PGmc. *-awj-) correspond continental forms with -ōj- (e.g., MLG
dōien beside douwen 'to thaw,' OS dōian 'to die,' strōgen beside
stro(u)wen 'to strew,' OS hōi, gen. hōgias ‘hay'), but occurrences of

=== page 247 ===
247
PGmc. *-iwj-show a surface w (OS acc. pl. m. niuua 'new').89 Van
Helten derived the forms with -ōj- from an ungeminated sequence
*-awj- (> *-auj-> *-aoj-> -ōj-).⁹0 In the light of the account of the
Old English forms proposed above, one wonders whether these dialects
could have derived forms with j from geminated forms as well. The
development *-awwj-> *-auwj-> *-aowj-> *-aoj-> -ōj with a loss of
*w in the inconvenient sequence *-aowj- seems perfectly plausible. As
was the case in Old English, *j was extended from such forms to un-
geminated forms (OS nom. sg. hōi, OS pret. pl. strōidun), and *w could
also be generalized at a stage prior to w-loss (inf. strouwen). Under
this interpretation, the gemination of *w before *j can be considered a
West Germanic change.
8.5.2 The Old English outcome of PGmc. *-ŴwijV-
Evidence for the outcome of sequences of PGmc. *-VwijV- in
Old English is limited, and many questions are open to speculation. It is
generally assumed that *w was preserved after long vowels, except in
word-final position or before the vowel *u (cf. rōwan ‘to row,' gen. sg.
snāwes m. ‘snow' vs. w-loss in pret. pl. rēon, nom. sg. snā). Un-
fortunately, it is not known with certainty to what extent *w was lost
before *i, the environment which immediately concerns the outcome of
PGmc. *-ŴwijV- (cf. Brunner 1965, 141-43).
89 Forms are taken from van Helten (1892) and Holthausen (1921, 61).
90 This opinion was also expressed by Holthausen (1921, 61).

=== page 248 ===
248
The prime examples of the structure PGmc. *-ōwijV-, namely,
the forms of PGmc. *stōwijV- and *tōwijV- (Go. stojan, OHG stouwen,
Go. taui, ubil-tōjis, ON tøja), do not have exact cognates in Old English,
and its outcome in that language is an open question.
If the structure PGmc. *-ōwijV- had lost *w on the way to Old
English, as was the case in Gothic and Old Norse, the expected outcome
of an infinitive of the weak Class I would be OE *-ēzan (with umlaut).
For this reason, Flasdieck (1935, 92-93) justly rejected the suggestion
that OE bōzian, OFr. bōgia ‘to live' were the phonological result of
PGmc. *bōwijan (the root PIE *bhuh,-); he expected umlaut to yield
OE *bēzan, OFr. *bēia.91 The same argument can be applied against
Seebold's reconstruction *gōwjan for OE 3ōian ‘to moan,' which he
connected to ON geyja ‘to bark' (Seebold 1970, 216). If a rule of w-
loss applied here at all, only the variant form OE zēzan could be phono-
logical from PGmc. *gōwijan. As it is doubtful indeed that either OE
bōzian or OE 3ōian, çēzan derives from PGmc. *-ōwijan, these verbs
do not provide any proof for the Old English treatment of that
structure.92

Old English preserved intervocalic *w after *ī in examples such

influence of related words, if it had been lost in that environment.
Old English does not show the outcome of the structure PGmc.

unavailable. There is no unequivocal exact cognate of ON byggva ‘to
inhabit' (< PGmc. *būwijan). Northumbrian forms of this root with
umlaut and Class I inflection (North. bya) have sometimes been equated
with ON byggva (Seebold 1970, 125). If this were correct, it would
a loss of *w before consonants; the latter would be parallel to the loss of
*w in word-final position after long vowels (Brunner 1965, 141).

indicate a loss of *w. In fact, the Northumbrian forms do not provide
conclusive evidence for this development, for they can have grown out

7.1.2). A comparison with cases of Verschärfung thus does not reveal
the Old English outcome of PGmc. *-ūwijV-; derivatives of OE scu(w)a
m. ‘shadow,' e.g., ofer-scu(w)ian, -scyian 'to overshadow,' fore-

constitute examples of PGmc. *-ūw- (cf. section 8.3.5 above).96
The rules of w-loss in Old English have been obscured by the
strong tendency to generalize w in paradigms, as we have seen in the
development of the verba pura and sequences of PGmc. *-VwjV-. If
the loss of *w was phonological in forms of PGmc. *-VwijV- (possibly
depending on the preceding vowel), one can, likewise, expect its ana-
logical restoration through the influence of related forms with *w
preserved. In addition, the scarcity of examples of PGmc. *-ŴwijV-
makes it difficult to make firm judgements about the phonological
treatment of such sequences in Old English.
8.6 Conclusion
It was claimed in this chapter that sequences in which a short
u-diphthong was followed by ** were realized in Proto-Germanic as
structures of *-VwjV-. This environment did not meet the conditions
96 Bosworth and Toller cited these forms with a long vowel; a metri-
cally short vowel in OE scu(w)a m. ‘shadow' has led others to recon-
struct WGmc. *skuwō (Brunner 1965, 142).

=== page 252 ===
252
for the Proto-Germanic loss of intervocalic *j. PGmc. *-VwjV-
included descendants of several Indo-European structures:
a) PIE *-Vu-iV- (PIE *neu-jo-> PGmc. *niwja-
> Go. niujis, ON nýr, OE niowe, OHG niuwi 'new')
b) PIE *-Vuh⭑-įV- (PIE *kou̟h₂-jo-> PGmc. *hawja-
> Go. hawi, ON hey, OE hĩez, OHG houwi ‘hay')
c) PIE *-Vh,u-įV- (PIE *sih,u-je/o-> PGmc. *siwjan
Go. siujan, ON sýja, OE siowian, OHG siuwen 'to sew')
d) PIE *-Vų-V¡V- (PIE *ki̟ou̟-eje/o- > PGmc. *hawjan
> ON heyja, OE hēzan 'to carry out')
Inner-Germanic formations with j-suffix merged with the inherited
forms, e.g., PGmc. *hliwjan, a j-present derivative of the noun *hlewa-
n. 'protection' (ON hlýja, OE hlywan 'to protect').
After the separation of the dialects, PGmc. *-VwjV- developed
into u-diphthongs before *-jV- in East and North Germanic (PGmc.
*-VwjV-> *-VujV-). On the other hand, the consonantal *w was
geminated before *j in West Germanic (PGmc. *-VwjV-> *-VwwjV-).
It has not been known with certainty to what extent this gemination of
*w was West Germanic; some scholars have viewed this development as
an instance of the general West Germanic consonant gemination before
*j with possible limitations, at least as far as Old English is concerned,
others have even excluded the possibility of gemination of *w. The
description of the Old English development of PGmc. *-VwjV- given
above demonstrates that the gemination of *w before *j can be con-
sidered a common West Germanic change.

=== page 253 ===
253
In sequences with a long vocalism, the Proto-Germanic realiza-
tion was *-ŴwijV-; the changes which affected the *w and the *j in
the Proto-Germanic period were the gemination of *-ūwijV- to
*-uww(i)jV- (ON byggva) and the loss of intervocalic *j in forms
with i-initial endings (PGmc. *-Vwiji-> *-Ŵwi'i-).
PGmc. *-ōwijV- lost *w in Gothic and Old Norse but kept *j; Old
Norse has i-umlaut (Go. stōjan, ON tøja). Old English *-ēzan
corresponds to the Gothic and Old Norse results. It is clear, however,
that the rule by which *w was lost after *ō in such structures did not
operate in Proto-Germanic. The *w seems to have been retained in this
environment long enough to be geminated in pre-Old High German and
yield the outcome OHG -ouwen (*-ōwijan> *-ōwwjan> *-awwjan >
-auwen > -ouwen). An attempt at establishing the merger of PGmc.
*-awjV- and *-ōwij V- as a West Germanic phenomenon would be
handicapped by the lack of evidence for the Old English outcome of
*-ōwij V-. The ambiguity of forms such as OS tōgean 'to make'
(< PGmc. *tawjan or *tōwijan) and of the spelling -ouw- in the
continental dialects (e.g., MDu. touwen 'to make, prepare') would also
make such a claim difficult.97
PGmc. *-ēwijV- turned out as a normal structure of PGmc.
97 In van Helten's opinion (1892, 308), *-ōw- was behind MDu., MLG
touwen, and he considered MDu., MLG clouwen 'to scratch' to have
had *-ōw- from the noun *clowa 'claw'; his postulation of *-ōw- seems
certain in MLG ro(u)wen 'to rest' from the noun rouwe 'rest' (< PGmc.
*rōwō(n)). Peeters (1970, 278-81) proposed a West Germanic change
*-ōwj-> *-ōuw- with a subsequent merger of *-ōu- and the reflex of
PGmc. *-au-, but he did not discuss actual Old English examples nor
describe in detail the consequences of this asumption for the Continental
West Germanic dialects.

=== page 254 ===
254
*-ēCijV-; *w was preserved in Gothic (lēwjan), Old Norse (skæva), Old
English (lawan), and well into Old High German (*lā(w)en). These
examples, as well as examples of PGmc. *-īwijV-, argue against a
general loss of PGmc. *w (OHG hīwen 'to marry,' OE 3e-brīwan 'to

were no doubt created or analogically restructured in the individual

*-ēwijV-, viz., a Proto-Germanic shortening to *-awjV-. His motiva-

PGmc. *tawjan (Go. taujan 'to do, make') from PGmc. *tēwō f. (Go.
tēwa 'order'). This is completely unnecessary; the verbs do not have to
be denominative.

EPILOGUE
THE LOSS OF PROTO-GERMANIC INTERVOCALIC *j
It was suggested in Chapter 1 above that there were reasons to
suspect that the loss of intervocalic *j in Proto-Germanic was more
extensive than is generally assumed. It was proposed that the sound law
formulated by Cowgill (1959; 1960a), which restricted the loss of
PGmc. *j to the position between vowels of noninitial syllables, as well
as earlier rules involving PGmc. *j before or after *i (notably, Mahlow
1879), could be replaced by a wider formulation. The proposed rule
allows j-loss after stressed vowels and is formulated as follows:
Single *j was lost in Proto-Germanic between vowels, except
where the preceding vowel was *i and a different vowel
followed.1
The results of an investigation of forms which are likely to have
contained an intervocalic *j in Proto-Germanic after stressed vowels--
or have previously been interpreted in that way--were explained in the
following chapters. The majority of examples consisted of j-presents of
1 A slightly different version was also mentioned (section 1.4.2), which
would be appropriate if sequences which later underwent Verschärfung
still had the form *-aijV- and *-ījV- when j-loss started to operate:
Single intervocalic *j was lost in Proto-Germanic, except when
preceded by *i, *ī, or *ai and followed by a vowel other than *i.
255

=== page 256 ===
256
roots which ended in vowels at the Proto-Germanic stage. There were
examples of such j-presents with the root vowels *a (PGmc. *stajan ‘to
stand'), *ē (PGmc. *sējan ‘to sow³), *ō (PGmc. *rōjan ‘to row’), and *ū
(PGmc. *būjan 'to live'). It was argued that the loss of PGmc. *j had
led to hiatus forms which preserved the hiatus into the Germanic
daughter languages.2
Examples of phonological development:
inf. *stajan > *sta'an
> OHG, OS, OFr. stān
inf. *sējan > *sē'an
> Go. saian, ON sá
inf. *rōjan > *rō'an
> ON róa
inf. *būjan > *bū'an > Go. bauan, ON búa, OE būan
A thorough discussion of the development of the forms of the
verba pura in Chapters 3, 4, and 5 showed how hiatus forms were pre-
served in Gothic (saian) and into North Germanic (ON sá with a late
contraction from Pr.-N. *sā'a). It was explained that the complicated
West Germanic attestations can be understood as showing that the
Proto-Germanic hiatus has not survived in any West Germanic dialect.
Old English and Old Frisian broke the hiatus by *w (OE sāwan, pre-
OFr. *sēwa(n) with loss of *w in ModEFr. ziāhn). A secondary j-
present was created in the prehistory of Old High German (OHG sāen
2 In fact, the exact time of the contraction of *sta'an to *stan is not
known; the only genuine attestations are West Germanic.

=== page 257 ===
257
< pre-OHG *sājan), and forms spelled with -i- (OHG säien) show a
"tertiary" hiatus breaker. Old High German hiatus forms with a in the
ending (OHG saan), which have been interpreted as belonging to a
separate j-less present formation (analogical or preserving a simple
thematic present), are in all probability late dialectal byforms of the
secondary j-present.
The prime example of a j-present with a root-final *ū is PGmc.
*būjan, which by comparative evidence is best reconstructed as PIE
*bhuh¸-je/o-; this verb shows a phonological hiatus in Go. bauan, ON
búa, OE būan, but Old High German has created a secondary j-present
buen (< pre-OHG *būjan). Old Norse verbs in -ýja allegedly showing
the retention of the *j of PGmc. *-ūjan (e.g., ON dýja ‘to shake') can be
explained as secondary j-presents.
The verbal forms therefore confirm the assumption of a Proto-
Germanic loss of intervocalic *j after stressed vowels. It also turns out
that nominal forms deriving from preforms containing the sequences
PGmc. *-ájV-, *-éjV-, *-ốjV-, and *-újV- accord with the proposed
rule of j-loss. It can be shown that nominal forms which have been
thought to demonstrate the preservation of *j into the daughter
languages did not contain an intervocalic *j at the time of j-loss.
Alleged counterevidence can thus be dismissed, e.g., in the case of Go.
ajukdūps 'eternity' (< PGmc. *aiwuki-, not *ajuki-), ON blær ‘gust of
wind' (phonological from Pr.-N. *blār, not with umlaut triggered by a
preserved *j of Pr.-N. *blājar), ON gnýr ‘din' (< PGmc. *gniwja-z, not
*gnūja-z).
Finally, sequences of u-diphthongs followed by *-jV- (Go. taujan
'to do,' ON heyja ‘to perform') are properly reconstructed as PGmc.

=== page 258 ===
258
*-VwjV- and are thus not relevant to the question of the development of
intervocalic *j.
The assumption of an extensive loss of intervocalic *j in Proto-
Germanic renders superfluous various dialectal j-loss rules which have
been postulated to account for the attested forms in the Germanic
daughter languages. This is achieved by the formulation of a sound law
which is at the same time phonologically plausible and attractively
simple.
